GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 3

PUBLIC OPENING CEREMONY
Sunday, October 8, 2000
2:30 PM

The Public Opening of Grand Lodge was held in the Masonic
Auditorium of the California Masonic Memorial Temple, 1111 California Street,
San Francisco, California beginning at 2:30 p.m. Grand Master Alvin J. Weis,
accompanied by his Grand Lodge Officers, entered the Auditorium to the sound
of trumpets and organ, and assumed their stations. The Grand Chaplain gave the
invocation.

The Grand Master welcomed all in attendance to the Public Opening of
the 151° Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of California. He thanked
Brother Edwin D. Clarke, Executive Officer of Northern California DeMolay,
and Brother David A. Lowry, Executive Officer of Southern California
DeMolay, for raising the Grand Lodge.

The Grand Master then noted that the gavel used by Brothers Clarke
and Lowry to raise the Grand Lodge had first been presented to him in April,
1952, when he was installed Master Councilor of Manteca Chapter, Order of
DeMolay. The gavel was again presented to him on December 3, 1981, when he
was installed Master of Acalanes Fellowship Lodge No. 480. And the gavel was
presented to him a third time on October 13, 1999, when he was installed Grand
Master.

The Grand Marshal was then asked to retire to escort into the
auditorium the leaders of the concordant and appendant bodies of the Family of
Freemasonry in California. The following were present:

The Grand Master expressed to each of the representatives of the
concordant and appendant bodies the appreciation of all Masons in California
because of their support for Masonic principles, and also for the magnificent
charities each group supports.

The Grand Master then asked the Grand Marshal to direct the guests to
the seats reserved for them in the auditorium.

The Grand Master asked the Grand Marshal to escort Brother Melvyn
B. Stein, Potentate of Al Bahr Shrine Temple, to the East, and the Grand Master
asked him to bring the greetings on behalf of all the members of the Family of
Freemasonry in California.

Brother Stein spoke eloquently about how important it is for the Family
of Freemasonry to stand together, and jointly promote freedom of thought,
action, and speech. He spoke powerfully of the need for the individual members
of the Masonic Family to talk about our principles, about what we do, and about
the great charities we support for the benefit of our neighbors in our
communities, towns and cities. His remarks were very well received by those
assembled. The Grand Master thanked Brother Stein for his remarks, and asked
the Grand Marshal to conduct Brother Stein to a seat in the auditorium.

The Grand Master then asked the Grand Marshal to retire and escort to
a place west of the Altar the adult leaders of our Masonic Youth Groups in
California, and the elected youth leaders of the Order of DeMolay, the

International Order of Job’s Daughters, and the International Order of Rainbow
for Girls. The following persons were introduced:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 7

The Grand Master then expressed the appreciation of the Grand Lodge
of California for their leadership in the Masonic Youth Groups, and for all the
work the adult leaders and the elected youth leaders do on behalf of their
organizations.

The Grand Master then asked the Grand Marshal to conduct the adult
leaders of the Masonic Youth Groups to seats in the auditorium, along with other
special guests, and then to conduct the four elected youth leaders from California
to seats in the East.

Throughout the remainder of the afternoon’s program, the Grand
Master called on each of the elected youth leaders in turn for their very special
remarks. Each speaker was well-received with applause from the audience.
Each elected youth leader presented a special gift to the Grand Master.

The visiting dignitaries from other Grand Jurisdictions were then
escorted into the auditorium, accompanied by the flag of his state or nation. The
flags were presented by the Aahmes Shrine Patrol Unit. The Grand Master
invited the audience to take special note of the significance of each flag as it was
presented, described and posted. The Grand Master praised the flags as symbols
of freedom and independence, and called on those assembled to consider how
privileged we are to inhabit a land that is so unique, so meaningful, and so free.

The parade of flags was most dramatic and inspiring, accompanied by
an informative dramatic narration describing the history of each flag as it was
carried into the auditorium.

The Grand Marshal then introduced the visiting Grand Masters,
representatives of other Grand Masters, and visitors from other jurisdictions:

R.. We. James H. May....scsssecssssseresssssssssessesssseseessseeseseeesenees Deputy Grand Master
Grand Lodge F.& A.M. of Arizona

8 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

 

R..W.. Bruno Verges .. . Deputy Grand Master

French National Grand Lodge
W.. Didier Orelio.... Grand Inspector
French National Grand Lodge

 

The Grand Master graciously welcomed the distinguished guests and on
behalf of the Masons of California expressed our appreciation for them having
joined us. The Grand Master then asked the Grand Marshal to afford the guests
seats in the auditorium.

The Grand Master then directed the Grand Marshal to retire and present
the Flag of Our Country before the Altar of Freemasonry. As the flag was
presented at the Altar, it was accompanied by a stirring patriotic narration.

The Grand Master then invited all citizens to join him in the Pledge of
Allegiance to the Flag of Our Country.

The Grand Master then gave tribute to the Flag, with these remarks:

“This flag, which we honor today, is, as President Woodrow
Wilson said, “the emblem of our unity, our power, our thought,
and our purpose as a nation. It has no other character than that
which we give it from generation to generation.” The Grand
Master noted that the day’s program made a special emphasis
on the flag as a reminder to all present as Masons and
members of the Masonic family that, as the authors of our own
history, we have a duty to our Country that is greater than
ourselves, a duty to love our country, support its constitution,
and respect its flag. This flag, which is affectionately called
“Old Glory,” embodies every value Americans rejoice in.
May we each remind ourselves everyday of its value in our
lives that a “new glory” is born each day in our hearts.”

The fifth and sixth grade students from Sims Elementary School in Elk
Grove, California, then entertained and inspired the audience with a performance
of “Millenium Mix,” a wonderful patriotic and satiric look at American history.
The students were rewarded with thunderous applause, and the Grand Master
praised them and their teachers for the patriotism shown in their performance.

The Grand Maser then called upon Brother Raymond W. Peltier,
Chairman of the Grand Lodge Youth Orders Committee to assist him in
presenting the Grand Master’s Youth Achievement Award to this year’s five
honorees. The award recognizes singular service to the young people in the
Masonic Youth Groups.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 9

The Grand Master asked the Grand Marshal to escort the recipients to a
place West of the Altar. The Grand Master and the Deputy Grand Master then
presented the awards while Brother Peltier read the biography of the following
honorees:

Worshipful Roy Anderson, Mount Jackson Lodge No. 295
Brother David Schwartz, San Juan Lodge No. 732
Brother Dale Fisher, Temescal Palms Lodge No. 314
Worshipful Victor Keys, Inland Empire Lodge No. 306

The Grand Secretary remarked that the Grand Master had said there
would be five awards presented. In addition to the four already presented, he
then announced that the Grand Master had made a special selection this year:
Brother Raymond W. Peltier, Chairman of the Grand Lodge Youth Orders
Committee. Brother Peltier then joined the group of honorees at the Altar, as the
Grand Secretary read his biography, and the Grand Master and Deputy Grand
Master presented him with the award.

The Grand Master called on the Master Masons present to join him in
giving the distinguished Masons the Public Grand Honors. He asked the Grand
Marshal to conduct the brethren to seats in the auditorium.

The Grand Master then spoke of the vital importance of the Masonic
Youth Groups in their influence for good in this era of challenges facing our
youth. He emphasized that we cannot underestimate the important role of the
Masonic youth organizations in developing the lives of tomorrow’s business
professionals, government officials, parents, and religious leaders.

The Grand Master then announced that he is directing the funds from
the Grand Master’s Special Project to the leadership programs maintained by
each of the Masonic Youth Groups. He noted that during the year he had the
opportunity to present checks to the youth groups from the California Masonic
Foundation.

The Grand Master then called upon the four elected youth leaders to
join him at the lectern, where he presented each of them with a check from the
Grand Lodge of California. Each youth leader in turn thanked the Grand Master
and presented him with a special gift.


10 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

DEVOTIONAL SERVICE

The Grand Master said that it is fitting that we praise and thank our
Great Creator for the blessings celebrated in the day’s program. He then
introduced Miss Brenda Hereschski, a Past Honored Queen of Bethel No. 277,
International Order of Job’s Daughters, who favored the audience with the
devotional song “For the Beauty of the Earth.”

The Grand Chaplain then led the audience in prayer. The Grand Master
then said:

“All Masons must acknowledge a belief in God, how ever they may
refer to their particular deity, when first they enter our doors, and
throughout life. All Masons acknowledge a dependence upon the Great
Architect of the Universe for His sustaining presence in our lives.”

The Grand Master then called upon the Grand Chaplain, the Very
Reverend Lester W. Miller, for his remarks. [The Grand Chaplain’s remarks
appear in Appendix G.]

The Grand Master then called on those assembled as the Family of
Freemasonry to unite in a tribute to our Country and to our God by singing “God
Bless America.” The Grand Chaplain then delivered the benediction.

The Grand Master thanked all for attending the Public Opening of the
151" Annual Communication of Grand Lodge. He then led the Grand Lodge
Officers out of the auditorium.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 11

ONE HUNDRED FIFTY-FIRST
ANNUAL COMMUNICATION

Monday Morning, October 9, 2000

The 151 Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of Free and
Accepted Masons of the State of California was called to order by Grand Master
Alvin J. Weis, on Monday, October 9, 2000.

The opening session of Grand Lodge was raised by Worshipful Allen
Casalou, Master of Acalanes-Fellowship Lodge No. 480—your Grand Master’s
Lodge and the Lodge he served as Master, and by Worshipful Timothy Wood,
Master of Orinda Lodge No. 122, also your Grand Master’s Lodge. The
following gavels were used: an oak gavel and sounding block, presented at the
installation of officers of Normal Heights Lodge No. 632 on December 18, 1999,
by the Master, Worshipful Arnold Tojino; a bear foot gavel, presented at the
installation of officers of Orange Grove Lodge No. 293 on December 17, 1999;
and a setting maul and Christmas tree sounding block, presented at Sanger High
School cornerstone laying ceremony on February 14, 2000, by Worshipful
Richard L. Isom, Master of Sanger Lodge No. 316.

The Grand Secretary was asked to call the roll of Grand Lodge
Officers, Past Grand Masters, and Past Grand Officers, and to ascertain if a
constitutional number was present to convene the Grand Lodge of California.
The following were present as indicated by their response to the calling of the
roll:

The Grand Secretary announced that the Committee on Credentials had
reported that at the close of Registration on Sunday, there were 807 Master
Masons registered as of the end of the day, with at least twenty percent of the
Lodges chartered by this Grand Lodge represented, and that therefore a quorum
was present as required for the transaction of business.

The Grand Secretary reported that all Lodges have paid their annual per
capita assessment to Grand Lodge except for the following Lodges which are in
arrears in their payment to Grand Lodge of their annual per capita assessment:

The Grand Master then opened Grand Lodge in ample form. The
Grand Marshal and the Deacons retired to escort the Flag of the United States of
America into the Auditorium, which was followed by the Pledge of Allegiance.

Escort of the Past Grand Masters

The Grand Master then asked the Past Grand Masters to retire so that
they could be escorted into the auditorium and introduced. This being done, the
Grand Master welcomed the Past Grand Masters by expressing his appreciation
for the dedicated service that they have rendered to the Grand Lodge. The
Grand Marshal then introduced each Past Grand Master, following which the
Grand Master, accompanied by the Deputy Grand Master, personally greeted
each. They were then escorted to seats reserved for them in the auditorium.

The following were asked to rise as groups for recognition: Hiram
Award Recipients, Golden Veteran Award Recipients, 75-Year Recipients,
Worshipful Masters, Lodge Secretaries, Grand Lodge Committee members,
members of Constitutional Boards, District Inspectors, Former Grand Lodge
Officers, and Visiting Brethren from other jurisdictions. Each group was greeted
with a round of applause.

The Grand Master called on Worshipful Emile Laversin, former Grand
Chaplain, and Chairman of the Memorial Service Committee, to present a
service of remembrance for our departed brethren. Brother Don Nielsen
concluded the Memorial Service by singing “How Great Thou Art.”

The Grand Master then announced that Monday, October 9, 2000, was
a day of observance for the Jewish brethren, and that out of respect for them all
business of the Annual Communication would be conducted on Tuesday,
October 10, 2000.

make brief reports to Grand Lodge during the Monday Morning session. The list

an officer or committee chairman (as for the Trial Review Committee), the

Grand Master’s Message

The Grand Master then relinquished his chair to the Deputy Grand
Master for the purpose of delivering the Grand Master’s Message (Appendix A).
After his message, the Grand Master received a standing ovation, following
which he resumed his station.

At 12:00 Noon on Monday, October 9, the Grand Master called the
Grand Lodge from labor to refreshment, to be called on again promptly at 1:15
p.m. The Grand Lodge convened at 1:15 p.m., and after prayer by the Grand
Chaplain, the Grand Master noted that Grand Lodge was raised by Worshipful
Brother Arnold Tojino, Master of Normal Heights Lodge No. 632, in San Diego;
and by Worshipful Brother Duane Bergstrom, Master of Chula Vista Lodge No.
626. The gavels used this afternoon to raise Grand Lodge were the following: a
gavel and sounding block, presented at the cornerstone dedication for Jesse


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 15

Bethel High School in Vallejo on March 6, 2000, by Naval Lodge No. 87; an oil
derrick gavel, handcrafted by Steven P. Fleu, and presented at Huntington
Seacliff Elementary School Cornerstone laying on April 7, 2000, by Huntington
Beach Lodge No. 380; a gavel and sounding block, presented at the 150"
Anniversary Celebration for Nevada Lodge No. 13 on April 22, 2000, by
Nevada Lodge No. 13; a gavel and sounding block, presented at the cornerstone
ceremony for the City of Big Bear Lake Fire Department Headquarters and 75"
Anniversary of Big Bear Lodge No. 617, on June 16, 2000, by Big Bear Lodge
No. 617; and the Masonic Origins Research Lodge Traveling Gavel.

The Grand Master then directed the Grand Marshal to escort into the
auditorium the visiting Grand Masters from sister jurisdictions. The following
Grand Masters were received with Private Grand Honors and introduced:

The Grand Master welcomed the representatives to the Grand Lodge of
California, and asked them to convey to their respective Grand Masters best
wishes for the prosperity of Freemasonry in their Grand Lodges, and the
fraternal greetings of the Grand Lodge of California. He then asked the Grand
Marshal to afford the guests seats in the auditorium.

The Grand Master then called on Most Worshipful Brother John D.
Livie, Grand Master of Masons in Oregon, to favor us with his remarks as the
representative of the visiting Grand Masters and the other distinguished visitors.
His thoughtful and inspiring remarks were received with great appreciation by
the audience. The Grand Master then thanked Most Worshipful Brother Livie
for his remarks. The Grand Master of Oregon returned to his seat in the East.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 17

Grand Oration

The Grand Master then called upon the Grand Orator, Worshipful
Ronald E. Cooper, who presented the Grand Oration (Appendix H). The Grand
Oration was greeted at its conclusion with a standing ovation.

Presentation by Masonic Service Association

The Grand Master then invited to the lectern Most Worshipful Brother
Richard E. Fletcher, Past Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of Vermont, and the
Executive Secretary of the Masonic Service Association of North America. In
his remarks he thanked California Masons for their support of the special
projects of the Masonic Service Association of North America. He especially
thanked Worshipful A. Bruce Ivie and Very Worshipful Robert A. Klinger for
their work with the MSA Hospital Visitation Program. In his remarks he also
challenged the brethren to answer the question “Why are you a Mason?”

Most Worshipful Brother Fletcher’s remarks were well received by the
brethren. He then presented a Certificate of Appreciation to the Grand Master
for his support and personal participation in the MSA Hospital Visitation
Program.

The Grand Master then invited to the lectern Most Worshipful Brother
Paul E. Ellis, Past Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of Delaware and President
of the George Washington Masonic National Memorial. In his remarks he
conveyed the appreciation of the Memorial to the Masons of California for their
financial support throughout the years, and expressed his gratitude for Most
Worshipful Brother Anthony P. Wordlow’s enthusiastic service on the Board of
Directors of the Memorial.

Most Worshipful Brother Ellis then presented the Grand Master with a
poster of Commemoration Day, June 26, 1999, when a great Square and
Compass was dedicated on the grounds of the George Washington Masonic
National Memorial. The Grand Master him for his remarks, which were well
received by the brethren.

First Business Session —- Monday Afternoon

The Grand Master then called on selected Boards and Committees to
make brief reports to Grand Lodge during the Monday Afternoon session.

Pursuant to advice from Most Worshipful Brother R. Stephen Doan,
Chairman of the Jurisprudence Committee, the Grand Master read a Statement of
Position on the request he had received from three members of Grand Lodge that
all matters coming before the Grand Lodge be submitted to a vote by written
ballot. He stated that this request placed certain burdens on the Grand Secretary,


18 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

as no provisions had been made for this contingency. To avoid this burden, the
three Brothers agreed to withdraw their request if all matters other than those for
which a ballot had been prepared, be taken by a show of hands. The Grand
Master then directed that those in favor will raise their ballot cards when the
Chair requested a vote in favor of a matter under question, while those opposed
will evidence their preference by raising their ballot cards when the votes
opposed are requested. Those holding multiple ballots were instructed to hold
them in a fashion that the number of cards held may be seen from the Chair. If
the Chair believed that the vote was too close to call from a show of cards, then a
written ballot would be taken.

Recognition of Foreign Grand Lodges

The Grand Secretary reported that during the year the Grand Master
had extended interim recognition to six new Grand Lodges this year. The Grand
Secretary moved that the Grand Lodge of California extend full recognition to
the following Grand Lodges:

The Grand Orient of Brazil
The Grand Lodge of Croatia
The Grand Lodge of Estonia
The Grand Lodge of Mali
The National Grand Lodge of Guinea
The Grand Lodge of Sinaloa, Mexico

and confirm the recognition previously granted to the National Grand Lodge of
Romania and the Regular/Legal Grand Lodge of Portugal as the sole legitimate
Masonic powers in those two countries. The motion was seconded and was
adopted by the Grand Lodge.

Report of the Charters Committee

Worshipful Brother Vandell C. Owen presented the report of the
Charters Committee. He reported that he had met with Worshipful Steven A.
Chase, Master of Masonic Origins Research Lodge U.D., in Clovis, California,
and found their records and proceedings in good order and complete. The
Charters Committee therefore recommended Masonic Origins Research Lodge
UD be issued a Charter. He reported that eleven consolidations had been
completed this year, one of which was accomplished by the Grand Master’s
action under special circumstances.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 19

It was regularly moved, seconded and carried to approve the
consolidations made during the 1999/2000 Grand Lodge Year.

It was regularly moved, seconded and carried to grant a Charter to
Masonic Origins Research Lodge U.D.

Report of Committee on Trial Review

Worshipful Brother David R. Doan, Chairman, presented the written
report of the Trial Review Committee. It was moved, seconded and carried that
the Grand Lodge adopt the following Resolution:

“Resolved, that in each of the Trial Records numbered 2416,
2428, 2429, 2430, 2431, 2434 and 2435 the judgment and
penalty imposed be and are ratified, affirmed and approved.”

A separate report was received on Trial Record No. 2432. It was
moved, seconded and carried that the Grand Lodge adopt the following
resolution:

“Resolved that Trial Record No. 2432 be closed without a
retrial.”

A separate report was received on Trial Record No. 2436. It was
moved, seconded and carried that the Grand Lodge adopt the following
resolution:

“Resolved that in Trial Record No. 2436, the findings of the
Trial Master be affirmed and approved, except that the penalty
is raised to Expulsion.”

A separate report was received on Trial Record No. 2348, an
Application for Restoration. It was moved, seconded and carried that the Grand
Lodge adopt the following resolution:

“Resolved that the application for restoration in Trial Record
No. 2348 be denied.”

The Grand Master then called on Most Worshipful Brother George P.
Williams, Grand Master of Masons in Nevada, to address the Grand Lodge on
Freemasonry in Nevada. His remarks were well received by those in attendance.


20 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The Grand Master called on Most Worshipful Brother Stanley M.
Cazneaux, President of the Masonic Homes of California Board of Trustees, to
present a Masonic Homes Centennial Painting to each of the top four Lodges in
the Jurisdiction in contributions to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund.

The Grand Master then called on Most Worshipful Brother Mason E.
Teter, Grand Master of Masons in Hawaii, to address the Grand Lodge. He
reported that the Grand Lodge is now raising funds to build or purchase their
own Grand Lodge headquarters, and have started a program to raise funds to
support their indigent brethren. His remarks were well-received by the brethren
assembled.

The Grand Master then called on Most Worshipful Brother Oscar V.
Bunyi, Grand Master of Masons in the Philippines. He brought the fraternal
greetings and felicitations of the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of Free and
Accepted Masons of the Philippines, and its 13,000 members, to the Grand
Lodge of California. He noted that the Grand Lodge of California is the Mother
Grand Lodge of the Grand Lodge of the Philippines. He presented a book to the
Grand Master entitled “The Brethren,” a history of Freemasonry in the
Philippines. His gracious and heartfelt remarks were appreciated by the
brethren.

At 4:00 p.m. the Grand Master called Grand Lodge from labor to
refreshment, to be called on again at 9:00 a.m. on Tuesday, October 10.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 21

Tuesday Morning, October 10, 2000

Grand Lodge was called on promptly at 9:00 a.m. by Grand Master
Alvin J. Weis and the Grand Chaplain offered prayer. The Grand Master
announced that Grand Lodge had been raised this morning by Worshipful
Brother John Bigelow, Master of Santa Barbara Lodge No. 192, and Allen
Miller, Master of Point Loma Lodge No. 620.

The Grand Master announced that this session was raised with the
following gavels: a lighthouse maul and sounding block presented at a flagpole
dedication at Martin Luther King, Jr. Community Park in Oceanside on August
10, 2000, by Oceanside Lodge No. 381 and a handcrafted gavel and sounding
block presented to the Grand Master by Chula Vista Lodge No. 626 on
September 8, 2000, at the 75™ Anniversary Ceremony of their Lodge.

Before taking up the Legislation before the Grand Lodge, the Grand
Master called on Most Worshipful R. Stephen Doan, Chairman of the
Jurisprudence Committee, for his report.

The Grand Master then called on Worshipful Donald Finton, Chairman
of the Finance Committee, for his report.

The Grand Master announced that the Grand Lodge was then going to
consider the Grand Master’s Decisions and Recommendations, the Carry-Over
Legislation from 1999, and the new legislation which was properly submitted for
consideration at the Annual Communication. He reviewed the rules for
conducting debate on legislation outlined in Part V of the Ordinances, in the
California Masonic Code. The Grand Master also gave the following direction
regarding voice votes:

“We have received a request from three members of
Grand Lodge that all matters coming before the Grand Lodge
be submitted to vote by a written ballot. This request places
certain burdens on the Grand Secretary as no provision has
been made for this contingency. To avoid this burden, these
three brothers have agreed to withdraw their request if all
matters other than those for which a ballot has been prepared
be taken by a show of hands. It is therefore my direction that
those in favor will raise their ballot cards when the chair
requests the vote in favor of the matter under question while
those opposed will evidence their preference by raising their
ballot cards when the votes opposed are requested. For those
holding multiple ballot cards, they should raise them in a
fashion that the number of cards held may be seen from the
chair. If the chair believes that the vote is too close to call
from a show of cards, a written ballot will be taken.”

Election of Officers

The Grand Master then asked the Grand Secretary to explain the
balloting procedure for the election of officers. No ballot would be necessary if
the office were not contested. If more than one nomination were received for an
office, ballot cards would be distributed for the purpose of balloting for that
office.

Election of the Grand Master

Deputy Grand Master David C. Decker was nominated Grand Master
by Most Worshipful Allen B. Gresham, Past Grand Master. No further
nominations appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the nominations


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 27

be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot
for Right Worshipful David C. Decker to serve as Grand Master for the ensuing
Grand Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was declared unanimously elected.

Election of Deputy Grand Master

Senior Grand Warden C. Ray Whitaker was nominated Deputy Grand

nominations appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the nominations
be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot
for Right Worshipful C. Ray Whitaker to serve as Deputy Grand Master for the
ensuing Grand Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was declared unanimously
elected.

Grand Warden by Most Worshipful William H. Price, Past Grand Master. No
further nominations appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the
nominations be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a
unanimous ballot for Right Worshipful M. William Holsinger to serve as Senior
Grand Warden for the ensuing Grand Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was
declared unanimously elected.

Election of Junior Grand Warden

The Grand Master announced that pursuant to Section 510 of the
California Masonic Code, Worshipful Brother Howard Delane Kirkpatrick, a
Past Master of Reading Lodge No. 254, in Redding had been recommended for

stood before Grand Lodge. He was then escorted to the Grand Tiler’s Room,
while other nominations were called for. None appearing, it was moved,
seconded and carried that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous
ballot for Worshipful Brother Howard Delane Kirkpatrick to serve as Junior
Grand Warden for the ensuing Grand Lodge Year. Upon his return to the floor
of the Grand Lodge, and upon his acceptance, he was declared unanimously
elected by the Grand Master.

30

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

During the Installation ceremonies, Mrs. Gloria Cazneaux favored those

assembled with an inspired singing of “The Lord’s Prayer.”

Most Worshipful David C. Decker, kneeling at the altar, took his

obligation as Grand Master. The Grand Master’s remarks to the Grand Lodge
during his Installation were as follows:

“I first want to thank the Brethren for the confidence you have
placed in me. I assure you I will conduct the business of this Grand
Lodge with dignity and the consensus of the Executive Committee and I
will lead us into the 21" Century on a positive note.

“We are at a crucial moment in history — the threshold of the
21* Century. I ask you to think about how Freemasonry will begin the
next millennium. Will our fraternity be a symbol of the past or will it
be a signpost for the future? Clearly, our past is rich in tradition and
accomplishment. For three centuries, our fraternity has established a
firm historical record of advancing the fundamental tenets of
Freemasonry, dear to the hearts of all good men; ‘The Brotherhood of
Man under the Fatherhood of God.’

“On this sound foundation, which was built on both deed and
doctrine, generations of outstanding Masons have been taught lessons
to make them better men, benefited their communities and improved
our country.

“We must pledge to continue Masonry’s progress into the 21*
Century, for our work, dedication and accomplishments must not
simply celebrate the past. We must also chart the future and make it
happen for those who will follow us. Freemasonry will be both a
monument to our previous achievements and a bridge to a brighter
tomorrow for our craft and all mankind.

“I have selected as the motto for 2001 ‘Freemasonry —
Cornerstone of the New Millennium’ and as my theme, ‘Beginning
today, we’re building for tomorrow.’ Throughout our jurisdiction I
have witnessed growth taking place in many of our lodges, and through
community involvement our fraternity will continue to be recognized as
a good citizen.

“Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful Theodore Meriam, said
it best some thirty years ago, and I quote, ‘I want to challenge each of
you to do your personal part in adding to the wonderful heritage that is
ours in Freemasonry. We are prone to be complacent; and to take
things for granted. Freemasonry must never be stagnant; rather, it must
always be advancing. We must always be striving for improvement in
all that we do.’ End quote.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

“T ask whose responsibility is it to help us build this fraternity?
It is the responsibility of each of us as Master Masons. By living the
teachings of Freemasonry, those we are with each day will want to
become members of this successful organization.

“Let me share with you my priorities as Grand Master. I
would ask each of you to continue your support of those priorities
stressed by Most Worshipful Alvin J. Weis this past year. The
importance of Brotherhood, Masonic Charity, Masonic Education and
Leadership does not end with a change in Grand Lodge leaders. If we
are to accomplish our mission, we must continue to keep our lodges
informed on these issues. They must be ever before us.

“This year I will be adding two more priorities to this
important list. I ask each of you to take these additional priorities back
to your lodges and inform our brethren, as I would if I were there in
person.

“Stress the importance of community involvement. Thirty
years ago you could stop at a local community store and ask where the
Masonic Lodge was located. Nine times out of ten, the storeowner
would be a brother Mason and involved in lodge activities. Today, no
one knows who we are and what we do. I feel we need to extend our
lodges into the community and renew our interest in who we are and
what we do for humanity.

“Stress our involvement with our Youth Orders. Many of our
lodges have one or more of the youth groups meeting at their location.
We, as Masons, need to support their efforts and invite them to
showcase their work in our lodges. These young men are our future
members and, if we support them, they will join us when they become
adults. The young ladies will become members of the organizations
within the Family of Freemasonry.

“Remember, my Brethren, the purpose of Freemasonry in
California is to satisfy our members as we teach moral standards and
support a way of life that promotes fellowship, brotherhood and self-
improvement through excellence in leadership, education, family
involvement, charity and community involvement.

“In closing, let me summarize the issues I have stressed. As
the gavel is passed from Grand Master to Grand Master, it is very
important that the priorities are continued.

“We can either continue to follow our current pathway of
waiting to see what is going to happen or we can try to make a
difference in the growth of our membership through progressive
programs to express our values to the general public.

31


32 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

“California is experiencing some of the largest growth in its
history. We need to be part of that growth to see our fraternity into the
21* Century. This might sound like a very rash statement. But, let me
tell you my thoughts. We will see our lodges grow and prosper only
when our general membership decides they want it to happen.

“I can only lead and be successful if our members rally and
become involved in our fraternity. This is my mission and I will do all
in my power to ensure our success. I hope every member in the Family
of Freemasonry will support this effort which follows my theme for
2001, ‘Beginning today, we’re building for tomorrow.’

‘Thank you and God bless each of you.”

Following the newly installed Grand Master’s remarks, the Most
Worshipful Alvin J. Weis, Past Grand Master, was presented with his Past Grand
Master’s Apron, Jewel, and Wallet Card. Mrs. Virginia Weis was presented
with a bouquet of long-stemmed red roses and a gift in appreciation for her time
and support of her husband and the Grand Lodge during his year as Grand
Master.

The gavel the Grand Master used to preside during the Installation was
first presented to him on January 11, 1974, when he was installed as Master of
Upland-Mt. Baldy Lodge No. 419.

Worshipful Brother Vandell C. Owen, Master of Upland-Mt. Baldy
Lodge No. 419, presented the Grand Master with a check in the amount of
$1,000.00 as a donation from the Lodge for the David and Nancy Decker
Scholarship Fund of the California Masonic Foundation. Mrs. Martha Owen
presented a bouquet of long-stemmed red roses to Mrs. Nancy Decker, from
Upland-Mt. Baldy Lodge No. 419.

The Installation ceremonies were concluded with the Grand Chaplain
giving the Benediction.

The Grand Master then asked the audience to remain for a beautiful
patriotic Tribute to the New Millennium by Mrs. Gloria Cazneaux, singing a
magnificent medley of “God Bless America,” “The Battle Hymn of the
Republic,” and “America the Beautiful.” She was accompanied by Grand
Organist Joseph Von Geczy, and a sparkling light show created by Brother
Richard Gentschel.

The Grand Lodge Officers then retired from the Auditorium to attend
the Gala Reception in the lobby of the California Masonic Memorial Temple.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 37

GRAND MASTER’S MESSAGE
151sT ANNUAL COMMUNICATION

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

It is with a great deal of pleasure that I welcome all the delegates and
members of the Grand Lodge of California to this, your 151st Annual
Communication. This is your Grand Lodge and may we continue in harmony
for the betterment of this great Fraternity while we are together these next two
days. May friendships be renewed and new ones made while we join in
fellowship.

It is also my privilege to welcome our distinguished guests from other
jurisdictions. We feel honored with your presence, which reflects your affection
for our Grand Lodge. We sincerely appreciate your visit and sharing your
Masonic light with us. Although separated by geographical or legal constraints,
our Grand Lodges remain attached by our common interest in the fundamental
principles of Freemasonry. Please let us know if we can do anything to make
your stay more enjoyable.

A year ago, when I accepted your election, I thanked all of those who
made it possible for me to reach this highest honor and responsibility. And
today, at the end of a year filled with unforgettable experiences, moments of joy,
along with many difficult challenges, I thank you again.

I must also add my very special thanks to all the Grand Lodge Officers,
elected and appointed, who fulfilled their duties beyond those that are simply
stated in our Masonic code.

Every Grand Master has the opportunity to appoint a corps of officers
to assist him throughout his term. This year’s appointed officers are second to
none. They were chosen for their devotion to the craft, and more specifically for
their dedication to youth. They have traveled up and down the state of
California with Virginia, and with me and we extend to this Grand Family our
sincerest gratitude for the tremendous support given to us this entire year.

Section 2, Article VII of the first Constitution approved by our Grand
Lodge on April 19, 1850, established that:

“On the first day of the regular meeting of the Grand Lodge, or as soon
thereafter as possible, the Grand Master shall lay before the Grand Lodge a
written message; therein detailing an account of his official acts during the
recess, and the state and condition of Masonry in his jurisdiction; and shall
recommend to the Grand Lodge such measures as he may deem expedient and
necessary.”

To those who believe that modern life has compromised Masonic traditions,
here I am, 150 years after that mandate, standing in front of you to give my


38 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

account as the 135th Grand Master of Masons in California, the same as all my
predecessors have done before me.

The few minutes afforded me will not be sufficient to list all the
accomplishments, programs, celebrations and activities that have taken place
during this year. Nevertheless, let me report a few of the special events where
the light of Masonic spirit has shown brightest.

Sesquicentennial Celebration

During the Annual Communication in 1984 the membership of this
Grand Lodge approved a recommendation by Most Worshipful E. Arthur
Haglund to begin planning for a Sesquicentennial Celebration. Each Grand
Master from that time gave special consideration to this matter.

On April 19 of this year the Masonic Grand Lodge of California turned
150 years old. This entire year has been one of celebrating our Sesquicentennial
in a memorable way. For example, Grand Lodge assembled in our state’s
capitol on April 13, 14, and 15 to commit a full weekend for commemorating
the founding of the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted
Masons of the State of California on April 19, 1850. Although the weekend was
filled with many events, two stand out.

On April 13 I was received on the floor of the California State Senate,
together with Most Worshipful Ronald Robinson, Grand Master of the Prince
Hall Grand Lodge of California and Hawaii, and presented a framed certificate
commemorating our 150th Anniversary. This occasion was historic, not only
because it was the first time a Grand Master of Masons in California had been
received on the Senate Floor, but because this was the first time that the Prince
Hall Grand Lodge had been equally recognized for its historic role in the
development of Freemasonry in California. The harmonious fellowship existing
between our two grand lodges was visible to the political leadership of the State
of California, and a new era of fraternal goodwill began.

The ceremony on the Senate Floor was followed that same morning by
the recognition of five state senators and one state assemblyman who are
Freemasons. The Grand Lodge Officers, several hundred masons and their
families assembled on the west steps of the Capitol and there I presented to each
legislator Sesquicentennial Certificates from the Masonic Grand Lodge of
California to commemorate this auspicious occasion in a moving and patriotic
ceremony. Those legislators spoke in appreciation of the honor received and
praised the meaning of Masonry in their lives. Perhaps raising many eyebrows
of those legislators who, for the first time, realized that despite opposing
political opinions, Masons find ways to work together inspired by the same
principles.

On April 14 Grand Lodge opened in the Hall of Tehama Lodge No. 3,
one of the founding lodges of the Grand Lodge. The recount continued in the


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 39

spirit of 1850, when the Grand Lodge Officers boarded horse drawn carriages
and processed to a place near Sth and J Streets where Grand Lodge was
organized. There a bronze plaque was dedicated which is now affixed to the
building that occupies the site where Grand Lodge was formed. We thus
celebrated the creation of a Grand Lodge for this state, as well as the good works
of our brethren for the past 150 years.

The successful activities in Sacramento were the result of the
Sesquicentennial Committee’s exceptional work under the leadership of
Worshipful Brother Larry Adamson, Sunset Lodge No. 369 and Worshipful
Brother Gary Young, Tehama Lodge No. 3. To them and to all the members of
this special committee, I extend my sincere appreciation for their efforts in
making these the crowning days of our yearlong celebration.

History Book

The Grand Lodge History Committee, under the able leadership of
Most Worshipful Charles H. Alexander, produced a magnificent 150th year
history volume of the Masonic Grand Lodge of California. This volume is
appropriately titled, “California Freemasonry 1850-2000. The Past Fifty Years.”
If you have not already purchased a copy, you should do so before the close of
this Annual Communication. When they are sold, there will not be another
printing. The product was a labor of love that deserves our sincere thanks to
each member of the Masonic History Committee.

Special Issue of the California Freemason Magazine

Special recognition must also be given to the Sesquicentennial Issue of
the California Freemason, with its sundry articles covering 150 years of Grand
Lodge history and the Sacramento Celebration. For more than twenty-six years
now Right Worshipful Ralph Head has been our editor and in charge of
publication.

Brother Head recently created an electronic version of the California
Freemason on our web page including articles that can be downloaded by
anyone interested in learning more about our Fraternity. Once again we
recognize Brother Head for his dedication to Masonry and for his invaluable
work for this Grand Lodge.

My Brethren, I have talked at length about the central event of our year,
the Sesquicentennial, and I did so because it is important for us to evaluate the
past, not only to remember those who throughout these 150 years did the most
for Masonry, but to learn from their experiences what we must do today. We
build upon the work of our predecessors and hopefully provide a legacy for
those who will follow us.


40 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Grand Lodge
Membership

The number of members in our lodges continued to decline during this
1999/2000 Grand Lodge year. Although we lost a smaller percentage to death
this year than in previous years, the trend can only be attributed to a smaller base
of membership. As off June 30 of this year we had the following statistics:

Total Master Masons: 90,914
Deaths reported: 3,383
Resignations: 977
Suspended for Non-Payment of Dues: 1,184
Dues Remitted: 2,433
Assessable for Per Capita: 88,481

We can do nothing about the deaths, and probably little about the
discontinuations of multiple memberships. However, there is a great deal that
we can do about members suspended for non-payment of dues, and a great deal
that we can do about the number of replacements for those who die or resign. In
the previous three years we did experience a modest growth in the number of
Entered Apprentices. This year the trend reversed itself, with 205 fewer Entered
Apprentices this year than last year. This is at a time when the population of
California is growing and we have a robust economy.

It seems to me as Grand Master that we have not yet taken seriously the
fact that men need Masonry. I do not believe that there are fewer men today
who would make good Masons than in earlier times when Freemasonry
flourished. Unfortunately, many of our lodges are so preoccupied with mere
survival that they lack a vision for the future of Freemasonry. If we truly
believed in the Mission of Masonry — in its value to our families and our
communities — we would have men beating down the doors to join our lodges.
And if each one of us placed as high a value on Freemasonry as we do with
many other things that occupy our attention, we would be overflowing with
enthusiasm to tell others about it. Some of our lodges are growing, some have
more candidates than they can comfortably accommodate in a regular monthly
schedule, and some are so deeply involved in their local communities that their
disappearance would be seen as a serious loss by the local citizens.
Unfortunately, that is not universally the case, and in all too many lodges there
is no sense of community involvement, no sense of the impact of the teachings
of Masonry on its members, no effort expended except on finding enough
officers to fill the line for the ensuing year. When we once more believe as
passionately in Freemasonry as did our forbearers, when a lodge once more has
greater value to its members than sporting events on television, and when we
take Freemasonry home with us after the close of lodge, then, and only then will
these statistical trends change.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 4l

Leadership Training
Wardens’ Leadership Retreats

The Wardens’ Leadership Retreats are now in their tenth year of
operation. Although they are our premier leadership training program, they still
only attract about half our lodges each year. I set a goal at the beginning of this
year to increase attendance by 10%. That goal was not reached, although more
attended this year than last. I am appreciative of the leadership of Wor. Bro.
Fred Sorsabal who took over the Wardens’ Retreats this year. I believe that the
retreats will continue to be one of the best bargains in Masonry in California.

Officers’ Management Workshops

Another important leadership training program is the Officers’
Management Workshops. This year the workshops were delivered to outlying
areas — areas far from our metropolitan centers — to afford lodge officers a
greater opportunity to attend. The Officers’ Management Workshops are geared
for the beginning Masonic leader— the officers just starting up the line who need
to know some of the fundamentals about lodge management. My thanks go to
Chairman Rick Hutchinson, and his team of presenters, who delivered seven
outstanding workshops this year.

Other Leadership Training Opportunities

In addition to the Wardens’ Leadership Retreats, and Officers’
Management Workshops, we held two Inspectors’ Leadership and Management
Workshops, one in Northern California and one in Southern California. These
were designed to help our Inspectors become more effective leaders in our
Fraternity, and give them the base of knowledge to help them support our other
leadership training efforts. In addition, the Grand Secretary continued to offer
six administrative workshops as he has done in past years. In all these
endeavors we have attempted to help our local lodge leadership to become
effective officers, and thus, promote more effective lodge management and
administration.

Long Range Planning

This year the Long Range Planning Committee completed its second
year of activity. A year ago the committee reported that it had developed a
Mission Statement for Freemasonry in California, and established five strategic
priorities for this Grand Lodge to follow.

The five strategic priorities are:

= To be well governed, organized and managed.


42 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

" To be well led, with many opportunities to lead, and become a leader.

" To attract, satisfy, develop and retain our members.

= To be a fraternity that makes good men better, with members who un-
derstand and practice the tenets of Freemasonry.

" To be a relevant and member-centered organization of men of high
quality.

The Committee met this past year to refine these five strategic
priorities, and to recommend programs to implement them. The Fourth
Strategic Priority received special attention, and the adoption of the Mentoring
Program was a result of their concentration on this priority.

Mentoring Program

The Mentoring Program is simple in concept, but profound in its
implications for the Fraternity and its future growth. In 1998 Grand Lodge
voted to shorten the candidate’s proficiencies for the First and Second Degrees.
It was commonly believed that mere memorization did not constitute an
understanding of Freemasonry, and that the advancement of candidates would
benefit by a shortening of the amount of memory work required. Unfortunately,
at that time no alternative was adopted to promote greater understanding of
Freemasonry. The Long Range Planning Committee felt that this was a formula
for disaster, and that we could have a whole generation of Masons who knew
little about Freemasonry or the fundamental reasons that they were Masons in
the first place. I accordingly charged the Masonic Education Committee to
develop: (1) a Mentoring Manual, as well as (2) a pilot program to train local
lodge leaders in the implementation of a quality Mentoring program. The pilot
program is now ready to go.

Our Deputy Grand Master, Right Worshipful David C. Decker, has
adopted this program as a priority during his term as Grand Master. My thanks
go to Most Worshipful Jack R. Levitt, Grand Master in 1987, for his leadership
in this effort, and to Brother Bill Wine, Chairman of the Masonic Education
Committee, for a significant accomplishment for Grand Lodge this year. I am
confident that the next generation of Masons will be “well-skilled in our Ancient
Craft.”

Diamond Veteran’s Awards

This year I have presented 15 Seventy-Five Year Pins to members who
have achieved this significant milestone in their lives. Since, of necessity, they
became Masons after the age of 21, you can readily see that the youngest was 96
years of age, and some were older still. It was a great privilege to present these
tokens of our esteem for these venerable brethren, whose lives have chronicled
so much of Masonic history in our state. In addition, 2135 Golden Veteran’s


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 43

Awards, presented for the most part by our Inspectors on behalf of Grand
Lodge. This large number of 50 and 75 year awards reflects the great years of
growth in the Craft during earlier generations. My vision is to see a renewal of
Freemasonry in California, so that another Grand Master may have the privilege
of presenting a similar number of awards 75 years from now.

Public Relations Program

The Public Relations Program was launched in 1998 when Grand
Lodge approved a special one-time per capita assessment to let the public know
that ‘Freemasonry was alive and active in the present generation’. Year two
focused on building from that sound foundation to refine the program. To
achieve this goal, I commissioned a study by a public relations firm in San
Diego. Their charge was to determine the most effective way of telling the story
of Freemasonry to our “prime time” audience. The study focused on
determining where — young men between the ages of twenty-five and forty—
would ordinarily look for information. That study noted the following:

= Most young men get their primary information from the Internet today.
Though print media and television might be important, it is the Internet
where young men go to find out about organizations like Freemasonry.

= They are likely to be interested in an organization, like Freemasonry, if
it enhances commitments to their families and to their communities.
Surveys of Masons in this age group demonstrated that the best known
project was the Child I.D. program, because they readily identified with
its purposes.

= Lodges that are active in their local community are much more likely to
receive an application from a young man than those that are not. And
lodges that welcome their wives and children, are probably the only
ones that will keep a new Mason on their rolls.

Our published materials need to be contemporary, reflecting a positive
image of Freemasonry that emphasizes commitments to the community and to
the family. The use of print media, such as newspapers, should be coordinated
with local lodges that have a significant presence in their local communities.
“Broadcast” advertising probably is not worth the time or the money.
“Focused” advertising, coupled with “newsworthy” local events, is money well-
spent.

I am pleased to report that the following actions have been taken:


44

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Our Internet site, www.freemason.org, now has a newly designed Por-
tal Page, and a newly designed page for non-masons inquiring about
Freemasonry.

We have a newly designed information pamphlet that tells the public
about Freemasonry in contemporary terms. This pamphlet is available
to our lodges to use in connection with their community programs, and
in particular, with the Child I.D. Program.

We have a new “press kit”, to be used by Grand Lodge, and by our
lodges, to get proper, accurate, and current information in the hands of
the local press.

We have successfully launched a pilot program of targeted paid adver-
tisements in selected local newspapers, using two lodges in Southern
California, and one in Northern California. We will assess the efficacy
of these paid advertisements and expand the program over the next
year. Our Deputy Grand Master has given his full support to the ex-
pansion of this pilot program.

Once these new projects are firmly in place the Public Relations team
will turn to some of our long-awaited other projects. Grand Lodge has
received financial support from the Homes Board, the Board of the
California Masonic Foundation, the Board of the California Masonic
Memorial Temple, and the Board of Nob Hill Masonic Management.
In addition, we have received pledges from the York and Scottish Rites,
and from the Eastern Star and Amaranth, among others. The third
phase of our public relations project will use these pledges to tell the
public about the Family of Freemasonry — those organizations who are
closely associated with Grand Lodge, and who are partners in our Pub-
lic Relations Project. If nothing else, Freemasonry in California is
much more visible than it was just two short years ago.

Financial Health of Grand Lodge

It is always a pleasure to report on the ‘good state’ of financial health.

The financial outlook of Grand Lodge has never been better. Ten years ago we
resolved to live within our means without compromising our responsibilities as a
Grand Lodge.

You have met the challenge by supporting increases in the per capita to

offset increases in the costs of services provided to you. And we have met the
challenge by trimming the budget each year to match the lower income from
reduced numbers of members.

Upon presenting the budget to you last October I pledged a balanced

budget, and a prudent reserve. I also pledged that I would work with our future
leaders to guard the resources available for them so that the programs of Grand


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 45

Lodge would be guaranteed for the future as well as for the present. I have
succeeded, not only because of your support for the per capita, but with the
support and help of our Inspectors, committeemen, and our officers. The
delivery of better services has been a long-term goal of your Grand Lodge, and
we are doing that more effectively every year.

Masonic Homes Endowment Fund

For a dozen years contributions to the Masonic Homes Endowment
Fund had declined steadily. The decline in our membership was partly
responsible, but a decline in the participation of our membership was also to
blame. With the full support of the Masonic Homes Board, I challenged the
Masonic Homes Endowment Committee to do something about it. Under the
able leadership of its chairman, Wor. Bro. Richard Pumerantz, that challenge
was accepted. For the first time within memory, more money was raised for our
Masonic Homes this year than the previous year! And we received larger
donations in every category.

On behalf of those who most need our help — the residents of our
Masonic Homes and those receiving other means of support - THANK YOU!

Child I.D. Program

The Child I.D. Program continues to be the best-known program
among all Masons, especially younger Masons. We can justly take credit for a
significant service when we are able to go to the public with programs that help
reassure parents that their children will be safer because of work that Masons
perform.

My thanks go to those who organized the program, especially to
Worshipful Brother Allen Winter, the Chairman of the Child I.D. Program. We
also cannot forget the Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation
for funding the program, and to those lodges who have purchased machines and
donated them to the Foundation. In a very real sense, the Child I.D. Program is
one of our biggest and most important public service projects.

Masonic Student Assistance Program

The Masonic Student Assistance Program continues to serve the public
schools of our state while growing in reputation with our public school teachers
every year. This year we sponsored eleven workshops and five “refresher”
courses for teachers who had previously participated in the program. This
program is sponsored and operated by the California Masonic Foundation, with
the assistance of the Masonic Homes of California, which underwrites the costs


46 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

of housing and food for the participants. Together these two entities have made
this program one that has earned a national reputation for excellence.

Teacher of the Year Program

In addition, the Foundation sponsors awards for the California State
“Teacher of the Year” Program. Each of the five top winners in this program is
given a $1,000 stipend to spend on his or her classroom. It has been my
privilege to present these awards.

Also a $1,000 check is presented to each new school that participates in
a Masonic cornerstone laying ceremony. Twelve, $1,000 checks have been
presented on behalf of the California Masonic Foundation to help new schools
with essential books and supplies. Freemasonry has earned a well-justified
reputation as a strong supporter of public education in California.

Unity Statement

A debate has raged this past year over the decision of the Imperial
Shrine of North America to discontinue its past practice of requiring the Scottish
and York Rites as a condition of membership. This debate was made more
frenzied by a similar decision of the Imperial Shrine to permit expelled Masons
from Arkansas to remain Shriners despite their expulsion. As Grand Master I
have been asked my position on this issue many times. I therefore feel that it is
important to let you know where I stand.

The Grand Lodge of California will not recognize as Master Masons
any Masons who have been expelled from a Lodge in any other jurisdiction,
including Arkansas. That is our Masonic law in California. At the same time, it
is not necessary that we become involved in the internal affairs of the Shrine of
North America. The seven Shrine temples in California have been very
supportive of your Grand Lodge, and of me as Grand Master, this year. Until
the issue becomes one that affects us directly in California, I cannot support any
efforts to punish the Shrine for their decision. Time may reveal to them that this
decision was unwise, and in the meantime we need to help and support the
Shrine in California as a part of the Family of Freemasonry. Unity means, in
part, that we stand with them when they may be wrong, with the hopethat better
judgment may prevail, and that peace and harmony will be restored when it
does.

Miscellaneous

The Grand Master of Masons in California is the Chief Executive
Officer of the Masonic Grand Lodge of California, and I had the privilege of
representing you at two important conferences outside of the state during my


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 47

term of office. In February Virginia and I traveled to Savannah, Georgia, to
attend the Conference of Grand Masters of Masons in North America. The
conference is not only an opportunity to meet other Grand Masters, but an
opportunity to learn from other jurisdictions, and to share information with them
about Freemasonry in California. This year there were discussion groups
focusing on activities in four areas: The Internet as a means of Masonic
communication; public relations; conducting lodge business on the First
Degree; and membership development. Needless to say I had much to report on
these topics from California, and also found the information from other grand
lodges to be a valuable addition to my own.

In May the Deputy Grand Master and I went to Sitka, Alaska, to attend
the Western Conference of Grand Lodges. While there, I presented a major
paper on membership development, particularly, on the subject of “membership
proposals”. It was out of this experience that I developed my Recommendation
No. 2, which will be considered by you at this Annual Communication. The
experience of sharing with other leaders in Freemasonry is an important and
valuable one, and I am proud to tell you that the Grand Lodge of California is
respected and listened to in the international world of Masonic Grand Lodges.

Finally — the great influence upon my life is Virginia. She has been my
constant companion, sharing the challenging times during these past four years.
She has always been at my side, keeping me on course. To her, I extend my
love as my wife and my sincere thanks as my best friend.

And now — your approval of my stewardship in the execution of my
responsibilities is very important. I strive to practice “self-giving” and Dr.
Norman Vincent Peale said it this way: “When you come detached mentally
from yourself and concentrate on helping other people with their difficulties,
you will be able to cope with your own more effectively.” The value of giving,
taught by Freemasonry, has had such a pervasive impact on my life that I cannot
imagine where I would be today if it were not for the principles of our fraternity
and the friendships of Masonic brethren.

Thank you for giving me this opportunity to serve as your Grand
Master. God Bless you and good luck to us all.

Respectfully submitted,
Alvin J. Weis, Grand Master

MARCH 2000 ~ MASONIC YOUTH ORDERS MONTH

Whereas, the Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of California holds that the
Masonic Youth Orders are major contributors to the vitality, growth and renewal of
Masonry in general and in California in particular, and

Whereas, the California Youth Orders provide the environment whereby the principles
of Freemasonry, namely: Faith, Hope and Charity can be inculcated in future generations
through the values taught and exemplified by the Youth Orders, namely: The International
Order of Job's Daughters, The International Order of the Rainbow for Girls and The
International Order of DeMolay, and

Whereas, the Masonic Youth Orders teach the values of Charity, Cleanness,
Comradeship, Faith, Patriotism, Reverence for God and Honor of Parents and Guardians
and which when taken together with lessons from the Holy Writings provide assurance
that the young men and women who avail themselves to these valuable lessons will use
them as a guide and source of strength as they assume the responsibilities of adulthood,
and

Whereas, the Membership of the Masonic Youth Orders can have significant impact on
the future membership and vitality of Freemasonry, its Concordant and Affiliated Bodies
and are a source for future leaders of our fraternal family, communities and nation, and

Whereas, the Masonic Fraternity has a long history of concern for the moral and
political health of society, and it is therefore in the best interest of Masonry to set aside a
‘special time and with extra effort help nurture and develop the young men, women, and
the adult leaders of the Job’s Daughters, Rainbow for Girls and DeMolay.

I therefore proclaim the Month of March to be

MASONIC YOUTH ORDERS MONTH

and direct that this Proclamation is to be read by the Master at the Stated Meetings of each
lodge in the months of February and March and, where practicable, that it also be
published in the Lodge Bulletins or distributed to each member.

Dated January 1, 2000

Fraternally,

Alvin ef

Grand Master

PROCLAMATION
APRIL 2000 ~ PUBLIC SCHOOLS MONTH

“PUBLIC EDUCATION ~ OPENS THE DOOR TO A LIFETIME OF OPPORTUNITIES”

Concern for the common good and well-being of all citizens is one of the highest virtues of American democracy. Generations
ago our ancestors recognized the importance of this and created the public schools system. The founders of public education were seeking
to achieve more than teaching young people to read, write, and cipher. They believed that a system of publicly supported schools ought
to:

e Prepare people to become responsible citizens;
e Improve social conditions;

e Promote cultural unity; and

e Help people become economically self-sufficient.

Although the public schools have problems today that need to be addressed, there are ways to improve the schools without
undermining the basic concept of public education. As we weigh various proposals for education reform, we must not forget that our
forefathers developed public schools to unify our nation and to provide for the common good.

As individual Masons and as citizens of this great Country, we should occupy ourselves with the preservation of this system. We
can and should consider ourselves the guardians of a sacred trust.

Therefore, it is my fraternal request that each officer and member of every lodge in the state take an active part to the greatest
extent possible in the annual observance of public schools in their communities. Our theme for this seventieth annual event is:

“PUBLIC EDUCATION ~ OPENS THE DOOR TO A LIFETIME OF OPPORTUNITIES.”
I therefore proclaim the month of April to be:
PUBLIC SCHOOLS MONTH and
1 That all Lodges contact the schools in their areas to determine what activities or programs have been included in the

calendar for a week in April in recognition of Public Schools week, and where feasible, assist in planning and
implementation of this event;

2. That each and every lodge make every reasonable effort to promote Public Schools Week to the greatest extent possible,
and encourage every member and all Masons to visit a public school during the week of public schools observance; and
3. Thatno other Lodge programs or meetings, except Stated Meetings or those events supporting and celebrating our public

schools be held during the selected week for Public Schools Observance.

I further direct that this Proclamation be read aloud by the Master of the Lodge at the stated meeting for the months of March and
April and where practicable, that it also be published in the Lodge Bulletins or distributed to each member.

Dated: January 31, 2000
Fraternally,

Alvin ef.

Grand Master

LIVING THE DREAM ~ BUILDING A BETTER TOMORROW

PROCLAMATION
150 YEARS OF FREEMASONRY IN CALIFORNIA

‘Whereas, on April 5, 1850 four California Master Masons from four different Lodges, did cause to be
published in a California newspaper, a notice of proceedings at which a resolution was adopted that these
Brothers were taking steps to organize a just and legally constituted Grand Lodge of California; and

Whereas, a copy of that resolution was forwarded to every legally constituted Lodge throughout the
state; and

‘Whereas, the convention to establish a Grand Lodge in California met in Sacramento on April 17, 1850,
at 10:00 a.m., having satisfied themselves that there were enough duly and constitutionally warranted Lodges in
California to authorize the formation of a Grand Lodge, the Brethren present spent the day of April 18° drafting
and adopting a Constitution; and

Whereas, on the morning of April 19, 1850, a Lodge of Master Masons was opened “for the purpose of
organizing and opening in Masonic form” the Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of California. Then at
High Twelve on April 19, 1850, in the City of Sacramento, The Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of Free and
Accepted Masons in California was opened in AMPLE FORM.

I therefore proclaim the year 2000 to be:

THE SESQUICENTENNIAL YEAR OF THE GRAND LODGE OF
FREE AND ACCEPTED MASONS OF CALIFORNIA

and that the Lodges will put forth a special effort to:

1. Acquaint and familiarize the brethren of this jurisdiction with the history of the California Grand
Lodge;

2. Acquaint and familiarize the brethren of each individual lodge of THEIR OWN unique, colorful,
and yet collective history;

St Develop programs and observances celebrating the 150 years of Masonry under the Grand Lodge
of California, and encourage the participation of brethren, their families and friends; and

4. Actively promote men, good and true, to membership in the Blue Lodges of California.

I further direct that this proclamation be read aloud by the Master at the stated meeting for the months of
March and April and, where practicable, that it also be published in the Lodge Bulletins or distributed to each
member.
Dated: January 31, 2000
Fraternally,
hee J/Weis
Grand Master

LIVING THE DREAM ~ BUILDING A BETTER TOMORROW

PROCLAMATION
MAY 2000 ~ MASONIC HOMES ENDOWMENT MONTH

Faith, hope, and charity, but the greatest of these is charity. The word “charity” means much more
than just giving. It means loving one another. This is particularly fitting to Masons “who are linked together
by an indissoluble chain of sincere affection.”

Our Masonic obligations cannot be better exemplified than through the existence of our two Homes
in Union City and Covina. Through the years, both Homes have provided security, companionship, and
loving care for our older Brothers and Sisters. At the Home in Covina, children are given the love and
stability that might otherwise have been lost to them. The Nonresident Assistance (NRA) program provides
monthly financial support to those who wish to remain in their community and has enabled them to live
independently.

Each year the Masons in California continue to demonstrate their belief in the principles of brotherly
love and relief by contributing to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund. A perpetual fund that provides for
the care of those placed in our charge. One has only to visit these beautiful facilities but once to be
impressed by the tremendous amount of time and work required for the upkeep and maintenance of the
grounds and buildings.

As our costs climb, so must our dedication to caring for our own. Now is the time for all of us to
help! By every Mason in California contributing what we feel we can, we can reach this year’s goal of one
million dollars.

I therefore proclaim the month of May to be

MASONIC HOMES ENDOWMENT MONTH
and direct that this Proclamation is to be read by the Master at the Stated Meetings of each lodge in the
months of April and May and, where practicable, that it also be published in the Lodge Bulletins or
distributed to each member.

Dated: February 7, 2000

Fraternally,

 

LIVING THE DREAM ~ BUILDING A BETTER TOMORROW

Whereas, The Constitution of the United States of America is the most brilliant and, most gealy perfect

governmental document ever written by man, and

Whereas, ‘Bhs Constitution of the United States of America is the Law of the Land and guarantees our
individual freedoms, and

Whereas, The freedoms guaranteed in the Constitution of the United States of America over 200 yyears ago
enabled Americans to live freely without governmental oppression and interference, and

Whereas, Thirty-nine of the men who signed this magnificent document were either known or Believed to
be Ss and

Wherelg thi mation owes it’s great prosperity and leadership ‘itr-science, medicine, technology and
‘industry tothe creativity of men and women freed by the Consfitution of the United States of
America, and

Whereas, The anniversary\of the Constitution of the United States of America, September 17, 1787 and
receives no national recognition, and

Whereas, The threat of our loss of freedoms, as a self-governing people, is always but one generation away,
if our Constitution is not taught from generation to generation.

I therefore proclaim the Month of September shall be designated
MASONIC CONSTITUTIONAL OBSERVANCE MONTH
and each Lodge of Freemasons in this Jurisdiction shall, during the month of September, either

independently or in conjunction with other Lodges, set aside at least one day to commemorate this
occasion; and

That when possible, this program should be a community event, open to the public and held in a park or
other suitable place appropriate for such an observance.

I further direct that this Proclamation is to be read aloud by the Master of each Lodge at the stated meeting
for the Months of August and September, or published in the trestleboard, and that the local
/ observance is to be given the widest publicity possible.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 69

REPORT OF THE DEPUTY GRAND MASTER
2000-2001 Grand Lodge Budget
David C. Decker, Deputy Grand Master

Brethren, Section 7040 of the California Masonic Code requires the
Deputy Grand Master to prepare the Grand Lodge Budget for submission to
Grand Lodge each year. A copy of that Budget was sent to you more than 30
days ago in advance of the convening of Grand Lodge, as required by our law.

On August 21 each Lodge was mailed the proposed Grand Lodge
Budget for 2000/2001. The funding for that budget came primarily from the
July 1, 2000 per capita which has already been paid by our Lodges. The budget
as presented therefore reflects an accurate statement of income and the projected
expenditures are also an accurate reflection of our anticipated costs. As you will
note, despite several thousand fewer members this year, the budget holds the
line on expenditures and actually represents a reduction over the previous year.

The budget represents an investment in people and programs by our
Grand Lodge. It is prudent and responsible, but at the same time, it reflects our
commitment to the future of Freemasonry in this jurisdiction. It represents no
compromise on quality, but insures that our future leaders have the resources to
carry through on today’s promises.

I pledge to you my continued watchful care over the resources of this
Grand Lodge and I ask you to support the budget which is before you.
