MEMORANDUM

DATE: August 21, 2000
TO: All Secretaries of All California Lodges

FROM: David C. Decker, Deputy Grand Master
C. Ray Whitaker, Senior Grand Warden

SUBJECT: 2000-2001 GRAND LODGE BUDGET
2001-2002 GRAND LODGE PER CAPITA

Section 7040 of the California Masonic Code requires the Deputy Grand Master to prepare the
Grand Lodge Budget for submission to Grand Lodge at the next Annual Communication. That
budget is prepared with the assistance of the Grand Lodge Committee on Finance and the Grand
Treasurer. It is based on the per capita adopted by Grand Lodge at the previous Annual
Communication, and collected from each Lodge in accordance with the number of Master Masons
carried on the rolls of the Lodge as of June 30 next preceding the forthcoming Annual
Communication. At the 150" Annual Communication in October, 1999, the per capita was
established at $18.86 (including $1.35 for the California Freemason magazine). As of June 30,
2000, our Lodges reported 90,914 Master Masons, less 2,433 whose dues had been remitted. The
net assessable membership is therefore 88,481 Master Masons (a 4% decline from a year ago).

2000-2001 Grand Lodge Budget:

The 2000 per capita of $18.86 assessed on the basis of 88,481 Master Masons is expected to produce
a revenue of $1,668,751. This year’s per capita revenue is $11,557 less than last year due to the
decline in assessable members. Miscellaneous income, including interest income and the dividend
from Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc., will result in a total budgeted income of $1,853,981.

The budgeted expenses for 2000-2001 are $1,588,901, plus $129,460 for publishing the California
Freemason magazine. The difference between income and total expenses is $135,620, and is
allocated to Depreciation Reserve ($80,000), and the Grand Lodge Endowment Fund ($55,620). The
Grand Lodge Endowment Fund was established to help offset future declines in membership, thus
helping to soften the impact of the rapid decline in membership expected to continue over the next
few years. The Endowment Fund was established by a vote of Grand Lodge to smooth out future
increases in per capita by reserving some present income for future need.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 71

Memorandum
August 21, 2000
Page Two

As we continue to see a decline in our membership, it becomes increasingly evident each Grand
Master must continue his commitment to reducing the budget and the membership must support that
commitment by increasing per capita to offset inflationary costs. The Grand Lodge budget for 2000-
2001 has a 4% increase to meet the rising costs of inflation.

Wu0 COL Q
David C. Decker
Deputy Grand Master

2001-2002 Grand Lodge Per Capita:

In accordance with the principles above, the 2000-2001 Proposed Budget recommends that the per
capita for 2001 be established at $19.61, which is a 4% increase. With income expected to drop by
another 4% to 5% in 2001 from the continuing decrease in membership, this increase will allow us
to support programs important to your Lodge and to our Grand Lodge.

As you study the attached pages, please be reminded that figures represent many hours of hard work
to bring you a responsible budget based on the best information possible. It represents a
commitment to prudently manage the resources of Grand Lodge now, and for the future. And it
represents my commitment to you to continue to strengthen our beloved Fraternity by ensuring a

sound financial base of operations for your Grand Lodge.

/ C. Ray Whitaker

APPENDIX C

 




GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA 79

REPORT OF THE SENIOR GRAND WARDEN
Per Capita Recommendation for 2001-2002
C. Ray Whitaker, Senior Grand Warden

This is the per capita recommendation for 2001-2002.

In accordance with the principles outlined by our Grand Master, the
2000-2001 proposed budget recommends that the per capita for 2001 be
established at $19.61, which is a 4% increase. With the income of the Grand
Lodge expected to drop by another 4% to 5% in 2001 from the continuing
decrease in membership, this increase will allow us to support programs
important to your Lodge and to our Grand Lodge.

The continued reduction in expenses reflects the reality we face. While
we also recognize the need to support important programs and activities, the
proposed 2001 per capita includes an amount for the California Freemason
magazine, our constant and prime means of communicating with our members.
Please keep in mind that the 2001-2002 proposed budget and per capita needed
to fund it, represents many hours of hard work necessary to bring you a
responsible budget based on past performance and current available information.
It represents a commitment to prudently manage the resources of the Grand
Lodge now and for the future. It also represents my commitment to you to
continue to strengthen our beloved Fraternity by ensuring a sound financial base
of operations for your Grand Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 83
REPORT OF THE GRAND TREASURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

The Grand Treasurer is charged with the responsibility of reporting on
the financial condition of Grand Lodge and its related investments, businesses
and charities at the Annual Communication. This includes the financial
statements, operations and planning for Grand Lodge, as well as for those of the
Masonic Homes of California, the Masonic Homes Pooled Income Fund, the
California Masonic Retirement Plan, the California Masonic Foundation, the
California Masonic Memorial Temple, and the Nob Hill Masonic Management,
Inc. (please refer to the opinion letters attached in the annual Proceedings by
your auditors).

The reports and comments of the Grand Treasurer are based on the
official examinations and statements of our Certified Public Accountants whose
reports are an integral part of the Grand Treasurer’s Report. Their audits cover
the years most recently ended and contain both a complete statement of assets
under management as well as a record of the income and expenses for the
period.

As delegates to the Annual Communication, you are, in effect, the
shareholders of Grand Lodge and its entities. You are encouraged, and it is
recommended therefore, that you read and review this report. Your observations
are appreciated.

Both the Grand Lodge and each of its related entities are in sound
financial condition. The extent and size of our investments, businesses, and
most importantly our charitable activities were financially possible in large
measure due to the generosity of prior generations. To ensure the future of these
fine programs, we must continue our Masonic tradition of financial support.

Respectfully submitted,
Thomas C. Holm, Grand Treasurer

ARTHUR ANDERSEN

REPORT OF INDEPENDENT PUBLIC ACCOUNTANTS

To The Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of Free
and Accepted Masons of the State of California:

We have audited the accompanying consolidating statement of financial position of The Most Worshipful
Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of the State of California (the Grand Lodge) (a nonprofit,
unincorporated fraternal organization) and Subsidiary as of October 3 1, 2000, and the related consolidating
statement of activities and changes in net assets and consolidated statement of cash flows for the year then
ended. These financial statements and the schedules referred to below are the responsibility of the Grand
Lodge’s management. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on these financial statements and schedules
based on our audit.

We conducted our audit in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States. Those
standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the
financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audit includes examining, on a test basis, evidence
supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial statements. An audit also includes assessing the
accounting principles used and significant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall
financial statement presentation. We believe that our audit provides a reasonable basis for our opinion.

In our opinion, the financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all material respects, the
individual and consolidated financial positions of the Grand Lodge and Subsidiary as of October 3 1, 2000,
and the individual and consolidated results of their operations and their consolidated cash flows for the year
then ended in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States.

Our audit was made for the purpose of forming an opinion on the basic financial statements taken as a whole.
The accompanying Supplemental Schedules I to V are presented for purposes of additional analysis and are
not a required part of the basic financial statements. This information has been subjected to the auditing
procedures applied in our audit of the basic financial statements and, in our opinion, is fairly stated in all
material respects in relation to the basic financial statements taken as a whole.

(bhun ALersern LLP

San Francisco, California,

THE MOST WORSHIPFUL GRAND LODGE OF FREE AND
ACCEPTED MASONS OF THE STATE OF CALIFORNIA AND SUBSIDIARY

NOTES TO CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
OCTOBER 31, 2000

1, ORGANIZATION AND SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES:
Organization

The Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of the State of California (the Grand Lodge) is a
Nonprofit unincorporated fraternal organization and is governed by a constitution and ordinances setting forth its
Jurisdiction and authority. The jurisdiction of the Grand Lodge includes all lodges and Masons within the state of
California, and members of lodges of California, wherever residing. Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. (Nob Hill), a for-
profit California corporation, is the wholly owned subsidiary of the Grand Lodge. Nob Hill operates the California Masonic
Memorial Temple (the Temple) auditorium, parking garage, exhibit hall and various rental properties under the terms of
an operating lease with the Temple.

The Grand Lodge provides general and administrative services to its related entities: the Masonic Homes of California
(Masonic Homes); the Temple; the California Masonic Foundation (the Foundation); the California Masonic Retirement
Plan; Nob Hill; and the Masonic Homes Pooled Income Fund (collectively, the Masonic Entities). These services are
reimbursed by the related entity.

Principles of Consolidation

The financial statements include the accounts of the Grand Lodge and its wholly owned subsidiary. All material
intercompany amounts have been eliminated. The accounts of the other Masonic Entities have not been included in the
Grand Lodge's financial statements because the Grand Lodge does not have both control and an economic interest in
such related entities.

Income Tax Status

The Grand Lodge has been granted tax-exempt status for both federal income and California franchise tax purposes.
Nob Hill income taxes are accounted for under Statement of Financial Accounting Standards (SFAS) No. 109,
“Accounting for Income Taxes.”

Inventory

Inventory is stated at cost on a first-in, first-out basis.
Investments

Investments in marketable securities are stated at fair value based on quoted market prices. Gains or losses that result
from market fluctuations are recognized in the period such fluctuations occur. For the Grand Lodge, changes in
unrealized gains and losses on investments are included in investment income. For Nob Hill, such gains and losses are
excluded from investment income and are included as a component of net assets.

Property and Equipment

Property and equipment, including leasehold improvements, are recorded at cost and are depreciated using the straight-
line method over estimated useful lives of three to twenty-five years.

Collections

The Grand Lodge owns a collection of jewelry and other objects, most of which have historical significance. The jewelry
collection is used in Lodge ceremonies, and the other objects are displayed in the Temple Museum. Purchases of
collection items are reflected as decreases in unrestricted net assets in the year in which the items are acquired. The
Grand Lodge does not recognize these assets as contributions as they are protected and preserved, held for public
exhibition, and are subject to an organizational policy that requires proceeds from the sale of collection items to be used
to acquire other collection items. Accessions and deaccessions during 2000 were not significant.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 91

Deferred Per Capita Revenue
Dues received for the subsequent fiscal year are recorded as deferred per capita revenue until earned.
Unrestricted Net Assets

Unrestricted net assets are available to support all activities of the Grand Lodge, including amounts designated by the
Grand Lodge as follows:

* The Grand Lodge Endowment Fund (the Endowment Fund) was established at the Grand Lodge's
October 1993 Annual Communication. If in any fiscal year there is an excess of support and revenues over
expenses, as defined, the excess shall be added to the Endowment Fund if the cumulative excess exceeds
$700,000. For the year ended October 31, 2000, the excess added to the Endowment Fund was $213,715
resulting in an ending balance of $1,926,197.

The 150 Yearbook Fund, which had a balance of $0 as of October 31, 2000. This fund was used in 2000 to
defray the costs of commemorative items.

Temporarily Restricted Net Assets

Temporarily restricted net assets represent contributions whose use by the Grand Lodge is limited by donor-imposed
stipulations that either expire with the passage of time or can be fulfilled and removed by the Grand Lodge pursuant to
those stipulations.

Permanently Restricted Net Assets

Permanently restricted net assets are restricted by the donor for investment in perpetuity, the income from which is
available to provide grants for scholarship purposes to the California Masonic Foundation MacKechnie Fund.

Revenue Recognition

The Grand Lodge reports gifts of cash and other assets as restricted support if such gifts are received with donor
Stipulations that limit the use of the donated assets. When a donor restriction expires, that is, when a stipulated time
restriction ends or a purpose restriction is accomplished, temporarily restricted net assets are reclassified to unrestricted
net assets and reported as net assets released from restriction. Temporarily restricted contributions are reported as
temporarily restricted support and net assets released from restrictions during the period when the restriction is met in
the same period as the contribution is received.

Functional Expense Allocations

Expenses, such as depreciation and amortization, supplies, travel, personnel and occupancy costs, are allocated among
program services and general and administrative expense classifications based on employee ratios and estimates made
by the Grand Lodge’s management.

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States
requires management to make estimates and assumptions. These estimates and assumptions affect the reported
amounts of assets and liabilities and disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities at the date of the financial statements
and the reported amounts of revenue and expenses during the reporting period. Actual results could differ from those
estimates.

5. RELATED PARTIES:

The Grand Lodge provides general and administrative support to the Masonic Entities for which it is reimbursed. The
reimbursement is recorded as an offset to general and administrative expenses for the Grand Lodge. The Grand Lodge
determines the amount to be allocated to the entities on an annual basis, subject to the approval of the boards of trustees
of the Masonic Entities. Expenses allocated to the Masonic Entities in 2000 were as follows:

Masonic Homes $627,559
Temple 16,454
Foundation 23,333

Total $667,346

 

In addition, $49,973 was allocated to Nob Hill and has been eliminated in consolidation.

On June 30, 1998, Nob Hill (the lessee) and the Temple (the lessor) entered into an operating lease, expiring June 30,
2005, of the Temple auditorium, exhibit hall, parking garage and various rental properties. Under the terms of the lease,
Nob Hill is required to pay rent equal to 90 percent of the gross rental revenues in excess of a base amount of $860,000
per year, adjusted annually for inflation, until Nob Hill has paid $240,000, after which the payment shall be one-third of
the remaining gross rental revenues. Beginning in 1998, the aggregate rent calculated under this formula is subject to a
limit that increases with inflation. For the fiscal year ended June 30, 2000, rent is limited to $430,000, and for fiscal years
thereafter through June 30, 2005, is limited to $445,000. For the year ended October 31, 2000, lease expense totaled
$435,000.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 93

6. INCOME TAXES:

The provision for 2000 income taxes for Nob Hill, which is based on Statutory rates, is $210,251. The tax effect of
temporary differences between the financial statements and tax returns is insignificant.

7. PENSION PLAN:

The Grand Lodge, Masonic Homes, the Foundation, the Temple and Nob Hill sponsor a defined benefit Pension plan (the
Plan) that covers all employees. Benefits are to be Paid to eligible employees at retirement based primarily upon years of
service and compensation levels near retirement. Contributions to the Plan Teflect benefits attributed to employees’
services to date, as well as services expected to be provided in the future, Plan assets consist primarily of equity and
fixed income mutual funds.

Pension expense and other activity of the Plan for the year ended October 31, 2000, included the following components:

Pension expense $ 486,392
Employer contribution 859,300
Plan participants’ contributions 0
Benefits paid 733,516

respectively.

The prepaid pension cost has been allocated to the five contributing employers. The amount recognized by the Grand
Lodge as of October 31, 2000, was $224,301. The amount recognized by Nob Hill is an accrued pension cost of
$(31,854).

RTHUR ANDERSEN

REPORT OF INDEPENDENT PUBLIC ACCOUNTANTS

To the Board of Trustees of
Masonic Homes of California:

We have audited the accompanying statements of financial position of Masonic Homes of California (a
California not-for-profit corporation) as of June 30, 2000 and 1999, and the related statements of activities
and changes in net assets and cash flows for the years then ended. These financial statements are the
responsibility of Masonic Homes of California management. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on
these financial statements based on our audits.

We conducted our audits in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States.
Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the
financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audit includes examining, on a test basis, evidence
supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial statements. An audit also includes assessing the
accounting principles used and significant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall
financial statement presentation. We believe that our audits provide a reasonable basis for our opinion.

In our opinion, the financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all material respects, the financial
position of Masonic Homes of California as of June 30, 2000 and 1999, and the results of its operations and
its cash flows for the years then ended in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the
United States.

Our audits were made for the purpose of forming an opinion on the basic financial statements taken as a
whole. The supplementary fund information included in the accompanying statements of financial position
and statements of activities and changes in net assets is presented for purposes of additional analysis and is
not a required part of the basic financial statements. This information has been subjected to the auditing
procedures applied in our audits of the basic financial statements and, in our opinion, is fairly stated in all
material respects in relation to the basic financial statements taken as a whole.

ith. Crdlron LLP

San Francisco, California,
September 8, 2000

MASONIC HOMES OF CALIFORNIA

NOTES TO FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
JUNE 30, 2000 AND 1999

4. ORGANIZATION AND SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES:

Organization

Masonic Homes of California (Masonic Homes), a California nonprofit corporation, administers a home for adults at Union
City, California, and a home for adults and children at Covina, California. The homes are supported by The Grand Lodge
of Free and Accepted Masons of the state of California (the Grand Lodge) and members of the Masonic Fraternity in
California.

The adult residents who enter Masonic Homes are required by a life-care agreement to assign all of their assets to
Masonic Homes in exchange for continuing care for the remainder of their lives. In the event that a continuing care
contract is canceled by the resident through withdrawal within the first 90 days of residency, the resident is entitled to a
pro rata refund of all his or her assigned assets. No assigned assets were subject to refund at June 30, 2000 and 1999.

Masonic Homes is subject to statutory reserve requirements. At June 30, 2000 and 1999, Masonic Homes’ reserves as
calculated in accordance with Continuing Care Contract Statutes of the California Health and Safety Code were in
excess of such requirements.

Masonic Homes annually calculates the present value of the net cost of future services and the use of facilities to be
provided to current residents and compares that amount with the present value of related anticipated cash inflows. If the
present value of the net cost of future services and the use of facilities exceeds the present value of related anticipated
cash inflows, a liability is recorded with a corresponding charge to income. Masonic Homes has determined, using a
discount rate of 7 percent, that no accrual for the obligation to provide future services and use of facilities to current
residents was required at June 30, 2000 and 1999.

Tax-Exempt Status

Masonic Homes is a nonprofit corporation as described in Section 501(c)(3) of the Internal Revenue Code and has been
granted tax-exempt status by the Internal Revenue Service and the California Franchise Tax Board.

Basis of Presentation

Masonic Homes reports information regarding its net assets and activities according to three classes of net assets:
unrestricted, temporarily restricted and permanently restricted.

The assets, liabilities and net assets of Masonic Homes are presented in the accompanying statement of financial
position as follows:

* General and Earthquake Funds—Resources are available to support the operations of the homes at Union
City and Covina, California. The net assets of the general fund are primarily unrestricted.

«Permanent Endowment Fund—Ordinances of the Grand Lodge provide that no portion of the principal of the
Permanent Endowment Fund shall be expended for any purpose, and income from the principal shall be used
only for the maintenance and general operation of Masonic Homes. Therefore, the value of contributions made
to the Permanent Endowment Fund are permanently restricted, while the accumulated investment earnings and
net market appreciation are temporarily restricted until the associated donor restriction on use has been
satisfied.

* Union City Fund—The Union City Fund provides for the operation, rehabilitation, preservation and betterment
of the home at Union City, as deemed necessary. The Union City Fund was established with bequests from the
estates of Hugo M. Lorber and Florence V. Grider, which provided that the amounts be used for the home for
adults at Union City, California. Because the purpose restrictions related to the principal of the bequests have
been met and no donor restrictions were placed on the investment earnings and net market appreciation from
the principal, the net assets of the Union City Fund are unrestricted. The net assets of this fund have been
designated by the Board for construction purposes.


106 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

« Restricted Trust Funds—Restricted Trust Funds include bequests made for various restricted purposes and
various irrevocable trusts of which Masonic Homes is the trustee. The value of bequests is permanently
restricted for investment in perpetuity, while the earnings from the bequests are either restricted for scholarship
purposes or available for unrestricted use. Masonic Homes’ interest in the irrevocable trusts is temporarily
restricted as to time. Once received, the contribution is temporarily restricted or unrestricted according to the
donor stipulations.

Cash and Equivalents

Cash equivalents consist of highly liquid investments with original maturities of three months or less. Cash and
equivalents, for purposes of the statement of cash flows, include unrestricted and restricted cash.

Restricted cash includes residents’ accounts at Union City and Covina, which comprise unexpended portions of monthly

allowances made to residents and a construction retainer account related to construction at the homes. For the residents’
accounts, a corresponding liability for such agency funds is included in accounts payable and accrued liabilities.

Investments
Investments in debt and equity securities are stated at estimated fair market value based on quoted market prices.
Investments received through gifts are recorded at estimated fair market value at the date of donation. Unrealized gains

or losses that result from market fluctuations are recorded in the period in which such fluctuations occur. Realized gains
or losses resulting from sales or maturities of securities are calculated on a cost basis.

Assets Held for Sale

Assets held for sale consist primarily of tangible property received from residents, including real estate, that is held for
sale. Assets are recorded at appraised value on date of assignment.

Property and Equipment

Property and equipment are carried at cost and are depreciated on the straight-line method over the following estimated
lives:

Buildings and improvements 5-50 years
Equipment 3-25
Furniture and fixtures 5-20
Vehicles 2-7

Other Assets

Other assets consist primarily of notes receivable, resident-assigned assets awaiting liquidation, life insurance policies
on residents, and fine art at the two homes.

Other assigned assets are recorded at fair market value on the date of assignment. Fine art is recorded at appraised
value at the date of donation.

Deferred Revenue from Pooled Income Fund

Deferred revenue from pooled income fund represents the discount for future investment earnings on Masonic Homes’
remainder interest in the Masonic Homes Pooled Income Fund (the Pooled Income Fund) (see Note 4). Masonic Homes
has determined the amount deferred using investment returns consistent with the composition of asset portfolios, single
or joint life expectancy from IRS Publication 1457 tables, and discount rates of 7.7 and 7.7 percent at June 30, 2000 and
1999, respectively.

Liability to Beneficiary of Split-Interest Agreements

Liability to beneficiaries of split-interest agreements represents the income beneficiaries’ interest in various charitable
remainder trusts of which Masonic Homes is the trustee (see Note 4). The liability is calculated as the present value of
the estimated future cash flows to be distributed to the income beneficiaries over their expected lives. Masonic Homes
has determined the amount of the liability using investment returns consistent with the composition of the asset portfolios,
single or joint life expectancies from IRS Publication 1457, Table S, or IRS Publication 1458, Table D, as applicable, and
discount rates of 8.0 percent and 6.4 percent at June 30, 2000 and 1999, respectively.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 107

Deferred Revenue from Assigned Assets

The fair market value of real and personal property assigned to Masonic Homes by residents entering into a continuing
care contract is deferred and amortized over the actuarially determined individual or joint and last survivor life expectancy
(using the straight-line method based on the State of California Department of Social Services life expectancy table), with
any unamortized balance recognized as income upon death of the individual or last survivor,

Temporarily Restricted Net Assets

Temporarily restricted net assets represent contributions whose use by Masonic Homes is limited by donor-imposed
stipulations that either expire with the passage of time or can be fulfilled and removed by actions of Masonic Homes
pursuant to those stipulations (see Note 5).

Permanently Restricted Net Assets

Permanently restricted net assets are restricted by the donor for investment in perpetuity (see Note 6). During fiscal year
2000, Masonic Homes’ management formalized a policy whereby permanently restricted net assets consist of original
corpus plus an adjustment for inflation to maintain the purchasing power of such net assets. Management performed
calculations considering increases in the consumer price index since the original contribution dates. Based on these
calculations, management determined that permanently restricted net assets should be increased by approximately
$154 million. Transfers totaling this amount were made first from amounts previously included in unrestricted and
temporarily restricted net assets and then, in the case of the Permanent Endowment Fund, from amounts previously
included in the General and Earthquake Fund.

Revenue Recognition
Contributions are recognized as revenue when received or unconditionally promised.

Masonic Homes reports gifts of cash and other assets as restricted support if such gifts are received with donor
stipulations that limit the use of the donated assets. When a donor restriction expires, that is, when a stipulated time
restriction ends or a purpose restriction is accomplished, temporarily restricted net assets are reclassified to unrestricted
net assets and are reported as net assets released from restrictions.

Resident retirement benefits that are assigned to Masonic Homes are recognized over the life of the resident.

Functional Expense Allocations

Expenses, such as depreciation, supplies, personnel and occupancy costs, are allocated among program services and
supporting services classifications on the basis of time records and on estimates made by Masonic Homes’
management.

Fair Value of Financial Instruments

The carrying amount of cash and cash equivalents approximates fair value because of the short maturity of those
instruments. The carrying amounts of the accounts receivable and payable approximate their values. The carrying value
of investments and assets related to split-interest agreements approximates fair value because investments and assets
related to split-interest agreements are carried at estimated fair value.

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with generally accepted accounting principles requires management
to make estimates and assumptions. These estimates and assumptions affect the reported amounts of assets and
liabilities, disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities at the date of the financial statements and the reported amounts
of revenues and expenses during the reporting period. Actual results could differ from those estimates.

Contributions Receivable from Split-Interest Agreements

Contributions receivable from split-interest agreements represent the estimated net present value of Masonic Homes’
interest in various irrevocable trusts held by third-party trustees. The net present value of these receivables was
determined using investment returns consistent with the composition of the asset portfolios, single or joint life
expectancies from IRS Publication 1457, Table S, or IRS Publication 1458, Table D, as applicable, and discount rates of
8.0 percent and 6.4 percent at June 30, 2000 and 1999, respectively.

Assets of Pooled Income Fund

Assets of the Pooled Income Fund represent the fair value of assets held in the Pooled Income Fund, which was
organized in 1974 as a charitable remainder trust to which donors contribute irrevocable remainder interests in property
while retaining an income interest for life for one or more beneficiaries. All dividend and interest income of the Pooled
Income Fund is distributed quarterly to the beneficiaries, based on their proportionate share of the Pooled Income Fund.
Upon the death of each donor's last income beneficiary, the remainder interest becomes available for Masonic Homes’
unrestricted use.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 109

Assets of Split-Interest Agreements

Assets of split-interest agreements consist of cash and other assets received under various irrevocable charitable
remainder trusts of which Masonic Homes is the trustee. The assets received under these agreements are recorded at
estimated fair market value when received.

Beneficial Interests in Perpetual Trusts

Beneficial interests in perpetual trusts represent the net present value of Masonic Homes’ irrevocable interest in various
perpetual trusts held by third-party trustees. The assets of the trusts have been donor restricted for investment in
perpetuity. The income from $4,661,121 of the beneficial interests in perpetual trusts is unrestricted, and the income from
$348,245 of the beneficial interests in perpetual trusts is permanently restricted.

7. PENSION PLAN:

Masonic Homes, the Grand Lodge, Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc., the California Masonic Foundation (the
Foundation) and the California Masonic Memorial Temple sponsor a defined benefit pension plan (the Plan) that covers
all employees. Benefits are to be paid to eligible employees at retirement based primarily upon years of service and
compensation levels near retirement. Contributions to the Plan reflect benefits attributed to employees’ services to date,
as well as services expected to be provided in the future. Plan assets consist primarily of equity and fixed income mutual
funds.

Pension expense and other activity of the Plan for the years ended June 30, 2000 and 1999, consisted of the following
components:

Pension Benefits

Pension activity has been allocated to the five contributing employers. The prepaid pension expense allocated to
Masonic Homes was $964,114 and $508,518 as of June 30, 2000 and 1999, respectively. Pension expense allocated to
Masonic Homes was $405,101 and $544,260 in 2000 and 1999, respectively.

8. RELATED-PARTY TRANSACTIONS:

The Grand Lodge provides general and administrative support to Masonic Homes, for which it is reimbursed through an
allocation of certain expenses. The allocations during 2000 and 1999 were $615,266 and $575,886, respectively. In
addition, Masonic Homes provided similar administrative services to the Pooled Income Fund. Reimbursement to
Masonic Homes for these services during 2000 and 1999 was $41,920 and $5,861, respectively.

In 2000 and 1999, Masonic Homes provided a training site and incurred direct expenses for the Foundation, a related
party. The Foundation paid $64,593 and $60,215 in 2000 and 1999, respectively, to fully reimburse Masonic Homes for
the direct costs incurred.

9. COMMITMENTS:

At June 30, 2000, Masonic Homes had construction commitments of $14,300,000 at the home for adults in Union City
and $11,758,000 at the home for adults and children in Covina.

ARTHUR ANDERSEN LLP

REPORT OF INDEPENDENT PUBLIC ACCOUNTANTS

To the Board of Trustees of
Masonic Homes Pooled Income Fund:

We have audited the accompanying statement of trust principal of Masonic Homes Pooled Income
Fund (the Fund) as of December 31, 1999, the related statements of activities and cash flows for the
year then ended. These financial statements are the responsibility of the Fund’s management. Our
responsibility is to express an opinion on these financial statements based on our audit.

We conducted our audit in accordance with generally accepted auditing standards. Those
standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about
whether the financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audit includes examining,
on a test basis, evidence supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial statements. An
audit also includes assessing the accounting principles used and significant estimates made by
management, as well as evaluating the overall financial statement presentation. We believe that
our audit provides a reasonable basis for our opinion.

In our opinion, the financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all material respects, the
financial position of the Fund as of December 31, 1999, and the results of its activities and its cash
flows for the year then ended in conformity with generally accepted accounting principles.

Our audit was made for the purpose of forming an opinion on the basic financial statements taken
as a whole. The supplementary fund information included in the accompanying statement of
activities is presented for purposes of additional analysis and is not a required part of the basic
financial statements. This information has been subjected to the auditing procedures applied in
our audit of the basic financial statements and, in our opinion, is fairly stated in all material
respects in relation to the basic financial statements taken as a whole.

Crtbur. Crdbrog, LLP?

San Francisco, California,

116 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

MASONIC HOMES POOLED INCOME FUND

NOTES TO FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
DECEMBER 31, 1999

1. ORGANIZATION:

The Masonic Homes Pooled Income Fund (the Fund) was organized in 1974 as a trust. Donors
contribute irrevocable remainder interests in property while retaining an income interest for life for
one or more beneficiaries. The donors are entitled to a tax deduction equivalent to the present
value of the remainder interest in the property contributed to the Fund.

The property transferred by a donor is held in trust by the Board of Trustees of the Fund and is
commingled with and invested and reinvested with other properties transferred to the Fund. All
dividend and interest income of the Fund is distributed quarterly to the income beneficiaries,
based on their proportionate share of the Fund. Upon the death of each donor’s last income
beneficiary, the remainder interest is distributed to Masonic Homes of California, a charitable
corporation.

2. SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES:

Basis of Presentation

The Fund acts in the capacity of an agent on behalf of the life and remainder beneficiaries of the
Fund. The accompanying financial statements include the activities and trust principal for the
period presented.

Investments

Investments in marketable securities are stated at estimated fair market value based on quoted
market prices. Property received through gifts is recorded at estimated fair value at the date of
donation. Gains or losses resulting from sales or market fluctuations are recognized in the period
such sales or market fluctuations occur.

Income Taxes

The Fund is administered in conformity with all provisions of the Internal Revenue Code, Internal
Revenue Regulations and Rulings, procedures and guidelines covering pooled income funds. The
Fund pays no income tax on income distributed to the income beneficiaries or on long-term capital
gains. Applicable federal and state income taxes are paid by the beneficiaries on their share of
distributed income.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 117

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with generally accepted accounting
principles requires management to make estimates and assumptions. These estimates and
assumptions affect the reported amounts of assets and liabilities and disclosure of contingent
assets and liabilities at the date of the financial statements. Actual results could differ from those
estimates.

3. INVESTMENTS IN MARKETABLE SECURITIES:

Investments at December 31, 1999, are presented at estimated fair value and are composed of the
following:

Fixed income mutual funds $3,454,548
Equity mutual funds 654,328
Total $4,108,876

 

4, RELATED PARTY:

In 1999, administrative costs for the Fund totaled $65,521. Of this amount, $44,731 was paid by
Masonic Homes of California and $20,790 was paid by the Fund.

THUR ANDERSEN

REPORT OF INDEPENDENT PUBLIC ACCOUNTANTS

To the Board of Directors of
California Masonic Foundation:

We have audited the accompanying statement of financial position of California Masonic Foundation (a
California not-for-profit corporation) as of June 30, 2000, and the related statements of activities and changes
in net assets and cash flows for the year then ended. These financial statements are the tesponsibility of the
organization’s management. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on these financial statements based
on our audit.

We conducted our audit in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States. Those
standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the
financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audit includes examining, on a test basis, evidence
supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial statements. An audit also includes assessing the
accounting principles used and significant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall
financial statement presentation. We believe that our audit provides a reasonable basis for our opinion.

In our opinion, the financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all material respects, the financial
position of California Masonic Foundation as of June 30, 2000, and the changes in its net assets and its cash
flows for the year then ended in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United
States.

Our audit was made for the purpose of forming an opinion on the basic financial statements taken as a whole.
Supplementary fund information included in the accompanying statement of financial position, statement of
activities and changes in net assets, and Schedules I and II is presented for purposes of additional analysis
and is not a required part of the basic financial statements. This information has been subjected to the
auditing procedures applied in our audit of the basic financial statements and, in our opinion, is fairly stated
in all material respects in relation to the basic financial statements taken as a whole.

124 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION

NOTES TO FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
JUNE 30, 2000

1. ORGANIZATION AND SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES:

Organization

The California Masonic Foundation (the Foundation) was established in 1968 by the Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted
Masons of the State of California (the Grand Lodge). The Foundation is formed as a nonprofit charitable corporation to
further all phases of education, charity and science.

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States
requires management to make estimates and assumptions. The estimates and assumptions affect the reported amounts
of assets and liabilities and disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities at the date of the financial statements and the
reported amount of revenues and expenses during the reporting period. Actual results could differ from those estimates.
Cash and Cash Equivalents

The Foundation considers all highly liquid investments with an initial maturity of three months or less when purchased to
be cash equivalents.

Investments

Investments in marketable securities are stated at fair value and, to the extent that management intends to hold such
investments for more than one year, are classified as noncurrent assets. Gains or losses that result from market
fluctuations are recognized in the period in which such fluctuations occur.

Equipment

Equipment, which consists of data processing equipment and furniture and fixtures, is carried at cost and is depreciated
on the straight-line method over three and five years, respectively.

Net Asset Categories

The Foundation reports information regarding its net assets and activities according to three classes of net assets:
unrestricted, temporarily restricted and permanently restricted.

* Unrestricted net assets are available for support of the Foundation’s programs and supporting services.

* Temporarily restricted net assets represent contributions whose use by the Foundation is limited by donor-
imposed restrictions. The restrictions can be fulfilled by incurring expenses satisfying the restricted purposes.

* Permanently restricted net assets are restricted by the donor for investment in perpetuity. These permanently
restricted net assets consist of original corpus and an adjustment for inflation in order to maintain the purchasing
power of the scholarship funds.

The Foundation categorizes its financial position by fund based upon donor restrictions as follows:
General Fund—Resources are available for support of Foundation programs and supporting services.

Masonic Student Assistance Fund—This fund was established by the Grand Lodge to provide financial
support to ongoing programs aimed at assisting students in furthering their academic careers. All amounts in the
fund are donor-restricted specifically for use in these programs.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 125

Scholarship Funds

Amaranth Fund—Resources are restricted by a trust agreement with the Amaranth Homes of California.
The agreement specifies that principal is to be invested and the income is to be used to provide
educational grants to women under age 21. Net assets of $507,774 are restricted.

Wade-Amaranth Fund—This fund was established by a bequest from W.O. Wade. Income from the
fund is to be expended for scholarships and other like purposes for female students seeking degrees
from educational institutions in California. Net assets of $1,128,280 are restricted.

F.M. Robinson Memorial Fund—This fund was established by contributions from Mrs. F.M. Robinson.
The principal of the fund cannot be expended. Income from the fund is to be expended for scholarships.
Net assets of $73,994 are restricted.

Harry Walker Lister Scholarship Fund—This fund was established from a contribution of a variable
appreciable life insurance policy. The policy was redeemed during 1988. The principal of the fund cannot.
be expended. The income of the fund can only be used to fund scholarships. Net assets of $357,854 are
restricted.

Starr King Lodge No. 344 Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by the Starr King Lodge from
the Robert Swartz Charitable Trust. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the
fund is to be expended for scholarships for the benefit of Starr King members, their children,
grandchildren, nieces and nephews. Net assets of $79,576 are restricted.

MacKechnie Fund—This fund was established by a bequest from Jack |. MacKechnie. The principal of
the fund cannot be expended and is held by the Grand Lodge. The income of the fund is transferred to
the Foundation to be expended for scholarships for needy high school graduates of proven outstanding
athletic achievement. Net assets of $11,870 are restricted.

Coalinga Masonic Lodge Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by the Coalinga Lodge. The
principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be expended for scholarships
for Coalinga area students. Net assets of $101,515 are restricted.

Stanley and Gloria Cazneaux Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by contributions from
Stanley and Gloria Cazneaux. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund
is to be expended for scholarships for students who are interested in the arts. Net assets of $58,420 are
restricted.

George and Ethel McElwain Memorial Fund—This fund was established by contributions from
Lester S. McElwain. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be
expended for scholarships for worthy, needy or middle-class young men and women. Net assets of
$702,037 are restricted.

Brotherhood Lodge Fund—This fund was established by contributions from the Brotherhood Lodge.
The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be expended for
scholarships for the benefit of Brotherhood members, their children, grandchildren, nieces and nephews,
or, if at a particular time there are no qualified applicants, to residents of San Francisco or San Mateo
counties. Net assets of $51,349 are restricted.

Chowchilla Masonic Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by contributions from the
Chowchilla Lodge. The principal cannot be expended, and income of the fund is to be expended for
scholarships for deserving graduating seniors at Chowchilla Union High School and/or Le Grand High
School. Net assets of $48,291 are restricted.

Soquel-Pajaro Masonic Lodge for T. Fenton Ham Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by
contributions from the Soquel-Pajaro Lodge. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the
income of the fund is to be expended for scholarships for engineering students who are accepted into an
Engineering Council for Professional Development (ECPD) accredited engineering program. Net assets
of $276,614 are restricted.

Warren J. Blomseth Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by contributions from Warren J.
Blomseth. The principal of the fund cannot be expended. Income from the fund is to be expended for
scholarships. Net assets of $180,809 are restricted.


126 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Earold H. Barton and Merna |. Barton Memorial Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by a
bequest from Earold H. and Merna |. Barton. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the
income is to be expended for scholarships for worthy candidates selected by the Foundation, with
preference to children or grandchildren of Master Masons preparing for a career in education who
participate in extracurricular activities and attend summer school. Net assets of $357,751 are restricted.

Arthur Wilmott Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by contributions from Arthur Wilmott. The
principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be expended for scholarships
for the benefit of individuals chosen by the Greenleaf Gardens Masonic Lodge. Net assets of $760,049
are restricted.

Cecil and Bonita Baxter Scholarship Fund—This fund was established by contributions from Cecil and
Bonita Baxter. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be
expended for scholarships for the benefit of individuals chosen by the Soquel Pajaro Masonic Lodge. Net
assets of $59,298 are restricted.

Sincerity Fund—This fund was established by contributions from Donald Nelson. The principal of the
fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be expended for scholarships for the benefit
of individuals chosen by the Foundation Scholarship Committee. Net assets of $329,266 are restricted.

Karmann Fund—This fund was established by contributions from the Carl and Elsie Karmann Charitable
Remainder Unitrust. The principal of the fund cannot be expended, and the income of the fund is to be

expended for scholarships for the benefit of individuals chosen by the Foundation Scholarship
Committee. Net assets of $131,659 are restricted.

3, FEDERAL EXCISE TAX:

The Foundation is a not-for-profit organization that is exempt from income tax under Section 501(c)(3) of the Internal
Revenue Code (the Code). The Foundation is classified as a private foundation and is subject to an excise tax on net
investment income, including realized gains, as defined by the Code. Accordingly, federal excise tax has been
recognized in the amount of $9,771 for the year ended June 30, 2000.

4. COMMITMENTS:

Scholarships are renewable annually through the completion of four years of education if the applicant meets renewal
criteria and is approved for renewal by the committee. Total potential renewals for previously awarded scholarships for
the 1999/2000 school year are $484,500. Total potential renewals for school years beyond 1999/2000 are $1,444,500.

5. PENSION PLAN:

The Foundation, Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc., the California Masonic Memorial Temple, the Grand Lodge and the
Masonic Homes of California sponsor a defined benefit pension plan (the Plan) that covers all employees. Benefits are to
be paid to eligible employees at retirement based primarily upon years of service and compensation levels near
retirement. Contributions to the Plan reflect benefits attributed to employees’ services to date, as well as services
expected to be provided in the future. Plan assets consist primarily of equity and fixed income mutual funds.

components:

To the Board of Trustees of
California Masonic Memorial Temple:

We have audited the accompanying statements of financial position of California Masonic Memorial Temple
(a California not-for-profit corporation) as of June 30, 2000 and 1999, and the related statements of activity
and changes in net assets and cash flows for the years then ended. These financial statements are the
responsibility of the organization’s management. Our responsibility is to express an opinion on these
financial statements based on our audits.

We conducted our audits in accordance with auditing standards generally accepted in the United States.
Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the
financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audit includes examining, on a test basis, evidence
supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial statements. An audit also includes assessing the
accounting principles used and significant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall
financial statement presentation. We believe that our audits provide a reasonable basis for our opinion.

In our opinion, the financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all material respects, the financial
position of California Masonic Memorial Temple as of June 30, 2000 and 1999, and the changes in its net
assets and its cash flows for the years then ended in conformity with accounting principles generally

accepted in the United States.

CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE

NOTES TO FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
JUNE 30, 2000 AND 1999

1. ORGANIZATION AND SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES:

Organization

California Masonic Memorial Temple (the Temple) is a nonprofit organization. According to the Articles of Incorporation,
income from the Temple, less operating expenses and reserves, is to be transferred to the Endowment Fund of Masonic

Homes of California (Masonic Homes) and/or the California Masonic Foundation (the Foundation). The amount and
proportions of such distributions are at the sole discretion of the Board of Trustees.

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States
requires management to make estimates and assumptions. These estimates and assumptions affect the reported
amounts of assets and liabilities and disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities at the date of the financial statements

and the reported amount of revenues and expenses during the reporting period. Actual results could differ from those
estimates.

Cash and Cash Equivalents

The Temple considers all highly liquid investments with original maturities of three months or less when purchased to be
cash equivalents.

Investments

Investments in marketable securities are stated at estimated fair value based on quoted market prices. Investments
received through gifts are recorded at estimated fair market value at the date of donation.

Property and Equipment

Property and equipment are carried at cost and are depreciated on the straight-line method over the following estimated
lives:

Buildings and improvements 5-50 years
Equipment 5-10 years
Furnishings 3-20 years

Temporarily Restricted Net Assets

Temporarily restricted net assets represent contributions whose use by the Temple is limited by either donor-imposed
stipulations or from circumstances surrounding the receipt of the contribution that make clear the donor's implicit
restriction on use. These restrictions may either expire by the passage of time or be fulfilled by actions of the Temple
pursuant to those stipulations.

Collection of Books and Nonvaluable Contributions

The Temple library contains a book collection of approximately 10,000 volumes. The collection includes approximately
8,500 works on Masonic and related philosophic topics and nearly 1,500 non-Masonic volumes, including historical,
religious, scientific, political and biographical works. Fewer than 50 works in the collection could be considered rare. The
Temple museum contains donated items such as works of art and historical treasures contributed by members of the
Free and Accepted Masons of California. The Temple museum collection contains donated items from the membership
of The Masonic Fraternity in California and elsewhere. Artifacts in the collection include works of art, Masonic regalia and
numerous historical pieces. The Temple does not recognize books and nonvaluable contributions in its financial
statements. The Temple does not recognize these assets as contributions, as they are protected and preserved, held for
public exhibition and subject to an organizational policy that requires proceeds from the sale of collection items to be


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 137

used to acquire other items for collections. Accessions and deaccessions of books and nonvaluable contributions during
2000 and 1999 were not significant.

Revenue Recognition
Contributions are recognized when received or unconditionally pledged.
Functional Expenses Allocations

Expenses, such as depreciation, supplies, travel, personnel and occupancy costs, are allocated among program services
and general and administrative services classifications on the basis of estimates made by the Temple's management.

Tax-Exempt Status
The Temple is a nonprofit corporation as described in Section 501(c)(3) of the Internal Revenue Code and has been

granted tax-exempt status by the Internal Revenue Service and the California Franchise Tax Board. As discussed in
Note 5, the Temple is subject to certain federal excise taxes.

2. INVESTMENTS:

Investments comprised the following at June 30, 2000 and 1999:

3. PENSION PLAN:

The Temple, Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. (Nob Hill), the Foundation, the Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted
Masons of the State of California and subsidiary (the Grand Lodge), and Masonic Homes sponsor a defined benefit
pension plan (the Plan) that covers all employees. Benefits are to be paid to eligible employees at retirement based
primarily upon years of service and compensation levels near retirement. Contributions to the Plan reflect benefits
attributed to employees’ services to date, as well as services expected to be provided in the future. Plan assets consist
primarily of equity and fixed income mutual funds.

Pension expense and other activity of the Plan for the years ended June 30, 2000 and 1999, consisted of the following

components:

Pension activity has been allocated to the five participating employers. The prepaid pension expense allocated to the

was $1,574 and $1,989 in 2000 and 1999, respectively.

4, OPERATING LEASES:

Nob Hill has an operating lease with the Temple, expiring June 30, 2003, under which Nob Hill manages the Temple
auditorium, exhibit hall, parking garage and various rental properties. Under the terms of the lease, Nob Hill is required to
pay rent equal to 90 percent of the gross rental revenues in excess of $860,000 per year, adjusted annually for inflation,
until Nob Hill has paid $240,000, after which payment shall be one-third of the remaining gross rental revenues.
Beginning in 1998, the aggregate rent calculated under this formula is subject to a limit that increases with inflation. The
aggregate rent for the year ended June 30, 2000, is limited to $430,000 and for fiscal years thereafter through June 30,
2008, is limited to $445,000.

For the year ended June 30, 2000, adjusted gross rental revenues, as defined in the lease agreement, were $2,839,315,
which generated $430,000 in lease revenue to the Temple. For the year ended June 30, 1999, adjusted gross rental
revenues, as defined in the lease agreement, were $1,923,822, which generated $415,000 in lease revenue to the
Temple.

5. FEDERAL EXCISE TAXES:

In accordance with applicable tax provisions, the Temple is classified as a private foundation and is subject to an excise
tax on net investment income, including realized gains. Accordingly, federal excise taxes have been recognized in the
amount of $3,357 during 2000 and $10,476 during 1999.

6, RELATED-PARTY TRANSACTIONS:

The Temple leases the Temple auditorium, exhibit hall, parking garage and various rental properties to Nob Hill (see
Note 4).

The Grand Lodge provides general and administrative support to the Temple, for which it is reimbursed through an.
allocation of certain expenses. The allocations for the years ended June 30, 2000 and 1999, were $16,036 and $15,419,

respectively.

NOTES TO FINANCIAL STATEMENTS.
JUNE 30, 2000 AND 1999

4. ORGANIZATION AND SUMMARY OF ACCOUNTING POLICIES:

Organization

Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. (the Company), a for-profit corporation, is a wholly owned subsidiary of the Grand
Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of the state of California (the Grand Lodge). The Company operates the California

Masonic Memorial Temple (the Temple) auditorium, parking garage, exhibit hall and various rental properties under the
terms of an operating lease with the Temple, an affiliated entity.

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States
requires management to make estimates and assumptions. These estimates and assumptions affect the reported
amounts of assets and liabilities and disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities at the date of the financial statements

and the reported amounts of revenue and expenses during the reporting period. Actual results could differ from those
estimates.

Cash and Cash Equivalents

The Company considers all highly liquid investments with original maturities of three months or less when purchased to
be cash equivalents.

Investments
Available-for-sale securities are recorded at fair value. Unrealized gains and losses on available-for-sale securities, net of
tax, are reported as a separate component of stockholder’s equity until realized. Dividend and interest income are

recognized when earned. Realized gains and losses for securities are included in earnings and are derived using the
specific identification method for determining the cost of securities sold.

Equipment

Equipment is carried at cost and is depreciated on the straight-line method over five to seven years.

Retained Earnings
The Company’s Board of Directors has designated $80,428 and $45,000 of retained earnings for specially designated

projects as of June 30, 2000 and 1999, respectively. These designations are discretionary and may be removed by the
Board at any time.

Revenue Recognition
Rental revenue is recognized at the time the physical facilities are used.

Income Taxes

Income taxes are accounted for using the asset and liability method under Statement of Financial Accounting Standards
(SFAS) No. 109, “Accounting for Income Taxes.”


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 147

4, PENSION PLAN:

The Company, the Temple, the Grand Lodge, the California Masonic Foundation and the Masonic Homes of California
sponsor a defined benefit pension plan (the Plan) that covers all employees. Benefits are to be paid to eligible employees
at retirement based primarily upon years of service and compensation levels near retirement. Contributions to the Plan
reflect benefits attributed to employees’ services to date, as well as services expected to be provided in the future. Plan
assets consist primarily of equity and fixed income mutual funds.

5. OPERATING LEASES:

The Company has an operating lease with the Temple, expiring June 30, 2003, under which the Company manages the
Temple auditorium, exhibit hall, parking garage and various rental properties. Under the terms of the lease, the Company
is required to pay rent equal to 90 percent of the gross rental revenues in excess of $860,000 per year, adjusted annually
for inflation, until the Company has paid $240,000, after which payment shall be one-third of the remaining gross rental
revenues. Beginning in 1998, the aggregate rent calculated under this formula is subject to a limit that increases with
inflation. The aggregate rent for the year ended June 30, 2000, is limited to $430,000, and for fiscal years thereafter
through June 30, 2008, is limited to $445,000.

For the year ended June 30, 2000, adjusted gross rental revenues, as defined in the lease agreement, were $2,839,315,
which generated $430,000 in lease payments. For the year ended June 30, 1999, adjusted gross rental revenues, as
defined in the lease agreement, were $1,923,822, which generated $415,000 in lease payments.

The Company has a management agreement with AMPCO Auto Parks, Inc. (AMPCO) for the operation of the parking
garage. Under the terms of agreement, the Company receives all receipts collected by AMPCO. The Company pays
AMPCO a management fee of $1,500 per month plus 1.5 percent of the net revenue, as defined, of the garage. Net
receipts under the agreement were $1,103,754 and $938,533 for 2000 and 1999, respectively. The agreement can be
terminated by either party with 30 days notice.

6. RELATED-PARTY TRANSACTIONS:

The Grand Lodge provides general and administrative support to the Company for which it is reimbursed through an
allocation of certain expenses. The allocations for 2000 and 1999 were $47,350 and $46,154, respectively.

The Company provides office space to the Grand Lodge at $26,500 per year.

156 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

CALIFORNIA MASONIC RETIREMENT PLAN

NOTES TO FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
DECEMBER 31, 1999 AND 1998

1. DESCRIPTION OF THE PLAN:

General

The California Masonic Retirement Plan (the Plan) is a defined benefit plan that provides retirement benefits for
substantially all employees of Masonic Homes of California, Masonic Grand Lodge and Component Organizations
(collectively, the Masonic Homes) who have reached the age of 21 and completed one year of service. It is subject to the
provisions of the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 (ERISA). Participants should refer to the Plan's
documents for a more complete description of the Plan.

Employees with five or more years of service are entitled to annual pension benefits beginning at normal retirement age
(65). The benefit formula varies based on an employee's final year of service. For current employees, the annual
retirement benefit at normal retirement shall be equal to 2 percent of the participant's average annual compensation
earned during the five calendar years prior to January 1, 1999, times years of service through 1998 plus the sum of

2 percent of the participant’s compensation earned in each full and partial year of service after January 1, 1999.

The Plan permits early retirement at ages 55 to 64 for participants with a minimum of 10 years of service. Married
employees may elect to receive their pension benefits in the form of a joint and survivor annuity. If employees terminate
before rendering five years of service, they forfeit the right to receive the portion of their accumulated plan benefits
attributable to the Masonic Homes’ contributions.

If an active employee dies at age 55 or older, a death benefit equal to the value of the employee's accumulated required
contributions is paid to the employee's beneficiary. A death benefit will also be payable to an employee who dies after
accumulating five years of service. Active employees who become disabled and totally incapacitated may receive an
annual disability benefit that is equal to the normal retirement benefits they have accumulated as of the time they become
disabled, reduced for early withdrawal. Disability benefits are paid until normal retirement age, at which time disabled
participants begin receiving normal retirement benefits.

The assets of the Plan are maintained in trusts established pursuant to trust agreements among the Masonic Homes,
Wells Fargo Bank, N.A. and SEI trust.

Funding Policy

Prior to January 1, 1982, contributions to the Plan had been made by participating employers and employees based
upon a percentage of covered payroll. Effective January 1, 1982, the requirement for employee contributions was
terminated. The Masonic Homes contributes actuarially determined amounts sufficient to amortize the unfunded present
value of accumulated plan benefits over the working lifetime of the active participants. The Plan complies with the
minimum funding requirements of ERISA.

Plan Termination

Although the Masonic Homes has no intention to terminate the Plan, it has the ability to do so, Should the Plan terminate
at some future time, its net assets generally will not be available on a pro rata basis to provide participants’ benefits.
Whether a particular participant's accumulated plan benefits will be paid depends on both the priority of those benefits
and the level of benefits guaranteed by the Pension Benefit Guaranty Corporation (PBGC) at that time. Some benefits
may be fully or partially provided for by the then-existing assets and the PBGC guaranty, while other benefits may not be
provided for at all.

Information about the plan agreement, the vesting and benefit provisions, and the PBGC’s benefit guaranty is available
from the Masonic Homes.

Investment Management and Administrative Expenses

Investment management and administrative expenses are principally paid by the Plan.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 157

2, SUMMARY OF SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING POLICIES:
Basis of Accounting

The financial statements of the Plan are prepared based upon accounting principles generally accepted in the United
States.

Use of Estimates

The preparation of financial statements in conformity with accounting principles generally accepted in the United States
requires management to make estimates and assumptions that affect the reported amounts of assets, liabilities and
changes therein, and disclosure of contingent assets and liabilities and the actuarial present value of accumulated plan
benefits at the date of the financial statements. Actual results could differ from those estimates.

Investments

Investments in marketable securities are stated at fair value based on quoted market prices or net asset values as
reported by the asset custodians.

Income Recognition

Securities transactions are recorded as of the trade date. Interest income is recorded on an accrual basis. Dividends are
recorded when received.

Actuarial Present Value of Accumulated Plan Benefits

Accumulated plan benefits are those estimated future periodic payments, including lump-sum distributions, that are
attributable under the Plan's provisions to services rendered by the employees to the valuation date. Accumulated plan
benefits include benefits expected to be paid to (a) retired or terminated employees or their beneficiaries and (b) present
the employees or their beneficiaries. Benefits payable under all circumstances (retirement, death, disability and
termination of employment) are included to the extent that they are deemed attributable to employee service rendered to
the valuation date.

Benefit Payments to Participants

Benefit payments to participants are recorded upon distribution.

4. ACTUARIAL INFORMATION:

The actuarial present value of accumulated plan benefits is determined by the Plan’s consulting actuary and is the
amount that results from applying actuarial assumptions to adjust the accumulated plan benefits earned by the
participants to reflect the time value of money (through discounts for interest) and the probability of payment (by means
of documents such as for death, disability, withdrawal or retirement) between the valuation date and the expected date of
payment.
