APPENDIX E

 




GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 165

REPORT OF THE GRAND SECRETARY

This is the tenth time that I have been privileged to report to you as
Grand Secretary of this Grand Lodge. The Grand Lodge Year just now
concluding has been one of great challenge for your Grand Lodge Office. At the
beginning of the year we were asked to assume record keeping responsibility for
donations to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund. In previous years only the
total funds sent to us by the lodges were entered into our accounting system, and
there were no records of individual donations. The old system simply did not
allow us to account for individual donations in a manner now required by law
and proper accounting procedures. In order to do so the Membership Database
had to be expanded, and new computer programs written to accomplish this
purpose.

A great deal of planning time and effort went into developing a system
which is both accurate and easily accessible. The task would have been
impossible without the help of the secretaries of our lodges who worked with us
to perfect the system. It is difficult to change old ways of doing things, and the
changeover to a new system entailed a lot of work on the part of our lodge
secretaries. But together we did it. Lodges now receive a monthly printout of
contributions made by members of their lodges, and your Grand Lodge is able to
properly acknowledge every contribution, and to provide accurate records for
income tax purposes.

The effort was worth it. For the first time in almost twenty years our
donations to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund were greater than the year
before. In addition, our members gave gifts to the Masonic Homes in larger
amounts than ever before. The average amount of each gift increased, and the
number of gifts which were at least $100 each increased by nearly 53% over a
year ago. By every measure, the new program was a success. Thanks must go
to the Chairman of the Masonic Homes Endowment Committee, Bro. Richard
Pumerantz, for his dogged determination and the hours and hours that he put
into the project. Thanks also must go to the Grand Master and the Executive
Committee who gave wise counsel and advice, as well as support. And above
all, thanks must go to the President of the Board of Trustees of the Masonic
Homes of California, Most Worshipful Stanley M. Cazneaux, and to all the
trustees, for their faith that we could accomplish it. “Thank you” is much too
little to say, but it is heartfelt, and especially by our future residents at the Home
who will be the real beneficiaries of this renewed enthusiasm on the part of our
members to support their Masonic Home!

This year we held our usual six workshops for lodge secretaries, with
an emphasis this year on lodge finances, and the part that secretaries play in
good lodge management. A new Secretary’s Manual was produced, and tested
with our secretaries through the workshops. The final copy was handed out just
yesterday at our Annual Secretaries’ Workshop here at Grand Lodge, and
reflected the input from hundreds of our faithful secretaries last spring.


166 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In addition to supervision of the Grand Lodge Office the Grand
Secretary serves as secretary to our several Masonic boards. One of these is the
California Masonic Memorial Temple, the board of which governs the Henry
Wilson Coil Masonic Library and Museum. It also governs the Institute for
Masonic Studies, which provides the Grand Lodge Internet Site. The Grand
Master has already told you of our new image on the website, an image which
you can see downstairs on display at this Annual Communication. The brother
responsible for the direct supervision of this site under my direction as Grand
Secretary is Worshipful Bro. Norm Schultz, a Past Master of Sunset Lodge
No. 369. Along with Worshipful Bro. Dave Stites, a Past Master of Lakeside
Lodge No. 760, who is our technical director, the site made significant advances
in serving the Craft in California, and indeed, around the world. Before you
leave this Annual Communication, take a look at our new lodge locater program
which is on display downstairs in the Exhibit Hall. It enables a Mason who is
traveling to easily locate a lodge which he can visit, with readily available
contact information. Masonic fellowship should be enhanced as it is now much
easier to find a lodge to visit when traveling.

In addition, the Institute for Masonic Studies brought to a successful
conclusion a four-year project of publishing the Book of Gold. The Book of
Gold is a translation from the French of the rituals belonging to the first lodge in
Hawaii, which was one of our chartered lodges until 1989 when the Grand
Lodge of Hawaii was formed. The rituals were brought to California in 1950 for
our Centennial Celebration, and then disappeared. The exciting story of these
rituals, and their rediscovery, is only a part of the fascinating material in this
book, the first scholarly work published under the auspices of the Institute for
Masonic Studies. The books are on the press right now in Redding, and will be
shipped shortly. This is a token of that publication, and the first copy off the
press has been set aside for you as the Grand Master under whose leadership it
was finally published.

The Grand Master has already shared with you some of our
membership statistics for this year. I will only summarize them, because the
complete report will appear in the Proceedings of this Annual Communication.
We ended our reporting year on June 30 with 90,914 Master Masons. Our
lodges remitted the dues of 2,433 Master Masons, and the assessable number of
Master Masons was 88,481. We Raised 1,006 new Master Masons during the
year, and Initiated 1,189 Entered Apprentice Masons. We have 388 lodges on
the rolls of our Grand Lodge, and five research lodges. The newest lodge in our
jurisdiction is Masonic Origins Research Lodge which received its dispensation
from our Grand Master in November, 1999.

Finally, it is my responsibility as Grand Secretary to report to you on
the Grand Lodges which our Grand Master believes should be accorded full
recognition by our Grand Lodge. The motion will include the confirmation of
recognition of two Grand Lodges which had experienced splits in the recent
past, and confirms our recognition of the correct representatives of those Grand


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 167

Lodges. The recommendations are based on the report of the Commission on
Recognition of the Conference of Grand Masters of Masons in North America,
which endorses these recommendations. I therefore move the following:

That the Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of
California grant full recognition to:

The Grand Orient of Brazil
The Grand Lodge of Croatia
The Grand Lodge of Estonia
The Grand Lodge of Mali
The National Grand Lodge of Guinea
The Grand Lodge of Sinaloa, Mexico
The Grand Lodge of Newfoundland & Labrador

and confirm the recognition previously granted to the National
Grand Lodge of Romania and the Regular/Legal Grand Lodge
of Portugal as the sole legitimate Masonic power in those two
countries.

Respectfully submitted,
John L. Cooper III, Grand Secretary

Most Worshipful, that concludes the Grand Secretary’s Report.

Amendments to By-Laws

Uniform Code of By-Laws (C.M.C. Section 19040-19050):

Alisal Sequoia San Ramon No. 321 changed name to Pleasanton No.
321.

Stated Meetings (C.M.C. Section 19040, Article III):

Changing Time: Henry Clay No. 95; Stanislaus No. 206; and Carmel
No. 680.

Changing Date: La Parfaite Union No. 17.

Table of Fees (C.M.C. Section 19040, Article VIII):

Increasing: Woodbridge No. 131 to $111; Lodi No. 256 to $161; Santa
Monica-Palisades No. 307 to $200; Montebello-Whittier No. 323 to
$138; Morgan Hill No. 463 to $168; A.M. Daylight No. 625 to $150;
Carmel No. 680 to $150; and Anacapa No. 710 to $50.

Dues (C.M.C. Section 19040, Article IX):

Increasing: San Joaquin No. 19 to $70; Morning Star No. 68 to $48;
Woodbridge No. 131 to $40; Abell No. 146 to $48; Hope No. 234 to
$50; Lodi No. 256 to $65; Santa Monica-Palisades No. 307 to $60;
Tehachapi No. 313 to $75; Artesia No. 377 to $50; Granada Hills No.
378 to $50; Perris No. 411 to $60; Elysian No. 418 to $50; Upland-Mt.
Baldy No. 419 to $60; San Dimas No. 428 to $60; Libertas-Security
No. 466 to $80; Southern California No. 529 to $60; A.M. Daylight
No. 625 to $60; Chula Vista No. 626 to $60; Greenleaf Gardens No.
670 to $45; and Carmel No. 680 to $75.

Decreasing: Georgetown No. 25 to $30 and Montebello-Whittier No.
323 to $30.

Charity Committee (C.M.C. Section 19040, Article X):
Increasing: Montebello-Whitter No. 323 to $250.

Life Membership (C.M.C. Section 27020):

Alternate: Nevada No. 13; Athens-Silveyville No. 201; Lompoc Santa
Ynez No. 262; Hesperian No. 264; and Camarillo No. 824.


174 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Consolidation of Lodges

All requirements of the law with respect to consolidation of Lodges
having been complied with, authorization is requested to properly endorse the
charters of these Lodges as follows:

San Bernardino Lodge No. 178 and Damascus Lodge No. 648,
effective November 1, 1999, to be known as San Bernardino Lodge No. 178.

Whittier Lodge No. 323 and Montebello Lodge No. 451, effective
December 1, 1999, to be known as Montebello- Whittier Lodge No. 323.

Hiram Lodge No. 43 and Lake Tahoe Lodge No. 707, effective January
1, 2000, to be known as Hiram-Lake Tahoe Lodge No. 43.

Redwood City Lodge No. 168 and San Carlos Lodge No. 690, effective
January 1, 2000, to be known as Peninsula Lodge No. 168.

Ionic Lodge No. 520 and Composite Lodge No. 595, effective January
1, 2000, to be known as Ionic Composite Lodge No. 520.

Live Oak Lodge No. 61 and San Leandro-Brooklyn Lodge No. 225,
effective January 1, 2000, to be known as Live Oak Lodge No. 61.

Woodbridge Lodge No. 131 and Rio Vista Lodge No. 208, effective
March 1, 2000, to be known as Woodbridge Lodge No. 131.

Alta Mt. Sierra Lodge No. 408 and San Marino Lodge No. 588,
effective April 1, 2000, to be known as San Marino Lodge No. 408.

Silver Gate Lodge No. 296 and Three Stars Lodge No. 839, effective
September 1, 2000, to be known as Silver Gate Three Stars Lodge No. 296.

Dunsmuir-McCloud Lodge No. 297 and Sisson-White Pine Lodge No.
310, effective September 1, 2000, to be known as Siskiyou Lodge No. 297.

Surprise Valley Lodge No. 235 and Alturas Canby Cross Lodge No.
248, effective September 22, 2000, to be known as Modoc Lodge No. 235.

APPENDIX F

 




GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 187

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
Most Worshipful and my brethren:

Since our last Annual Communication membership in our jurisdiction
has declined to a level under 90,000. A continuing shortage in officers and an
attendant lack of interest has resulted in the consolidation of nine more lodges.
This notwithstanding, we are both pleased and grateful for the esprit de corps of
those tireless brethren who manage to keep their lodges intact.

Eighteen seminars held throughout the jurisdiction continue to stir the
interest of our brethren and we thank them for their attendance.

As the year draws to a close, we say “thank you” to our Grand Master
and his lady, Gini, for his leadership and for their friendship.

To the rest of the Grand Lodge team and their ladies, please accept our
thanks both for your team effort and for the dignity with which you discharged
your duties.

To our Grand Secretary and his staff for their timely and ever-willing
assistance, much thanks.

To the Inspectoral Corps for all the time and service that you give to
our Fraternity, many, many thanks.

To all of our coaches for their time, patience and perseverance, you
have our undying gratitude.

To my colleagues on the Ritual Committee, for your friendship, support
and assistance, thank you.

And to you, my brethren, for all of your support and words of
encouragement, please accept our thanks.

Respectfully submitted,
Ulysses G. Patropulos, Grand Lecturer


188 PROCEEDINGS OF THE


APPENDIX G

 




GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 191

PUBLIC OPENING OF GRAND LODGE
Invocation — Sunday, October 8, 2000

Great Architect of the Universe, we assemble here this afternoon for
the Public Opening of Grand Lodge to recognize the leaders of our Concordant
Bodies and the adult and youth leaders of our youth orders.

As Masons, we are taught that we owe a duty to GOD, to our country,
to our neighbors or communities and to ourselves.

Our Youth Leaders in their presentations will remind us of the duty we
Masons owe to our country and our communities.

Our forefathers were Yesterday’s Legacy, We are Today’s Vision and
our Youth are Tomorrow’s Reality.

We are reminded that our individual conduct away from the formal
setting of our Lodge Rooms is the brightest light that shines on our Fraternity. If
we Masons take our duties seriously, our Fraternity will become a brilliant light
in our Communities and our Country.

We ask a special blessing for those participating in this Public Opening
this afternoon.

All of this we humbly request in your name.

AMEN


192 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

PUBLIC OPENING OF GRAND LODGE
Inspirational Message — Sunday, October 8, 2000

HOPE, OPTIMISM and DETERMINATION

At the Cornerstone Laying Ceremony, for the Huntington Seacliff
Elementary School, we were told a story that every day, a child walked to the
beach with a pail, picking up starfish that had been washed in by the tide, and
throwing them back into the sea. One day, an old man stopped this child and
asked, “Why do you throw the starfish back? It doesn’t matter. They will only
wash up on the shore again tomorrow.” The child picked a starfish out of his
pail, threw it as far as he could into the sea and replied... “It mattered to that
one.” Just a child doing what he could for those starfish washed up on the shore
by the tide.

That child was not influenced by the old man’s negative attitude of
“what difference does it make”. Likewise our Fraternity should not be
influenced by that same attitude of “what difference does it make.”

Oliver Wendell Holmes said, “greatness is not where we stand, but in
what direction we are moving. We must sail sometimes with the wind and
sometimes against — but sail we must, and not drift, nor lie at anchor.”

The challenge to each of us and our Fraternity is to continue to move
forward!

In our journey through life, we need to examine where we are going.
Which direction are you heading? The direction you are heading is more
important than how fast you are going. If you do not have a direction that you
are heading, you are lost, for how do you know where you are going?

As the famous philosopher Yogi Berra said, “If you don’t know where
you are going — how do you know when you get there?”

Which direction are you headed? For yourself, your Family, your
Community, your Country and last but not least, your Masonic Fraternity?

Do you have any guidelines for your travels? The three principal
tenants of Masonry — Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth will be adequate
resources for your journey through life. It takes courage to travel toward your
destination. Courage does not come easy. Sometimes, it is a barely audible
voice at the end of the day saying, “I will try harder again tomorrow”.

As you travel on your journey, have you conducted a searching
inventory of yourself, of your life, of your beliefs? (Who are you? How did you
get this way? Whom did you model yourself after? What’s important to you:
money, fame, a good job well-done?

What areas of your life, career, personal life, values are ripe for
change?


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 193

As you review your possibilities - What new directions and new ways
of living are open to you? Do you want excellence? The only way to obtain
excellence is to shoot for perfection and practice, practice and practice. Get off
the treadmill of mediocrity!

There is also opportunity for rebirth and renewal as you travel along
your journey. What feels right for you on your journey? What price are you
paying with your family, associates, your community and your fraternity?

Do you waste time looking for the “needle” in the haystack? If finding
the “needle” in our “life’s haystack” is so difficult, what’s the sensible response?
Stop trying to find the impossible “needle” hidden deep in the haystack. It is the
haystack (life itself) that we ought to be looking for all along.

What gifts; time, talents and financial resources, do you want to bestow
on others?

The most precious gift we can give to our family, our friends and our
Masonic Fraternity is TIME. It costs nothing and maybe that is one of the
reasons we do not bestow this precious gift of TIME to others.

Remember that yesterday is past, today is now upon us, and tomorrow
will be here in a very short time.

What have you done to best utilize this opportunity of time? At the end
of your journey, how do you want to be remembered?

Have you taken time to think about what is important in your life?
How you will be remembered? What are your treasures? How will those
treasures be divided? What about your intangible treasures, for there your heart
will be. Should we treasure the finer things in life; Brotherly Love, Relief and
Truth, for that’s where our remembrance will be!

Should we not look into the eyes of others to see what God sees— a
Brother or Sister to be treasured and loved with our own life. What does God
see in each of us that He treasures so much? Can I see that if I look in a mirror
and observe myself? Do we have to trust that whatever God sees in each of us is
really there? Are we in partnership with Him?

As we travel this passage of life, can we see in others what God
treasures in them? With His help, we can prepare for a life where death is not
the end, but only a new beginning.

Let each of us look forward with HOPE, OPTIMISM and
DETERMINATION, as we travel on our journey through life.

APPENDIX H




GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 199

“PORTRAIT OF A MASON”
Ronald E. Cooper, Grand Orator
October 9, 2000

Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, Visiting Dignitaries
of Other Jurisdictions, and my Brethren of the Grand Lodge of California:

It is my sad burden to tell all of you that Masonry is dead. Haven’t you
heard the news? I certainly have! I’ve heard it in Lodge rooms from San Diego to
Yreka. I’ve read it in nearly every Masonic publication delivered to my mailbox
during this year that I have served as Grand Orator.

That’s what they are saying. Masonry is dead. Or at least, doomed to
extinction within the next 20 years, 10 years, 5 years, or perhaps, if we’re not
careful, it’s going to happen right here today at this Annual Communication.
Hundreds of years of Masonic tradition and heritage have already perished or will
succumb any day now.

In fact, I recently received a Lodge newsletter where Masonry’s demise
was talked about in the Master’s column; in the Junior Warden’s column; and of
course never to be outdone, the Lodge Secretary had to devote half of his column to
predicting the death of our fraternity. Well, that’s to be understood, of course.
After all, the Lodge Secretary is always a Past Master -- and they usually have a
hard time keeping their opinions to themselves!

However, when I look around this Grand Lodge today, I don’t see the faces
of mourners weeping. I see the faces of good men working together to become
better men. I see fraternity and brotherhood working, hand in hand, for the
continued success of Freemasonry.

I see the faces of men who have inherited the mantle of truth from our
Brother Masons who, 150 years ago, had the vision and foresight to found the
Grand Lodge of California. I see dedicated Masons who are making a difference.
Do you see them, too?

While it’s true that the number of Masons has declined in recent years.
This can be traced primarily, not to the flaws in Masonry, but to flaws in society
itself.

Within the pages of the insightful book, “Bowling Alone-- The Collapse
and Revival of American Community”, author Robert Putnam explains how over
the past 25 years, Americans have become increasingly disconnected from family,
friends, and neighbors, and in doing so, have weakened the threads that bind our
society together. PTA’s, churches, synagogues, social clubs, youth organizations,
political parties, and yes, our Masonic Fraternity, have all suffered declining
membership since the 1960’s.

Does this mean that we, as Masons, should lower our own standards just to
increase our numbers? Is it more important to ensure that Masonry simply survives


200 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

and be damned with the consequences? Should we, like so many politicians listen
to “spin doctors” and then change our beliefs, like a chameleon-- simply desperate
to blend in with the scenery around us?

I do not believe that this is the prescription for restoring Masonry’s health.
Alexander Hamilton wisely said, “Those who stand for nothing -- fall for
anything.” What Masonry needs, more than anything else, is a few good men.
Fewer in numbers, perhaps, but mightier than most -- because we have honor and
truth as our constant guideposts.

During the most trying years of the Civil War, there were less than 6,000
Masons in California. And yet, this relatively small number of men were able to
work together to fulfill their dream of constructing the first Grand Lodge Temple in
San Francisco. That magnificent gothic building, located at the corner of Post and
Montgomery Streets was, at the time, the most beautiful and expensive Masonic
Temple in America.

Is it simply the number of Masons who have made this fraternity so
important? No. It is the measure of the individuals who live by their principles,
and their dreams of making the world a better place, that continues to make me
proud that I am a Mason.

Our Grand Master, Alvin J. Weis, carefully chose a theme for this, the
Sesquicentennial Year of our ancient and honorable Fraternity in California:
“Living the Dream -- Building a Better Tomorrow.” In preparation for this year-
long celebration of 150 years of Masonic heritage, I had the privilege to create the
official Sesquicentennial Painting. It isa montage of many Masons who contributed
their time, and their talents, to make California America’s true Golden State.

Each Mason depicted in this painting had a dream, and just as importantly,
the passion, to put his Masonic principles into action. Of course, the most difficult
part for me as an artist was choosing, among hundreds of thousands of California
Masons, the men whose portraits would be included upon my canvas. After
considerable research, I selected just 34 men to be included in this commemorative
painting.

I think you’ll recognize a few of these men as you gaze at the painting.
Their faces are familiar and their accomplishments are well known: former
Governor of California and Supreme Court Justice, Past Grand Master Earl Warren;
Samuel Clemens, better known as Mark Twain; the “singing cowboy”, Gene Autry;
the extraordinary botanist, Luther Burbank; Cecil B. DeMille, the brilliant film
director; and comedian Oliver Hardy.

There are other portraits of Masons in my painting whose faces are not so
familiar, but their extraordinary contributions are legendary: Leland Stanford, C.P.
Huntington, Charles Crocker, John Bidwell, Charles Gilman and John Sloat.

However, after completing my research, and before I even picked up a
brush and paint, I knew that there were other Masons whose stories so moved me,


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 201

that I felt compelled to include them in the Sesquicentennial Painting. These men
were not famous in their lifetimes, and few Californians even remember their names
today. Their deeds were not chronicled in the newspapers, nor have their tales been
told and retold in classrooms or universities. Yet, these men believed in Masonry
and turned their dreams into realities -- and in doing so, they ensured a better
tomorrow for their families, for their communities and for all of California. They
proved to me just what that one man, one Mason, can do.

I'd like to share two of these Mason’s stories with you. It is the tale of two
men whose unselfish acts of charity and compassion illustrate how good men witha
dream can build a better tomorrow. Journey back with me to 1832 to rural Stark
County, Ohio. It is there that you’d find Dr. John Townsend. The son of English
Quaker parents, Dr. Townsend had been migrating westward from his native
Pennsylvania nearly all of his life. It was while he was practicing medicine in Ohio,
that he met his wife, Elizabeth Schallenberger. A few months after the Townsen!’s
marriage, both of Elizabeth’s parents died, leaving Elizabeth’s 6-year old brother,
Moses, an orphan. Dr. Townsend immediately adopted the young boy and lovingly
raised him as his own son.

Dr. Townsend’s wanderlust had guided him westward throughout his life.
Soon the family moved farther west to Indiana, and then to Missouri. In the spring
of 1844, Dr. Townsend, Elizabeth and Moses embarked upon the journey of a
lifetime -- to California by wagon train. Joining forces with another party of
immigrants, the group headed West with 11 wagons and 40 hopeful pioneers. By
the time they reached the Sierra Nevadas it was already a chilly October. They
learned of a pass through the mountains by conversing with a Native American
whom the settlers called, “Truckee.” They followed the pass through difficult and
hazardous terrain. Along the way, they had to abandon several wagons near a lake.
Three men volunteered to stay with the wagons to guard their precious cargo. One
of these men was Moses Schallenberger, now a young man of 17.

With more than a little misgiving, Dr. Townsend agreed to let Moses stay
with the wagons, while he and Elizabeth journeyed with the rest of the party on to
Sutter’s Fort. Unfortunately, by the time they reached the fort, the snowfall up in
the pass was too deep for a rescue party to return to assist the three men who had
remained with the wagons. Moses and his two companions, sensing that they
quickly needed shelter, erected a log cabin. The cabin was barely completed when
the blizzards came. Fearing that they would perish before help arrived, Moses
agreed to remain at the cabin while the other two men trekked through the snow
drifts to find help.

But for Moses Schallenberger, there would be no rescue for months. He
survived in that barren cabin by himself, until the spring of 1845, subsisting on
whatever game he could trap and occupying his time by voraciously reading books
from Dr. Townsend’s library. He passed that perilous winter alone in the small
cabin that he had built. The same cabin which would later become the only
available shelter for the ill-fated Donner Party.


202 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Imagine his relief, when spring finally came and a rescue party sent by his
adopted father, Dr. Townsend, led this young pioneer, Moses Schallenberger, outof
the mountains. But the story of these two men does not end here. Dr. Townsend
settled in San Francisco and quickly became active in politics. He served as both
Alcalde and School Trustee in 1848 and was the city’s first resident physician. He
moved to San Jose in 1850 and helped to organize San Jose Lodge No. 10. That
autumn waves of deadly cholera were sweeping the state and both Dr. Townsend
and his beloved wife, Elizabeth, spent countless hours nursing the ill and dying.
Tragedy struck when Dr. Townsend and his wife succumbed to the disease within
hours of one another, leaving their own two-year old son alone and orphaned.

And what became of Moses Schallenberger? Once orphaned himself and
then adopted by Dr. Townsend, Moses rushed to his young nephew and raised the
boy as his own son throughout his lifetime, even sending him to college in
Cambridge, England. Moses, who was also a member of San Jose Lodge, certainly
knew the meaning of Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth.

But Masonic principles are not just icons of our past. They are the
beacons of our fraternity’s future. This year we have celebrated our first 150 years.
Let’s look ahead to the year 2050, our Bicentennial year. Which one of our
Masonic brothers will have significantly contributed to our fraternity’s enduring
success? Look around you? Who will it be? Sam Pitassi? Perhaps Sean Foran or
Ron Koretz? What about Fred Kleyn? Don Nielsen? Howard Kirkpatrick or Rick
Hutchinson? Or what about a young Master like Allen Casalou? Perhaps it is the
Brother who is seated next to you right now. It might be your Lodge’s newest
Entered Apprentice. Or maybe, with determination and vision, it could be you.

From practically my first day as Grand Orator last October, I have had
Masonic Brothers asking me if I had finished writing my Grand Oration yet. Their
second question was even more to the point, “Will your Oration address the
problems of the fraternity and include some solutions?”

Since my profession is advertising and marketing, it’seasy to brainstorm
about the issues facing Grand Lodge and the many membership programs that are
currently being implemented to increase the positive image of Masonry. As an
advertising designer, I know the power and influence of a name, and the importane
of positive imagery. In fact, most corporations are willing to spend nearly any
amount of money to increase positive awareness of their products.

Recently, the Prune Marketing Board made a major announcement. They
are allotting 10 million dollars to improve the image of prunes, that much maligned
» but good-for-you fruit. How are they going to do this? Well, they are willing to
spend that 10 million dollars to change the name of prunes to dried plums.

And what does dried fruit have to do with Masonry? Well, it makes me
wonder if most Californians think of Masons in terms of shriveled up old prunes.
Or do you think they believe our fraternity is full of healthy young plums?


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 203

I am reminded, once again, of the old adage that, “the solution is to be
found within the problem itself.” Our fraternity’s longterm problem is that we need
to attract good young men who are interested in becoming better men, and the first
step in doing that is to renew our own sense of optimism and enthusiasm about our
fraternity. Each and every one of us in this Grand Lodge can do this.

What can one Mason do? Last week I heard about one very enthusiastic
Mason, Vartan Manoukian, the Junior Deacon of Tujunga Lodge 592, who has
already brought in 14 new members this year! Did he do this by complaining of the
“good old days?” No, he set his sights on dreams of the future of Masonry and he
has built up his Lodge’s membership through his optimistic attitude and winning
spirit.

What can one Mason do? I’d like to tell you about another Mason who is
making a better tomorrow for our fraternity by working with our Masonic Youth
Orders. My story opens with the tale of a young 12-year old girl who, at the urging
of some childhood friends, joined a Job’s Daughter Bethel here in California.

This young girl had experienced a difficult childhood. Her father
abandoned the family when the girl was just a toddler. Her mother struggled as best
she could to provide for her daughter without any financial support from her ex-
husband. He had just walked out of their lives. There were no father and daughter
weekends. No gifts from Dad under the tree at Christmas. No presents or even a
card on her birthdays from her father, and no dad at her graduation from high school
or any of the important occasions when a young girl wants, and needs, a father’s
love.

But if she was here today, this young woman would tell you, as she has
told me, that she was “lucky.” Lucky because she joined Job’s Daughters and
found, in her Associate Bethel Guardian, a father-figure who, in her own words,
“Showed me just what a man should be.” Her gratitude, affection and admiration
for this Mason who has meant so much in her life, touched me, as I am sure it has
you.

In knowing this Mason, she has been guided by a man who haslooked out
for her best interests, encouraged her in school, and has been a personal role model
of what a father should be. And now, as radio commentator, Paul Harvey, always
likes to say -- here is the “rest of the story.”

For many of you know this young woman personally and most of you saw
her yesterday in this very auditorium. Because the story of this young woman’s life
is the true story of the life of the current Miss California Job’s Daughter, Shanna
Fickentsher. That beautiful young woman who has, through the guidance of our
Masonic Youth Orders in general, and from one Mason in particular, overcome a
difficult childhood and now has a bright future ahead of her. I’m pleased to say that
the Mason who has been her mentor is also a member of my own Lodge, Burbank
No. 406. His name is Scott Nave and I applaud him for making the difference in the
lives of so many young people in our community.


204 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

What can one Mason do? I'd like to tell you about a man who was a
Mason for over 40 years. He was never an officer in his Lodge and he didn’t
always attend Stated Meetings or the functions at the Scottish Rite or his local
Shrine Temple. His name was Floyd Drendel. While it’s true that Floyd didn’t
always attend business meetings of the organizations he belonged to, he still was
one of the finest Masons that I have had the privilege of knowing. Why? Because
Floyd, along with his wife, Shirley, believed in putting Masonry into action.
Although this wonderful couple was not blessed with children of their own,they
were loved by more children than you could ever count. Because Floyd and Shirley
were lifelong volunteers at the Shrine Hospital in Los Angeles. They donated over
16,000 hours at the hospital helping youngsters who were burn victims or suffering
from orthopedic disorders.

It’s a heartwarming story, isn’t it? However, sometimes real life stories
don’t always have fairy tale endings. When Floyd died a few years ago, his widow
was left alone. She continued to volunteer at the Shrine Hospital, and she made a
real effort to attend dinners at her husband’s lodge. On one of these occasions, the
Brethren presented her with a Masonic Widow’s Pin. Yes, she received a pin but,
unfortunately, she never received much more. Because, when her husband died,
Shirley, like too many of our Masonic widows, was simply forgotten about by her
husband’s Lodge Brothers. She lived alone, too proud to ask for help, but too weak
and unsteady on her feet to do even the simplest of chores around her house. Even
an easy task like standing on a step stool to replace a kitchen light bulb was too
difficult for her to do alone. Shirley passed away this spring and left a sizeable
inheritance to the Shrine Hospital. The lodge trestleboards kept on being delivered
to her home because no one at her husband’s lodge even realized that she was gone.

This story has special meaning for me, because as I’ve traveled around
California this year with our Grand Master, I’ve had the privilege of hearing him
relate the story of Mrs. Pamela Murphy, another Masonic widow who seemed to
have slipped through the cracks of our Brotherly responsibilities. Grand Master
Weis received a letter about Mrs. Murphy detailing her life and making a special
note of her tireless volunteer efforts at the Veteran’s Hospital in Los Angeles. The
letter concluded by saying that if Pamela Murphy, the widow of America’s most
decorated war hero and our Masonic Brother, Audie Murphy, could be forgotten
about by the fraternity, then “what might happen to the widows of less famous
Brethren?” Well, I can tell you what happens much too often. Far too many of our
Masonic widows are at home, sitting alone in a darkened house with burned out
light bulbs, waiting for a simple act of kindness from a single Mason.

What can one Mason do? I'd like to share a final story of another Mason,
Jack Trumbower. Jack was a 50 year Mason in Monterey Park, and while in his
younger years, he had attended his own Lodge frequently, after retiring from the
Department of Water & Power in Los Angeles, he wasn’t able to attend Lodge as
often as he liked.

When Jack passed away a little over a year ago, the family met with his


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 205

close friends to plan the memorial service. After checking with Jack’s own Lodge, I
received permission to perform the Masonic Funeral Service. One of Jack’s friends,
who had been designated as executor of the estate, questioned whether or not a
Masonic service was appropriate. “After all,” he told me, “Jack wasn’t a very
active Mason.”

I pondered this for a while, and then gently insisted that the Masonic
service be a part of the funeral. But then I got to thinking, “Who was even going to
be there for the services? A few of his Lodge Brothers, perhaps? My wife, Jane,
my sister-in-law, Kathy?”

But really, all of Jack’s friends and family had either passed away or
moved out of the area, when in recent years, the demographics had switched from a
mostly older, Anglo neighborhood to what is now a community made up entirely of
newly arrived immigrants from Asia.

On the day of Jack’s services, we arrived at the funeral home early, and at
first, there were, as I had feared, only a handful of mourners. And then, one by one,
and later dozens by dozens, people entered the chapel. They were strangers to me.
All of them were Asian -- immigrants from China, Japan, Taiwan and other far off
places. Soon, the chapel was filled to capacity. They sat, respectfully, as I
conducted the Masonic Funeral Services. Then I asked if anyone would like to say
a few words of final tribute to Jack Trumbower.

My wife and her sister, rose first and told everyone of Jack’s kindness to
their family, and particularly to their mother, Virginia, who had passed away in
1984. And then, one by one, people began rising and coming forward to speak in
sorrow, and in gratitude, for the difference that Jack had made in their lives.

For you see, when Jack retired from the DWP he might not have had time
to visit his Masonic Lodge as often as he once did. However, he did find the time to
learn to speak Chinese, and to work at a local community center teaching English to
his new neighbors.

And he helped these new immigrants in many other ways. One young man
told of how in his first few weeks in America, he had gone into Jack’s backyard and
stolen fruit from his tree because his own family was poor and needed something to
eat. The next day, there was a grocery bag overflowing with fruit and other food on
this young man’s doorstep. A gift from Jack to feed a hungry family. Several
young persons followed with similar tales of Jack’s generosity. Books bought for
needy college students, tuition paid when a student loaned had failed to arrive, and
encouraging words from an elderly gentleman to young immigrants to stay in school
and live the American Dream.

The stories just kept coming, each more heartfelt than the last and
accompanied by tears of grief at losing a dear friend. At one point I whispered to
the funeral director apologizing that the services were running too long. He assured
me that everything was fine and to let the tributes keep going on. In all of his


206 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

career, he had never heard such touching stories of generosity.

At the close of the services, I thought to myself, “Was Jack Trumbower an
active Mason?” What better proof than what I had just witnessed! Masonry is felt
in the heart, and expressed in the acts of kindness and generosity which we do each
day. Here was a man, a true Mason, who might not have been able to attend Lodge
all of the time, but lived his life, actively, as a Mason.

What can one Mason do?

He can fulfill a promise to a Brother by taking care of a widow in need.
Several years ago, I had the pleasure of attending Grant Park Lodge near Atlanta,
Georgia. Once a month on a Saturday morning, a few of the Masons from this small
lodge get together for a cup of coffee and a doughnut or two. Then they take a look
at their “Honey Do” lists. You know what I mean. It’s the list of chores that wives
usually have for their husbands detailing little jobs that need fixing around the
house, Perhaps a dripping faucet that needs a new washer. A loose board that
needs to be nailed, A hedge that has to be trimmed, or even replacing a burned out
light bulb in the kitchen ceiling. But this “Honey Do” list hasn’t been made by their
own wives. No, this list is a compilation of things that the Lodge’s Sweethearts,
their Masonic widows, need to have fixed around their homes-- and these Brethren
get the job done!

Could any of us not afford to spare a few hours, once a month, to help our
widows by simply crossing a few chores off of their lists? Why not organize a
“Honey Do” committee at your lodge to check up on our widows and see just how
we can make their day to day lives a little easier.

What can one Mason do?

He can live his life actively as a Mason. All of us do need to work hard to
find the opportunities that will make our Lodges more welcoming and more
inclusive. Ask yourself this simple question, are you the kind of Mason who enters
a room saying, “Here I am!” Or do you come to Lodge and greet every Brother
with, “Ah, there you are!”

There is something else that many Masons could do to make their own
Lodges more successful. They should stay away more often. You heard me right.
Stay away once in a while. Far too many Masons spend every night of the week at
some Masonic related event. It’s Lodge on Mondays; Eastern Star on Tuesday;
Shrine Club on Wednesdays, Amaranth on Thursdays; White Shrine on Fridays; and
then York Rite (or was it the Scottish Rite?) on Saturdays. Maybe, just maybe,
these good intentioned Masons take an occasional Sunday off for “good behavior.”
Oops, I forgot, there’s probably a DeMolay, Job’s Daughters or Rainbow event on
Sundays!

Like most of you, I truly enjoy being a part of the Family of Freemasonry.
However, a big part of our dwindling numbers can be traced to how insular and
inbred we have become. How can you ever meet new people, and introduce them to


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 207

the fellowship and friendship of Masonic life, if you never have time yourself to
meet anyone new?

So take a night off from Masonry now and then. Coach a Little League
team; volunteer at a hospital; attend a mixer at your local Chamber; learn to line
dance; or master your home computer by taking a night school class. Make new
friends and, as you would do with any friend, welcome them into our Masonic
Family. Let’s widen our circle of friends and share the good news about our
wonderful fraternity.

Let’s also open our lodges, and our hearts to our neighbors in our local
communities. Set up an action plan to utilize your lodge building as a true
community resource. Invite the public to your lodge for blood drives; as a polling
center at election time; sponsor first aid classes or create an emergency center in
your lodge in case of a natural disaster or other emergency. Perhaps when our
neighbors see that there are no “hidden agendas” or “dark secrets” in our lodge
buildings, they will be more receptive to becoming members of the Family of
Freemasonry.

I don’t want anyone to leave here today thinking that it will be a simple
task to keep Masonry on the right track in the future. As Brother Will Rogers
reminded us, “Even if you’re on the right track, you’ll get run over if you just sit
there!”

We cannot procrastinate. The time is at hand to celebrate Masonry’s
historic contributions and it’s exciting future. Let’s not dwell on the negatives, nor
mourn the “what might have beens.” Let’s bury the destructive words of doom and
gloom and take up the challenge of building a better tomorrow.

My Brethren, I challenge each of you to go out each day and ask yourself,
“What can I, one Mason, do today for a child? What can I, one Mason, do today
for a widow? What can I, one Mason, do today for my community?” If each of
you, as one Mason, can answer these simple questions every day, I promise you that
you will not only be living your dream of being a better Mason, but you will be
building a better tomorrow where Masonry’s light will shine ever brighter.

This is a challenge that, together, we can meet, and conquer, only by
proudly upholding our Masonic principles and putting them into action on every
day that the Great Architect of the Universe graciously gives to us.

I’d like to leave you with these words of wisdom from Dr. Martin Luther
King, Jr. “The ultimate measure of a man isnot where he stands in moments of
comfort, but where he stands at the time of challenge.” My Brethren, join with me
and take up the challenge. Thank you.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 211

GRAND MASTER’S DECISIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

Grand Master’s Decision No. 1
Admission to the Masonic Home

I have been asked as Grand Master to interpret the meaning of Section
8010 of the California Masonic Code as it pertains to the ability of the Board of
Trustees to admit Master Masons, and their wives or widows, to the Home as
residents without requiring them to surrender all their assets to the Masonic
Home as a condition of admission. I have ruled that there is no requirement in
this section of the code, or in any other that requires a resident to surrender all
assets as a precondition to admission. My reasons for this are as follows:

= Section 8010 of the code empowers the trustees to provide facilities for
the “maintenance and support of the aged, indigent or infirm Master
Masons, their wives, widows and children...” There is nothing in this
language which requires any such person to surrender all personal and
real property to the Masonic Homes before he or she can become a
resident. In fact, there is no requirement that the person be indigent in
order to be accepted into the Home. The language of this section states
that the person admitted to the Home can be aged, or poor, or infirm,
but there is no requirement that he or she be all of these at the same
time.

«The present practice of requiring a Mason, or his wife or widow, to
surrender all assets is a policy of the Board of Trustees, but it is no
more than that. It is a policy that can be changed, as time and necessity
require it to be changed. The policy is based on a way of taking care of
the elderly that was once common practice, but which has now become
largely obsolete. The policy was established in the days before Social
Security or retirement plans became available, and, before home
ownership was as common as it is now. Although ’all Masonic homes
in this country once followed our practice, almost all have now
abandoned the total surrender of assets for a system that allows a
variety of means of admission to their homes. California is one of the
last in the nation that forcibly “impoverishes” an applicant by taking all
assets before allowing admission to the Home. Indeed, some Masonic
homes made the change as many as twenty years ago.

* Many California Masons, and their wives or widows, would like to
retire with dignity to our Masonic Home. We have the room, and they
have the ability to pay all or part of the costs without completely
surrendering their home and all their life savings as a condition of
admission. They would gladly pay rent, or they would gladly commit
substantial portions of their assets, to live in Union City or Covina. But
they do not want to surrender all their personal assets which, in many
cases, are expected to exceed the cost to live in one of our Masonic


212 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Homes. And after providing for themselves in their declining years,
they want to leave something to their children and grandchildren. The
present “complete surrender of assets” policy discourages them from
applying, and as it is not a requirement in the California Masonic Code,
there is no reason that the Homes Board of Trustees cannot provide
more than one option for admission to our Home, depending upon
individual circumstances.

The Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes has unanimously
endorsed this interpretation of our code. They wish to offer alternatives for
admission to our Homes. A recent survey of our members and their wives, and
of our Masonic widows, and published in the California Freemason magazine,
clearly shows that change is needed. Seventy-nine percent of those who
returned the surveys indicated that they were interested in moving into our
Homes. But only 8% of those returning the surveys were interested in
surrendering all their personal possessions and property in order to do so.

Expert legal counsel, specializing in tax law pertaining to organizations
such as our Home, has reviewed the proposed change in our admission policy,
and affirmed that the same is consistent with current state and federal tax law.

If you agree with me that the California Masonic Code allows the
Homes Board of Trustees to make these appropriate decisions, please vote “yes”
on Grand Master’s Decision No. 1. NOTE: A simple majority is sufficient to
sustain a Grand Master’s Decision.

If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to the
appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretative decisions are recorded:

Neither Section 8010 of the California Masonic Code nor any other
part of our law requires a resident of the Masonic Homes of California to
surrender all assets as a precondition to admission.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

Maintenance and support of the aged, indigent or infirm Master
Masons, their wives or widows and children is the intended purpose of our
Masonic Homes. We have the space in our facilities and it has never been the
practice of this fraternity to cause impoverishment or undue financial hardship.
The opinions outlined in this decision grant to the Board of Trustees of the
Masonic Homes some alternatives by which they may better serve our
membership.

Grand Masters’ Decision No. 1 is consistent with the policy and general
purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Decision AFFIRMED.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 213

Grand Master’s Decision No. 2
Suspension of Master of Brotherhood Lodge No. 370

In June of this year I suspended the Master of Brotherhood Lodge No.
370 for failure to properly discharge his duties as Master of the Lodge.
Although the suspension of the installed Master of a Lodge is an extreme
measure, it is the responsibility of the Grand Master to enforce the laws of this
jurisdiction, and to ensure that lodges conform to those laws. The failure of the
Master of Brotherhood Lodge No. 370 to do so, and his repeated refusal to
acknowledge the problems facing him, required the suspension.

In 1999/2000 Brotherhood Lodge had experienced repeated abuse of
the ballot box by different factions within the Lodge. In order to bring the
factions together, I appointed an experienced Master Mason from the Officers’
Management Workshop Committee to set up a Reconciliation Task Force to
investigate the problems in the Lodge, and to develop an action plan to resolve
them. The Master failed to cooperate with the task force, and did not encourage
his officers to attend task force meetings.

The Reconciliation Task Force confirmed that the factionalism in the
Lodge was caused by the Master and his officers offering free meals to some
members of the Lodge, but not to others. In addition, it found that the Master
had repeatedly submitted bills for reimbursement which had not been authorized
by the Lodge, and continued the practice of intimidating the Secretary and
Treasurer into paying them without a proper vote.

Although given several chances to stop the violations of the California
Masonic Code which were the root cause of the abuse of the ballot box, the
Master ignored my directives and failed to support any effort to resolve the
problem. After meeting with him personally, and at length in my office, I
determined that he had no intention of following the code, or working to resolve
the problem. As an alternative to suspending the charter under section 20050 of
the code, I suspended him as Master of the lodge under Section 1050—C of the
California Masonic Code. That section requires that I lay this decision before
Grand Lodge at the next Annual Communication, and therefore Decision No. 2
is before you for your consideration.

If you agree with me, please mark your ballots ‘yes’ on Grand Master’s
Decision No. 2.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

Grand Master’s Decision No. 2 is within the power and prerogative of
the Grand Master and is therefore consistent with the policy and general
purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Decision AFFIRMED.


214 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 1
Eliminating Restrictions on the Family of Freemasonry

Freemasonry is blessed with the existence of the organizations which
make up the “Family of Freemasonry”. These are the organizations associated
with Craft Masonry which either carry our teachings further (the concordant
bodies such as the Scottish Rite and the York Rite), and those whose
membership is made up of Freemasons, but which exist for fellowship and
charitable work (such as the Shrine or High Twelve clubs). Some of these
organizations include both men and women (such as the Eastern Star). The
latter are called appendant bodies. These concordant and appendant bodies are
“Masonic” in that they have a close association with Freemasonry, but they have
their own purposes, membership policies and governing structures. Over the
last decade Grand Lodge has continued to strongly support these members of the
Family of Freemasonry, while refraining from interfering in their internal
policies and affairs. It is now time to take this one step further. I believe that
the time has come to eliminate those sections of our code which give Grand
Lodge supervision over the few remaining internal affairs and policies of these
organizations. Master Masons participating in such organizations will still be
bound by their obligations as Masons, and as such, subject to the penal power of
their Lodge, and of Grand Lodge. But I believe that it is time for Grand Lodge
to set these organizations free from the few remaining restrictive sections of our
code. It is therefore my recommendation that the following modifications be
made:

= Sections of the Code regulating activities of “Masonic Organizations”
be amended to delete the references to a “Masonic Organization.”

"Section 15130 of the code, which requires Grand Lodge approval for
new degree-conferring organizations to operate in California, be
deleted.

If you agree with me that the California Masonic Code is too restrictive
with the concordant and appendant bodies, please vote “yes” on Grand Master’s
Recommendation No. 1. It will allow the Grand Lodge to spend its time and
efforts more properly with the governance of Ancient Craft Masonry as
envisioned by our Founders in 1850.

If you adopt this recommendation, Section 1050 of the California
Masonic Code will be amended as marked:

§1050. POWERS.
The Grand Master may:

A. Convene any Lodge, preside therein, inspect its proceedings, and
compel its conformity to Masonic usage;

DUES

orphan.

Section 2. The Master, at the Stated Meeting next succeeding his installation,
shall appoint an Auditing Committee, whose duty it shall be to examine all
accounts presented against the Lodge.

Section 3. A committee on delinquent members shall be appointed by the
Master at the Stated Meeting next succeeding his installation to personally, or
otherwise, interview each delinquent member, ascertain the cause, and work
closely with the Master and officers in a continuing effort to effect the retention
of all deserving members unable to pay.

Section 4. All reports of committees shall be in writing.

AMENDMENTS

Section 1. These By-Laws, relating to the times of meetings, and the amounts of
fees, dues, and disbursements by the Charity Committee, may be amended at
any Stated Meeting by a two-thirds vote of the members present, if the proposed
amendment, in writing, has been presented at a preceding Stated Meeting and
written notice has been given to each Lodge member setting forth the text of the
proposed amendment and the date of the Stated Meeting at which the vote is to
be taken. It shall have no effect until approved by Grand Lodge or the Grand
Master, and until approval has been transmitted to the Grand Secretary. The
Secretary of the Lodge shall note in the minutes of the Lodge the notice of
proposed amendments when made. When transmitting an amendment for
approval, he shall forward a certificate, under seal, showing that the law has
been complied with and that the amendment has received the required vote.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

Grand Master Weis’ Recommendation No. 1 seeks to eliminate
numerous provisions from the Masonic Code that are said to “interfere” with the
internal policies of Masonically affiliated bodies. Removal of these code
sections would effectively eliminate any and all authority of Grand Lodge to
require these affiliated bodies to conform to and abide by the principle tenets of
Freemasonry. In fact, removal of these code sections would deprive Grand
Lodge of any authority over these bodies whatsoever. Accordingly, the
Recommendation contradicts long standing Grand Lodge policy, does not


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 229

accomplish the stated objectives, would cause public confusion regarding the
nature of Freemasonry and would have a negative impact on the core values of
our fraternity.

Under this Recommendation, Grand Lodge would voluntarily give up
its right to approve the constitutions and by-laws of Masonically affiliated
bodies. Thus, Grand Lodge could not require that such bodies have only
Masons or Masonically related persons as members. Thus any organization
could claim to be “Masonically affiliated” when in fact it is not. Nor, under this
Recommendation, could Grand Lodge require that members of such
organizations be subject to the same standards of conduct and propriety as
Master Masons, which the CMC now requires. By voluntarily giving up these
rights, Grand Lodge may be deemed to have given up its right to discipline
Masons for conduct which violates Masonic law, if such conduct is permitted by
the affiliated organization.

The Recommendation would also have Grand Lodge voluntarily
remove the provision of the CMC that prohibits Masonic Organizations from
using the Square and Compass and words such as “Mason”, “Masonic” and
“Freemasonry” in connection with the solicitation of funds. Grand Lodge would
thus implicitly permit the use of such symbols and terms by any organization,
whether or not Masonically affiliated.

The very essence of Freemasonry is that all Masons live and act
according to upright moral standards. By voluntarily giving up its right to
require that members of affiliated organizations also conform to such standards,
the “Family of Freemasonry” would not be a family at all. Rather, true Masonic
conduct and character would be relegated to the Blue Lodges alone.

For all of the foregoing reasons, Grand Master Weis’ Recommendation
No. 1 is not consistent with Grand Lodge policy or general purposes.

Recommendation NOT ADOPTED.

Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 2
Proposing Members for Election

Master Masons in California have been taught, from Time Immemorial,
that they should never recommend a man for participation in our privileges
unless they have strong reasons to believe that he would ultimately reflect honor
on our ancient institution. They have been further taught that no one should
ever be solicited to take the degrees of Masonry, so that a candidate might
truthfully state that he is “freely and voluntarily” offering himself “as a
candidate for the mysteries of Masonry.” As Grand Master I believe in the
importance of this principle. A man must seek out Freemasonry of his “own
free will and accord.” Our obligations are of such a serious nature that we do
not want anyone to be “talked into” becoming a Mason.


230 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

That being said, it is evident that all too many Master Masons are afraid
to talk about Freemasonry to their friends and family members who are eligible
for the degrees of Masonry, and who would make good Masons. To make this
an easier task for our members, we have created the “Friend-to-Friend” program
through which prospective members can become familiar with Freemasonry,
and have sufficient knowledge about us so that they eventually ask for an
application. When conducted properly, the “Friend-to-Friend” program can
overcome the reluctance of many Master Masons to talk about Freemasonry to
their friends and family members who might want to become Masons if they just
knew how.

There is another problem which exists, however. If we share
information with a man who would make a good Mason, and he appears to have
all the qualifications to make a good Mason, and if we then have him fill out an
application form and handle it in the usual manner, we run the risk that the
application will not pass the unanimous ballot requirement at the end of the
process. That has some rather serious consequences for our “Friend-to-Friend”
program. I am therefore proposing a solution which will preserve the sanctity of
the requirement of a unanimous vote for admission to the Fraternity, but one
which will make the “Friend-to-Friend” program work much more smoothly. I
am proposing that we ballot on a proposal for membership before we approach a
man whom we believe to be a good man, and who would make a good Mason.
If the proposed member stands the test of a unanimous ballot, we then extend an
invitation to him to find out more about Freemasonry. If he responds favorably,
and fills out an application, it will then be accepted without a further vote,

because we have already unanimously approved the application. There are some
safeguards built into my proposal, and they are as follows:

 

= A member proposing a man for membership in his Lodge must know
enough about him to have “strong reasons to believe that he would
ultimately reflect honor on our ancient institution.” Those reasons
would have to be stated in the proposal form.

= No proposal for membership would be valid unless it had passed the
test of the unanimous vote by secret ballot in the traditional manner.
One black cube would be sufficient to stop the proposal from going
forward.

= If the proposal has a unanimous vote as in a regular application for
membership, the prospective Mason would be approached by the
Lodge “Friend-to-Friend” committee who would share information
with him about Freemasonry. They would be in a position to indicate
that he had been recommended to the Lodge by a friend, and that the
Lodge had voted to invite him to learn more about Freemasonry. The

committee would be prohibited from indicating to him that _he had
already been elected to membership.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 231

= — If he is interested, an application will be filled out in the usual manner,
and then received by the Lodge. Again, the Master will appoint an
investigating committee in the usual manner, and they will individually
interview the applicant, reporting confidentially to the Master as
always.

= If the report of the investigating committee to the Master is full and
favorable, or favorable, and if there is no negative report to the Master
by any member of the committee, the Master will announce it at the
next Stated Meeting, and the applicant will thereupon be declared
elected. If the report is not favorable, the Master will announce that
fact, and the investigating report will be read. A ballot in the traditional
manner will thereupon be taken, and one black cube will be sufficient
for rejection.

This proposal will have several beneficial effects:

1. The “Friend-to-Friend” committee will know that a prospective
member has approval of the Lodge in advance before they spend time
and effort acquainting him with Freemasonry. They will have
confidence in doing so knowing that he will become a member unless
something of a serious nature turns up in the course of the usual
investigation.

2. The prospective Mason will know that the Lodge wants him to become
a member, and that will build his confidence in asking for an
application.

3. It will cause our members to look around for good prospective Masons,
and propose them for membership in the Lodge. The fact that the
proposal will stand the test of the unanimous ballot in advance will
remove some of the fear of rejection when suggesting someone who
might make a good Mason, and avoid the embarrassment of leading a
man to believe that we want him as a Mason, only to suffer rejection at
the end of the process. Of course rejection is still possible, but I
believe that it will be much more unlikely. And this system will help
members of the Lodge to think through their responsibility when
casting a vote at the ballot box.

If approved, this new system will operate alongside the traditional
method of applying for membership. I am not proposing that the traditional
method be replaced. Instead, I am proposing an alternative that will work better
with the “Friend-to-Friend” program, while still preserving the requirement that
a man seek out Masonic membership of his “own free will and accord”, and also
preserving the requirement that every applicant for the degrees of Masonry be
elected by unanimous secret ballot.

If you adopt this recommendation, a new Section 26095 will be added to
the California Masonic Code to read as follows:


232 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

§26095. MEMBERSHIP PROPOSALS.
Any person not_a Mason who is eligible for membership in the Lodge may be

proposed to receive the degrees of Masonry at any Stated Meeting by a proposal in
writing signed by one member of the Lodge and seconded by another. Such
proposal may be made without the knowlede of the person proposed. A member
proposing a man for membership in his Lodge must know enough about him to
have strong reasons to believe that he would ultimately reflect honor on our
ancient institution. Those reasons shall be stated in the proposal form. The
proposal shall be received in the same manner as an application for the degrees.

At the Stated Meeting next following its receipt, the proposal shall be balloted
upon in the same manner as an application for the degrees. If all members of the
Lodge present have voted and no negative vote appears, the prospective Mason
shall_be approached by the Lodge “Friend-to-Friend” committee whose
members shall share information with him about Freemasonry. The members of
the “Friend-to-Friend” committee may indicate that he has been recommended
to the Lodge by a friend and that the Lodge has voted to invite him to learn more
about Freemasonry. The committee is prohibited from indicating to him that he
has already been elected to membership.

If the prospective Mason is interested, he shall complete an application for the
degrees in the usual manner. His application shall be presented to and received
by the Lodge in the usual manner. The Master shall appoint an investigating
committee in the usual manner whose members shall individually interview the
applicant, reporting confidentially to the Master.

If the report of the investigating committee to the Master is full and favorable, or
favorable, and if there is no negative report to the Master by any member of the
committee, the Master shall announce it at the next Stated Meeting, and the
applicant shall thereupon be declared elected. If the report is not favorable, the
Master shall announce that fact, and the investigating report shall be read. A
ballot in the usual manner shall thereupon be taken, and one black cube shall be
sufficient for rejection.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

This Grand Lodge created the “Friend to Friend” program to assist
constituent Lodges with membership enhancement. The Lodges that are using
this program in its present form have found the program to be very successful.
Presently a man petitions his Lodge for the Degrees of Masonry, “of his own
free will and accord”, is investigated and then voted on. He then becomes a
candidate to receive the degrees of Masonry in our ancient and honorable
fraternity. Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 2 would allow a secondary
path to membership in our mysteries.

There is a significant distinction between the present election process
of a candidate and the proposed election of a prospective candidate. Under this


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 233

proposed process, the Lodge votes on a prospective candidate prior to his
petition being read. Balloting on a proposed candidate before he is investigated
puts the cart before the horse. There is no real time limit on this proposed
election. If in the future the man petitions the Lodge and the report from the
Master’s Investigating Committee comes back not favorable, a second election
would then be held after the report is read in open Lodge.

This second ballot is in violation of Section 26130 of the CMC and is
in conflict with present Grand Lodge policy. One of the purposes of our
fraternity is to “make good men better.” The policy of our Grand Lodge is to
identify and investigate candidates from a pool of applicants prior to admission
into the mysteries of our craft. Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 2,
postponing the investigation until after an election of a potential candidate and
voting on a man who has not been investigated, is not consistent with the policy
and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Recommendation NOT ADOPTED.

Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 3
Increasing the Limit on Spending by Hall Associations

Section 6050 of the California Masonic Code requires that a contract
for or commencement of the construction, alteration, improvement, replacement,
repair, or maintenance of a Masonic Temple in excess of $15,000 be approved
by the Committee on Temple Properties. With increased costs today, this
limitation is not realistic. It is therefore my recommendation that the limit be
increased from $15,000 to $25,000. Raising the limit will allow greater freedom
for local hall associations to make necessary repairs and improvements without
having to seek approval from the committee in advance. Major projects in
excess of $25,000 must still receive approval from the committee.

If you adopt this recommendation, 6050 of the California Masonic Code
will be amended as marked:

§6050. TEMPLE PROPERTIES AND TAXATION.

The Committee on Temple Properties shall examine and approve all details
regarding any contract involving a Lodge, Temple Association, committee,
partnership or corporation or other entity representing itself to be Masonic in
character, or operating for the benefit of a Lodge or Lodges in this Jurisdiction
for:


234 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

A. Any option or contract for the purchase of real property, the
encumbrance of real property or the lease of real property, as lessor or
lessee, for more than three years;

B. A contract for or commencement of the construction, alteration,
improvement, replacement, repair, or maintenance of a Masonic
Temple in excess of $25,000 $45,000; and

Cc. Any option or contract regarding the sale or listing for sale of any real
property owned by such entity.

The committee shall, subject to the approval of the Grand Master, establish rules
and regulations governing the processing of applications for committee
approval.

The committee shall review the reports of transactions of Temple Associations
submitted to the Grand Secretary as provided for elsewhere in this Code and
may give counsel and advise concerning the operation of those Temple
Associations.

The committee shall, also, advise Lodges and the Grand Lodge on matters of
taxation with respect to income, property, sales and employment taxes.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

The Grand Master has recommended that CMC 6050b be changed from
$15,000 to $25,000 before Temple Properties would be called for approval of a
Masonic Center Project.

This change would allow greater flexibility in making needed repairs
and improvement without having to seek committee approval in advance.

This recommendation is consistent with the Grand Lodge Policy and
General Purposes.

Report of the Committee on Temple Properties and Taxation:

Your Committee on Temple Properties and Taxation is aware that costs
of construction, improvement, replacement, repair and maintenance of a
Masonic hall or center generally rise with economic trends. While it is
important to recognize that this committee is willing to counsel your association
regarding transactions under $25,000 on a voluntary basis, it is more important
to allow associations greater autonomy in spending their money for necessary
expenses. The committee strongly supports this increase from $15,000 to
$25,000.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 235

It is important to note however, if this recommendation is passed that
association projects, which cost in excess of $25,000, will continue to require
review by this committee.

This committee views the Grand Master’s Recommendation as a
necessary step in addressing inflationary adjustments and one that eliminates
unnecessary micro managing.

Recommendation ADOPTED.
