280 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

The proponents of this resolution believe that there is a lack of men to
assume leadership roles in many of the lodges in California, and that this void
could be filled by Past Masters. However, they also believe that many such Past
Masters are hesitant to undertake such responsibilities because of the
requirement that they must relearn the ritual. The proponents believe that the
ability to lead is not related to the ability to memorize ritual, and propose that,
since Past Masters qualified at some time before, they be excluded from the
requirement of certification thenceforth.

The senior officers of the Lodge, the Master in particular, are charged
with the responsibility of overseeing all of the activities in the lodge, including
the ritual. As such, they themselves must be proficient in that ritual. In 1989,
your Committee on Policy and General Purposes enunciated this principle by
noting that, if someone wants to sit in the East and judge the proficiency level of
another, his own knowledge must be complete and thorough. In 1997, in
response to a resolution intended to exempt officers from requalifying for two
years, the Ritual Committee observed that holding a certificate attesting that a
man was proficient at one time does not mean that he is proficient now.

This resolution will also establish a two-tier system for men who desire
to become Masters of their lodges, an act that is contrary to the egalitarian
nature of Masonry. It also does not consider the possibility that a man may have
served as Master of his lodge but not have been a Junior or Senior Warden, and
thus never have qualified for one or both of those positions.

It may be true that the ability to learn and deliver ritual and the ability
to administer a lodge are separate skills, and that one may exist in the absence of
another. However, this situation does not only apply to Past Masters. It is
illogical to use such an argument to remove the onus of requalification from Past
Masters but to insist that it remain for those who are going to be Masters for the
first time.

This resolution is not consistent with our current policy and general
purposes.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:

All members desiring to become officers must qualify in their
respective positions. All Inspectors are required to attend Schools of Instruction
in order to retain the accuracy of our ritual. Proficiency and accuracy are the
result of a diligent and constant application of effort and time. We recommend a
“no” vote.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 281

Resolution No. 00-09
Changes “Organist” to “Musician”

The proponents of this Resolution seek to change the title of Lodge “Organist”
to “Musician.”

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

WHEREAS, there is an office known in the constituent lodges as
“Organist,” whose duty is to provide keyboard music on the organ or piano,
musically bringing peace and harmony to the brethren for the various degrees of
Masonry and for enjoyment; and

WHEREAS, while the organ and the piano are instruments that
musically provide much enjoyment and great fulfillment, they are not the only
instruments in the musical family which could emanate beauty, peace and
harmony; and

WHEREAS, a constituent Lodge may deem it appropriate to have a
musician that is not an organist or pianist and therefore the definition of
“Organist” would confine a qualified and talented brother musician to the
limiting definition of Lodge “Organist.”

NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the constituent Lodge
office known as “Organist” be changed to “Musician”.

BE IT RESOLVED that the all references to “Organist” in the Ritual of
the constituent Lodges be changed to “Musician.”

BE IT RESOLVED that the references to “Organist” in Sections 19040
and 21080 of the California Masonic Code be changed to “Musician.”

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

Resolution No. 00-09 seeks to change the title of Lodge Organist to
Musician due to the fact that the organ and piano are not the only musical
instruments that could be used during the various degrees of Masonry. Even
electronic keyboards could be used to produce the desired type of music. The
type of music used during the degrees of Masonry should be left to the choice of
the constituent Lodges with the approval of the Ritual Committee.

This Resolution is consistent with Grand Lodge policy and general
purposes.


282 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Report of the Committee on Ritual:
Ritual is ambivalent on this resolution and has no recommendation.
Resolution CARRIED-OVER.

Resolution No. 00-10
Clarifies Ritual Requirements of Officers

The proponents of this Resolution seek to clarify the ritual requirements for
Lodge officers.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

WHEREAS, Section 26340 of the California Masonic Code states that
an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft shall only be required to recite from
memory the obligation of each degree, together with the signs and modes of
recognition of each degree; and

WHEREAS, Grand Master Wordlow’s Decision No. 2, interpreting
Section 26340 (affirmed by the Grand Lodge in 1998), a decision not binding
his successors, permitted a candidate to voluntarily use the traditional “long
form” proficiency to demonstrate his proficiency for advancement; and

WHEREAS, prior to 1997, it was essential for the Senior Warden to be
proficient in the candidates lecture of the three degrees in order to fulfill his
assigned duties as chief of Candidate’s coaching; and

WHEREAS, commensurate with the adoption of Section 26340 of the
California Masonic Code by the Grand Lodge of California, the unwritten
qualification requirements of the Senior Warden were and should have been
modified accordingly; and

WHEREAS, Section 25080 of the California Masonic Code prescribes
the minimum qualifications to be met by a Senior Warden-elect before he can be
installed in that office; and

WHEREAS, the presiding Master of any Lodge would be perfectly
justified and correct to deny the coaching and return of a proficiency other than
required by Section 26340 of the California Masonic Code, Decision No. 2
(1998), notwithstanding; and

WHEREAS, Resolution No. 98-03 which proposed to restore the “long
form” proficiency for the First and Second Degrees that existed prior to 1997
FAILED; and

WHEREAS, a District Inspector has mandated, with the concurrence of
the Assistant Grand Lecturer, that a Senior Warden-elect must be proficient in
the traditional “long form” proficiencies of the three degrees, an action having


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 283

the effect of perpetuating the “long form” proficiency of the First and Second
Degrees in direct contravention of the spirit and intent of Section 26340 of the
California Masonic Code.

NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 26340 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§26340. ADVANCEMENT.

A Lodge shall advance an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft to
a higher degree if he has given satisfactory evidence, by
examination in open Lodge, that he is proficient and well
qualified in the degrees which he has already taken. To
demonstrate such proficiency, an Entered Apprentice or Fellow
Craft shall only be required to recite from memory the obligation
of each degree, together with the signs and modes of recognition
of each degree. Any number of applicants may be examined at a
meeting, but not more than five shall be examined at any one
time with the questions being asked in rotation. Such
examination shall be held in front of the East between the dtar
and the Master’s station.

A member shall not be required for any reason to be proficient in
a Candidate’s Lecture in the Entered Apprentice or the Fellow

Craft _ degrees in excess of that required under this section for
advancement.

If for any reason there is an erroneous conferral of a degree or
degrees, the candidate retains the status reached but must wait
one year and present an application for a higher degree or
degrees.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

According to Section 25080 of the CMC, a Senior Warden-elect must
be qualified to give the work and lectures of the Entered Apprentice and Fellow
Craft Degrees. A District Inspector interpreted this section to mean that the
Senior Warden-elect had to be proficient in the long-form proficiencies of those
degrees, even though Section 26340 specifies that advancement only requires
knowledge of the obligation, signs and modes of recognition. In other words,
under current law no member is required to memorize the long-form
proficiency.

The authors of this resolution seek to clarify the requirements for
officer qualification. Specifically they are attempting to add to Section 26340 a
statement that no member of a lodge will have to memorize any more candidate
proficiency material than that which is required of candidates as they advance.


284 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

As Section 26340 deals with the advancement of candidates only, it is
not consistent with our policy to include any other expectations or requirements.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:

As we understand the resolution, it eliminates the “long form”
proficiency in the first and second degrees. Ritual has long supported the use of
only the “short form” proficiencies. We recommend a “yes” vote.

Resolution CARRIED-OVER.

Resolution No. 00-11
Revises Qualifications of Master, Senior Warden and Junior Warden

The proponents of this Resolution seek to revise the qualifications of the Master,
Senior Warden and Junior Warden.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

WHEREAS, Section 25060 of the California Masonic Code allows that
every voter shall be eligible for any office in the Lodge; and

WHEREAS, Section 25110 of the California Masonic Code allows
sixty days to qualify for installation unless, for good cause, additional time is
granted by the Inspector; and

WHEREAS, Section 19040, Article I, Section 1, lists the officers of
which a Lodge will consist, including a Master, Senior Warden and Junior
Warden; and

WHEREAS, from time to time, it becomes necessary for a Lodge to
elect a Master or a Warden with no prior qualification in the work of the Senior
Deacon; and

WHEREAS, nothing in Section 25090 of the California Masonic Code
requires prior qualification of a Junior Warden-elect in the work of the Senior
Deacon; and

WHEREAS, the words “qualified to give the work and lectures of the
Entered Apprentice and Fellow Craft Degrees,” in Section 25080A of the
California Masonic Code have been interpreted by some, and not others, to
make qualification in the work of the Senior Deacon a prerequisite for
qualification as Junior or Senior Warden; and

WHEREAS, Section 25100 of the California Masonic Code seems to
recognize that the Senior Deacon’s work and lecture of the Fellow Craft Degree
applies only to the qualification of a Senior Deacon; and


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 285

WHEREAS, a District Inspector has mandated, with the concurrence of
the Assistant Grand Lecturer, that a Junior Warden-elect not previously
qualified as Senior Deacon during the prior year must qualify as such; and, a
Senior Warden-elect not previously qualified as Junior Warden must qualify as
Junior Warden, including the work of the Senior Deacon; and

WHEREAS, imposing the requirement that anyone elected to any one
of the principal officer stations must also qualify in the work of the Senior
Deacon, in all degrees, virtually assures that an elected officer with no prior
service as Senior Deacon would not be able to meet such expanded requirements
within a reasonable time; and

WHEREAS, no legislative powers to amend or repeal Ordinances are
reserved to the Grand Lodge and therefore unilateral action by an Inspector or
any other person to add a qualification requirement not currently contained in
the Code is without basis of authority.

NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 25070 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§25070. QUALIFICATIONS OF MASTER.

Before a Master-elect can be installed there shall be presented
to the Installing Officer the Certificate of the Grand Lecturer,
Assistant Grand Lecturer of the division in which his Lodge is
situated, or the Inspector of the district in which his Lodge is
situated, certifying that:

A. He has personally examined the Master-elect and that
he is qualified to give the work and lectures in the

three degrees, excluding the work of any appointive

officer;

B. He has received a Certificate of Proficiency in the
Candidate’s Lecture of the Master Mason Degree;
and

Cc He is proficient in those portions of this Code that

relate to the government of a Lodge.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 25080 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§25080. QUALIFICATIONS OF SENIOR WARDEN.

Before a Senior Warden-elect can be installed there shall be
presented to the Installing Officer the Certificate of the Grand


286 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lecturer, the Assistant Grand Lecturer of the division in which
his Lodge is situated, or the Inspector the district in which his
Lodge is situated, certifying that:

A. He has personally examined the Senior Warden-elect,
and that he is qualified to give the work and lectures
of the Entered Apprentice and Fellow Craft Degrees,

excluding the work of any appointive officer;

B. He has received a Certificate of Proficiency in the
Candidate’s Lecture of the Master Mason Degree;
and

Cc. He is proficient in those portions of this Code that

relate to the government of a Lodge.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 25090 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§25090. QUALIFICATIONS OF JUNIOR WARDEN.

Before a Junior Warden-elect can be installed there shall be
presented to the Installing Officer the Certificate of the Grand
Lecturer, the Assistant Grand Lecturer of the division in which
his Lodge is situated, or the Inspector of the district in which
his Lodge is situated, certifying that:

A. He has personally examined such Junior
Warden-elect, and that he is qualified to give the
work and lecture of the Entered Apprentice Degree,

excluding the work of any appointive officer;

B. He has received a Certificate of Proficiency in the
Candidate’s Lecture of the Master Mason Degree;
and

Cc. He is proficient in those portions of this Code that

relate to the government of a Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

Section 25100 of the CMC requires that a Junior Warden be qualified
to give the work and lecture of the Entered Apprentice Degree, and section
25080 that a Senior Warden be qualified to give the work and lectures of the
Entered Apprentice and Fellow Craft Degrees. With regard to the Fellow Craft
Degree, more than one lecture exists, the one given by the Senior Deacon and
known by many Masons as the “Staircase Lecture” and the one given by the


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 287

Worshipful Master from the East. However, the CMC does not specify which
lecture or lectures are to be recited as part of the qualification process.

The proponents of this resolution are pointing out the potential
ambiguity of these sections, an ambiguity that has had practical ramifications.
In at least one Masonic District in this Jurisdiction, different inspectors have
made different interpretations of the law regarding what a man has to do to
qualify for Warden or Master. In that District, depending on the year, an
incoming Warden or Master who had not previously been a Senior Deacon may
or may not have had to recite the Staircase lecture.

It is appropriate that a uniform standard be established regarding
qualification for office. Of course, this standard could be established by edict
from the Ritual Committee rather than by the legislative process. Nevertheless,
ambiguity in our law is inconsistent with our policy, and this resolution does
address it. In addition to resolving this area of confusion, it is necessary to
provide a specific delineation of what is expected from those men who assume
leadership roles in our lodges. This resolution is consistent with our policies and
general purposes.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:

We believe California Masonic Code Sections 25070, 25080 and
25090 adequately define the required qualifications of these respective Officers.
We recommend a “no” vote.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 00-12
Establishes Right to Wear Religious Items

The proponents of this Resolution seek to give members the right to wear
religious items.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

WHEREAS, it has been the tradition of Freemasonry in general and
this Grand Lodge in particular to require the belief in a Supreme Being and a
continued existence; and

WHEREAS, it has been the tradition of Freemasonry in general and
this Grand Lodge in particular to support and encourage its membership to obey
the rules, traditions and laws laid down in their version of the Sacred Law.

NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED that a candidate or member
may wear any religious ornament or article that is required to be worn for the


288 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

reason of observing his religious beliefs and shall not be required to remove
same at any Masonic activity, whether or not tiled.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

Resolution No. 00-12 establishes the right to wear religious items
within a lodge. Under the present Code no such resolution is necessary.

The proponents of this Resolution seek to give members the right to
wear religious items. The Resolution seeks to permit a candidate or member to
wear any religious ornament or article that is requiredto be worn for reasons of
observing the religious faith or belief of the particular candidate or member at
whatever Masonic activity may be attended by the candidate or member whether
or not tiled. This would include social activities, degrees, stated meetings and
any other place where Masons may meet. While this statement seems simple
enough, the Resolution itself is only two lines long, the historical significance of
the Resolution regretfully is not.

Section 380 of the California Masonic Code states as follows:

“POLITICS AND RELIGION. Grand Lodge and constituent
Lodges shall not interfere or voice an opinion with regard to
political or religious matters. Members when they are
assembled in constituent Lodges or Grand Lodge, may not
discuss political or religious matters. Members must have a
belief in a Supreme Being and a future existence; however, no
invocation, benediction, or prayer shall be delivered which
would have a sectarian significance.”

We may go back even further as to the attitude of our Grand Lodge in
connection with religious practices. As long ago as 1894 as reported in the
Constitution and Ross Digest, California, and which was published in 1943, we
find the following comments:

“That law is older than Grand Lodges, and is a landmark which no man
or body of men has power to change. In recognition of that law we have
declared the rule to be inflexible that this Grand Lodge will not interfere with
religious or political matters or controversies or any kind. Masons may present
all shades of religious and political opinions, but when assembled in Grand or
subordinate Lodge they cannot even discuss them.” This report was adopted at
the Grand Lodge Communication in 1894.

Our present Code provides as previously set forth, that we should not in
any way interfere or voice an opinion with regard to political or religious
matters under our Masonic Code. Under that Section were we to prohibit any
Mason from wearing personal religious articles, apparel or other emblems or
symbols of his faith, then we have voiced an opinion about his religion. We will


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 289

have thus told him that we do not respect his particular practices and this is
against our law.

The proponents of this Resolution seek to, in specific words, define
what constitutes the practice of a religion by a member or a guest of a lodge
under Section 380. We do not believe that it is necessary to do so and to adopt
this Resolution may result in greater acrimony and divisiveness than that which
occurred just two years ago in this Grand Lodge. It makes the interpreter of this
Resolution, if it becomes a part of the Code, a person who has to determine
whether the ornament or article “is required” to be worn “for the reason of
observing his religious beliefs.”

Many people, particularly younger ones, may wear types of garb which
they view as necessary for religious observance. However, mainstream religion
would not view it as such. Each of us then would become an arbiter of everyone
else’s religion and religious faith, Your Committee can see many, many
instances in the present and future whereby the harmony of the lodge could be
disturbed unnecessarily. Any comment about religious apparel would be a
statement about religion and therefore this resolution is not consistent with the
policy and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Report of the Committee on Ritual:

The Ritual Committee has never, never, never objected to the wearing
or the displaying of ornaments, whether they be Masonic, religious, other
fraternal organizations, political parties, commemorative symbols, and a whole
myriad of other items. Our only objection is the wearing of head gear other than
the traditional officiant’s top hat. We recommend a “no” vote.

Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

294 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Organization of Staff

Brother Kelly Wiest (167), served his seventh year as Administrator at
Union City. Brother Dale Ankney (428), served his fifth year as Administrator
at Covina. Brother Jeff H. Eichner, Jr. (686), is Director of Charitable Giving.

Board Meetings

Meetings of the Trustees are held on the first Wednesday of the month,
alternating between the Union City and Covina Homes.

The administrators and committee chairmen of the Board of Trustees
contribute in the preparation of this Annual Report as follows:

Admissions Committee
by Victor Howard, Ph.D., and Ernest A. Pooleon, Co-Chairmen

The Admissions Review Program of the Board is a carefully developed
analysis and evaluation process that continues to be improved and refined to
meet the evolving needs of our Applicants, as well as the Homes Staff and the
Board itself.

Over the past several years experience has confirmed the value of the
required overnight visit to one of the facilities by all applicants in order that
Administrators and Medical Staff can meet with and evaluate them and assist in
determining the most appropriate placement. (The facility visited is not an
indication of where the most appropriate placement might be.) A Staff report on
this overnight visit, together with the Medical Director’s comments on his
review of their medical history and data, is then added to the information sent to
Committee members.

Prior to the regular Board Meeting on the first Wednesday of each
month, the six Trustees who form the Admissions Committee (three from the
North and three from the South) have each received and carefully studied the
applications for Masonic assistance, including both the Staff report and the
Medical Director’s comments. Each Trustee reviews and evaluates the
applications and accompanying data, and then submits his comments and votes
to the two co-chairmen, who discuss the results of the vote by phone, and then
present the results at meetings of the entire Admissions Committee. Homes
Staff Representatives and Administrators attend and assist in this discussion.
The Committee’s recommendations and comments are then presented to the full
Board for final confirmation.

The Masonic Assistance Staff works closely with the Lodges and their
Secretaries to simplify the application process. Lodges no longer are required to
vote at a Stated Meeting to sponsor an application. However, Lodges are
informed of applications by members or widows of their members and can
object if they so wish. Similarly, Lodge representatives are expected to


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 295

accompany Masonic Assistance Staff on their visit to the applicant’s home or
residence.

The number of applications handled by the Committee for couples,
individuals and children continues to vary from month to month. Over the past
several years we have noted a decrease in the number of people applying for
Masonic Assistance, although we still have people on the waiting list, due to the
reconstruction of the Union City Home.

The ongoing reconstruction at the Union City facility continues to
require close control of admissions. However, as room became available in the
Independent Ambulatory and Assisted Living/ Ambulatory sections, admissions
were accepted for these levels of care. And, as space permitted, a number of
couples were admitted where one of the members of the couple required
Assisted Living Non-Ambulatory or Skilled Nursing level of care while the
other member was capable of living at the Independent Living level of care.

Over the past several years, this reconstruction at Union City has
required both Staff and Residents to relocate to other areas of the campus to
allow the work to proceed. This has added to the need for close coordination
between the Administrators and Assistance Staff in monitoring Admissions as
well as overnight visits.

The Non-Resident Assistance program continued to grow over the past
year, to provide necessary support, financial, housing and care management
assistance to applicants who are in need of this assistance in order to remain in
their present communities or present residence. Members of the Staff monitor
these individuals to ensure that their needs are being met.

Over the past year and a half the adult facility at Covina has continued
with the expansion of Assisted Living services, resulting in the doubling of
Assisted Living beds, and new construction is on-going to provide more
efficient support and servicing of Assisted Living residents.

The Covina facility continues to provide hospice care for its terminally
ill residents, thus giving its residents a palliative care option previously only
available through placement in a skilled nursing setting.

The Child Care Program at Covina continues to develop. The
Behavioral Management Model, based upon reinforcement of positive behavior,
and allowing the Covina Home to provide a stable and nurturing environment
for children who so badly need its support, is being implemented successfully.
Due to the continuing success of this program, the Board has approved and
construction has begun on the building of new housing structures to facilitate the
successful implementation of the Model program.

A Web Page has been established: www. MasonicAssistance.Org., that
provides information about Masonic Assistance, activities offered at each
Masonic community, eligibility criteria for applying, and whom to call for more
information.

Facility Services Committee
by Warren T. Buschmann, Chairman

In 1994 the Board of Trustees initiated a long range plan to renovate
five of the major buildings at Union City. This major project updated all five
main buildings seismically to current earthquake standards and enlarged the
residents’ living quarters to meet today’s standards. The Lorber Building,
devoted to skilled nursing, was only 10 years old and was very well constructed
and thus was not included in the current program. For the first time at Union
City the residents will have telephones in their rooms and all rooms will have air
conditioning supplied from a central location.

The first building to be reconstructed, Wollenberg, was completed in
January 1997 and is occupied by some 28 residents who are very pleased with
the modernization. They and all residents are enjoying and greatly benefiting
from the new Grider Health Center which is part of Wollenberg and affords a
therapeutic swimming pool and extensive therapy and exercise equipment. The
second building of the plan was the Adams Building which was completed in
February 1998 and is occupied by some 45 residents. Adams also contains very
updated crafts activity rooms. The North Wing has been completed and serves
36 residents with very greatly improved facilities in a building that was first
constructed in 1923.

The building program for the final two buildings, Administration and
the South Wing, has started and interior demolition of the South Wing is
complete. Reconstruction will be accomplished for both buildings
simultaneously. The South Wing is expected to be ready for occupancy
February 2001 and the Administration Building should be finished July 2001 for
68 and 55 residents respectively. The architect for this project has been The
Ratcliff Architects, Emeryville, California and the contractor has been Tilden-
Coil Constructors, Riverside, California.

At Covina the program to build a two story connector building
including an adjacent elevator to facilitate the Assisted Living function in the
Adult section encountered numerous delays but construction should be starting
by October 1, 1999. Another project underway at Covina is the building of six
to eight new homes for children based on designs and suggestions of Boys Town
that will greatly improve the children’s care program. Design work is complete
and construction should start November 1, 1999 or sooner.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 297

The above describes Board approved projects which this committee
oversees. In addition, this committee annually reviews, approves and monitors
numerous projects proposed by the two Administrators and which are necessary
for the efficient operation of our physical plants.

The Facility Services Committee consists of two other members,
namely, Tom Joy and Roy Anderson. Their very helpful comments and counsel
are gratefully acknowledged.

Long Range Planning Committee
by Frederick Sorsabal, Chairman

The Long Range Planning Committee, comprised of the Chairman;
R.W. David C. Decker; R.W. C. Ray Whitaker; R.W. William Holsinger;
Douglas Scott, CFO; our new Executive Vice President Deborah Stebbins; Kira
Reginato, formerly Director of Masonic Assistance; and the two Administrators
of the Homes — Kelly Wiest and Dale Ankney, have been busy attempting to
implement the results of the Market Analysis Study conducted by
Gerontological Services, Inc. of Santa Monica for the Board of Trustees of the
Masonic Homes.

This survey of 10,000 California Masons and Masonic Widows was
conducted to assist the Board of Trustees in determining the future needs of
potential applicants to the Masonic Homes. This survey and study included the
preference types of accommodations, services, and contractual arrangements
needed in the future along with a survey of the present residents of both homes
to assess their satisfaction with housing, services, and programs currently
provided.

The survey was tremendously helpful to the Committee in making its
recommendations to the Board of Trustees at its’ annual retreat. The most
significant recommendation to the Board was the changing of the “surrender of
assets” policy for admissions to the Homes. The recommendation to allow
several options to eligible applicants was then adopted by Grand Master Alvin J.
Weis as his Decision No. 1 to be presented to Grand Lodge at the Annual
Communication in October, 2000. If this Decision is adopted, it is anticipated
that numerous eligible applicants will apply for admission into the Homes.

The study also made numerous recommendations for operational
changes at the Homes, which should improve the accommodations and services


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 299

rendered to the residents of the Homes. The Long Range Planning Committee
for approval and implementation is studying these recommendations. This study
will be the basis of future work by this Committee.

Additional services offered to Masons and the communities in which
the campuses are located have been studied and placed in abeyance until the
improvements on both campuses are completed which is anticipated during the
year of 2001.

This committee has been working diligently with the City of Union
City to develop a golf course on vacant property owned by the Masonic Homes
of California. While work on the golf course is several years away, the Long
Range Planning Committee needs to insure that the golf course blends in with
the primary mission of the Homes Board, that of providing quality care to
eligible Masons and children. Many options are available and will be studied
prior to any commitments made to develop the course.

The Committee continues to provide to the Board of Trustees those
recommendations which they feel will enhance the marketability of both homes
along with additional services that will improve the quality of life for the
residents of the Homes.

Medical Advisory Committee
by Victor Howard, Ph.D.

During the past year the role of the Medical Advisory Committee has
been modified to provide Homes Management with a pro-active focus and
professional support body, as well as continuing to ensure that Residents at the
Homes continue to receive the highest affective level of health support available.

As a Standing Committee of the Board, its purpose is to serve as a
forum for the discussion of policy and practice pertaining to efficient and
effective resident and patient care. The Committee will make recommendations
to the Board of Trustees on key clinical policies, licensing and accreditation
activities. The Committee shall review management’s systems for monitoring
and evaluating care and shall report to the Board on the appropriateness of
management’s response to clinical and quality improvement activities.

The Committee will meet, at least quarterly, or as called. The
responsibilities of the Medical Advisory Committee of the Board include the
following:

« Evaluate and improve quality of services to patients, residents and
clients of Non-Resident Assistance (NRA) program through the
continuous assessment of the results and effectiveness of quality
improvement programs; assure that systems are in place to review,
evaluate and monitor the quality of care delivered in the Homes.


300

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Review utilization trends in the Homes, including but not limited to
frequency of SNF admissions, acute hospital admissions, and
utilization of diagnostic and therapeutic interventions with the goal of
ensuring that residents receive a level of service that maximizes their
independence as well as cost-effective services.

Review and approve goals and metrics submitted by management for
annual Quality improvement plans and initiatives.

Review available data contrasting quality and utilization trends in the
Masonic Homes with available benchmark data from the industry.

Collaborate with management to identify 4-5 quality indicators each
year to be tracked and reported upon to Board. Sample quality
indicators are listed at the end of the Committee Charge document.

Monitor compliance with appropriate licensure, accreditation and
certification status. Make recommendations to Board regarding
participation in additional regulatory and voluntary accreditation
programs as appropriate.

Monitor results of resident and client satisfaction measurement tools,
including reviewing management’s recommendations for response to
these survey results.

Planned Giving Program
by Jeff H. Eichner, Jr., PM, Director of Charitable Giving

During the year, initial contact (telephone or letter) was made with over
100 Masons and/or their wives and widows regarding bequests, etc. that can
benefit the Masonic Homes in the future as well as themselves now. Assistance
was given to individuals, their attorneys and accountants with wills, charitable
remainder trusts, living trusts, and providing information about various planned
giving options.

Various forms of publicity were used to inform the membership of the
ways to support the Masonic Homes that provide life income and tax benefits:

« A different article on various planned giving subjects was in each issue
of the California Freemason.

= Letters went to all lodges to publicize planned giving options and
informing them that a speaker was available to give presentations on
planned giving.

= Inserts (for lodge bulletins) on planned giving topics and the toll-free
telephone number for more information were provided to all lodges.

= Speeches on planned giving were given to lodges and to High Twelve
Clubs.

"The Masonic Homes of California was publicized in the California
Edition of “The Guide to Gifts and Bequests”, provided free to
attorneys, bank trust officers, accountants, and financial planners.

= A new planned giving brochure was developed and supplemented with
detail fact sheets. They described various giving options, including


302 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

charitable remainder trusts, living trusts, wills, the Pooled Income
Fund, occasional gifts to the Masonic Homes such as Individual
Retirement Accounts, life insurance, savings accounts, and real estate.

Assistance to the brethren and their families included describing tax
advantages and life income plans available through various planned giving
options, answering questions, referrals to attorneys and Certified Public
Accountants, providing information on how planned giving options can fit into
estate plans, and meetings with them and their legal advisors regarding wills,
charitable remainder trusts, living trusts, etc.

The Pooled Income Fund continues to be a viable source of income and
tax benefits to donors to the Masonic Homes. Two new participants joined the
Pooled Income Fund and $70,000 was added to the Fund during the year, while
$764,096 was transferred from the Fund to the Masonic Homes. At year end,
there were 64 participants and a total of $3,147,930 (principal only) in the
Pooled Income Fund.

Policy Committee
by Charles H. Alexander, PGM, Chairman

The role of the Policy Committee has changed somewhat with the
arrival on board of our new Executive Vice-President. I proposed two policy
changes as a result of this new position. In Policy, Chapter III .015, it added
authority to the Executive Vice-President at approve emergency expenses up to
$100,000. Examples of emergency expenses would be items such as a broken
sewer line that would require immediate action for the safety and welfare of the
residents. Likewise, a change to Chapter III, .016, extended the authority of the
Executive Vice President to sign contracts up to $100,000.

In both of these cases it gave the Executive Vice President the authority
to act as needed, with such action being reported to the Board of Trustees as the
next regular meeting.

The Executive Vice President is making a thorough review of the entire
Policy Manual of the Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Homes. As
suggested changes are presented, they will be reviewed by the Policy Committee
and presented to the full Board of Trustees for discussion and adoption.

Real Estate Committee
by Victor Howard, Ph.D., Chairman

The Real Estate Committee of the Masonic Homes of California is
charged with the responsibility of disposing of all real estate and automobiles
made available to the Masonic Homes through asset assignment, wills and
bequests, gifts and grants.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 303

During the past fiscal year, July 1, 1999, through June 30, 2000, we
sold a total of 14 pieces of property for a total of $918,400.00. In addition, we
sold 13 motor vehicles for a total of $77,145.00.

We reduced notes receivable by $234,237.00 during the year, and were
left with 18 notes and $642,280.00 in receivables at fiscal year end.

Due to the terms of the Life Care Contract signed by each new resident
of the Homes, we almost always have an inventory of unsold property. This
allows the new residents a 90-day period to adjust and make up their own minds
that they have no intention of withdrawing from the Masonic Homes. The
resident then signs a release authorizing the sale of their property.

The Real Estate Committee wishes to thank the Staff in San Francisco,
the Administrators of our two Homes, and particularly Brothers Tom McKinney
and Doug Scott, as well as Ms Holly L. Hobson for their continuing assistance
and support in all of these transactions.

Scholarship Committee
by Charles H. Alexander, PGM, Chairman

The Scholarship Committee of the Masonic Homes of California met at
the Covina Home at 10:00AM, May 17, 2000. Five interviews had been
scheduled by Director Barbara TenBroek.

Those present were: Debi Stebbins, Executive Vice President of the
Masonic Homes of California; Dale Ankney, Adminstrator, Covina Home;
Barbara TenBroek, Director of Childrens’ Services, Covina Home; Ernest
Pooleon, Member of Board of Trustees, and member of Scholarship Committee;
Charles Alexander, Chairman of the Schoarship Committee, and member of the
Board of Trustees.

The procedure in each case was to interview the applicant for 15-20
minutes, and then discuss the impression of those present to the interview,
review the requested amount of the applicant, consider the educational progress
and goals of these asking renewal, and evaluate the goals of initial applicants.
In some cases the administration recommended making the grant contingent
upon some conditions and stipulations. The Committee concured in this action
and in some cases recommended additional items. Unfortunately, there seemed
to be, at least under the surface, a feeling on the part of some of the applicants
that the scholarship program was an entitlement, as was the privilege of
continued residence at the Home.

In three of the cases, the Committee had some hard questions of the
applicants, who did not seem to be making adequate progress in their
educational program, and in two of the cases had failed to live up to their
responsibilities in being permitted to continue residence at the Covina Home. In
both of these cases the administration has taken action to terminate their
residency at the Home. Allthough the committee might have been justified in


304 PROCEEDINGS OF THE
witddrawing further support, we instead made grants contingent upon meeting

certain conditions and requirements. We hope such will help instill in the
applicants a stronger sense of responsibility.

President’s Report
by Stanley M. Cazneaux, PGM

Your Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California has
worked diligently to assure that our Homes, located in Union City and Covina,
remain in a leadership role in California Senior Living and Residential Care for
at-risk children, Our commitment remains to provide the very best Living and
Health Care for the residents of our Masonic Homes. In order to remain in this
leadership category the Board of Trustees unanimously agreed to create the
position within the Masonic Homes of California of an Executive Vice President
to oversee all of the strategic functions within our Homes. On February 28,
2000, Deborah E. Stebbins, a well-known leader in the California health care
community, assumed this newly created role. In the short time she has been
with us, she has far surpassed the goals and objectives set by the Board of
Trustees. We look forward to a bright future under her outstanding leadership.

The major earthquake retrofit project at Union City is nearing its
completion, with a projected completion date of April 1, 2001. On that date we
will open 71 new studio and one-bedroom apartments. I want to take this
opportunity to thank the residents of the Union City Home for their patience and
understanding during the long construction period. I am sure they would agree
the beautiful results were well worth waiting for. If you did not have the
opportunity to visit the Union City Home during Grand Lodge Week, please
make plans for you and your lodge to see our beautiful new facilities.

The construction of eight new homes for the children at Covina is
moving along very well with the first occupancy scheduled for late November
2000. Needless to say the children and house parents, as well as the staff at
Covina are excited to see the construction moving so quickly. In addition the
new 10,000 square foot Assisted Living project at Covina is scheduled for a
March 1, 2001 completion date. I would like to thank the Facilities Committee
of the Homes Board, composed of Warren Buschmann, Tom Joy and Roy
Anderson, for their commitment to bring these important construction projects
to a successful conclusion.

Early this year, a survey was mailed to a sample group of 10,000
California Masons and widows over the age of 70. The key finding of the
survey clearly indicated that our membership favored alternative payment
options over the current assignment of assets, which has been in place for over


306 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

one hundred years. It is interesting to note that California is the only Masonic
Home in North America that still requires asset assignment for admission. After
several months of discussion by the Homes Board of Trustees, it was decided
that we should offer the option of Fee-for-Services. This new option will be
available to California Masons, their wives and widows only. Approval of that
action is before this Grand Lodge in the form of Grand Master’s Decision No. 1.
It is important to note that in no event will a potential resident without financial
resources be turned away. We will always be here to serve the members of the
fraternity, their wives and widows who are in need.

On a personal note, I would like to thank our Grand Master, Most
Worshipful Alvin J. Weis, for his advice, counsel and support during this very
busy year. He found time on various occasions to attend our meetings and give
his input to our deliberations. His dedication to our Homes and residents is very
much appreciated by each and every Board member. I also want to personally
thank each Board member for their hard work, commitment, dedication and
excellence in the way they have performed their jobs. This Grand Lodge owes
each of them a debt of gratitude.

Respectfully submitted,
Stanley M. Cazneaux, PGM, President
Warren T. Buschmann, Vice President

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE VICE PRESIDENT
SEPTEMBER 11, 2000

On February 28, 2000 I assumed the newly created position of
Executive Vice President of Masonic Homes of California— at a time when this
organization, which is steeped in a long and rich tradition of service, was
embarking upon the 21% Century. It was in the spirit of readying the
organization for the challenges of the future that led our Board of Trustees to
create my position.

My role is to provide leadership on both the program development and
operational front while supporting the Board in its formulation of strategy and
policy. Over the last six months the Board has made a number of critical
strategic decisions that will influence the future direction and success of the
organization.

In March the Board held a daylong retreat in Covina facilitated by Dan
Cain, a consultant who works with non-profit Boards and myself. Discussion
focused on delineating the role of the Board versus professional management.
The day provided the foundation for improving the effectiveness of Board and
committee meetings.

Major construction projects were underway at both the Union City and
Covina campuses at the time of my arrival. Early next year we will complete
the renovation of the South and Administration buildings in Union City, opening
71 new studio and one bedroom apartments. These are spacious private
apartments with large windows and extraordinary views of the hills and the Bay.
The new units are as, if not more, attractive than any retirement
accommodations being built in new senior communities in Northern California.

Those Masons and their families who visited the Home in years past
will recognize that these apartments will be a dramatic enhancement of our
accommodations. In addition, these two buildings will house a number of
graciously appointed public areas, including numerous lounges, an extensive
library, game rooms, ice cream parlor, gift shop, a carpentry workshop and a
movie room.

At Covina, where our adult program houses almost 70 residents and
Children’s program 38 children and teens, we are completing two major
building projects. Over the next few months we will build 8 new residences
(7500 square feet each) as replacements for the existing children’s residences.
Each house will accommodate up to 7 children (each with his or her own room)
as well as the family specialists who live and care for the children.

Among the many benefits these spacious homes will offer, each group
of children will be able to plan menus, prepare food and dine as a “family” in
their own homes. The first of the children’s homes will be completed by this
Thanksgiving.


308 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Finally at Covina, a 10,000 foot Health Center is under construction
that will provide new clinic, assisted living dining and exercise facilities for our
adult residents on that campus.

At the same time we’re renovating our facilities, we are also enhancing
our services to seniors at both campuses. This includes upgrading the menu and
dining options offered to our residents. In addition, we are adding nursing
assessment services and a wellness program in our assisted living areas to
optimize the health and independence of our residents as long as possible.
These changes, along with others in the future, have been made in response to
feedback and suggestions elicited from our current residents in a written
satisfaction survey conducted earlier this year.

Over the last few months, under the leadership of President Cazneaux,
the Board has deliberated on several strategic questions. The goal of these
discussions has been to ensure that the Homes remain a vital resource for all
California brethren, wives and widows in need. We also concluded that
admission criteria to the Homes must be designed to appeal to a broad spectrum
of the fraternity’s membership.

Recognizing that historically those entering the Home often had
significant financial needs, the Board has reaffirmed our absolute commitment
to accept any eligible person for admission to the Homes regardless of financial
circumstances or ability to pay. Our mission has and always remains focused on
supporting California Brethren in need.

Nevertheless the Board also acknowledged that as the characteristics of
our senior population has changed there are a variety of other types of need
beyond financial. These include the need for physical care, emotional support or
simply being part of a social community, particularly a community based on
shared Masonic values.

It is in the spirit of meeting all these needs -- what we are terming
“fraternal care” -- that the Board has decided our admissions should be
expanded beyond the historical requirement that residents assign or surrender all
their assets upon admission.

As a part of its strategic planning, the Board has also reviewed a
number of trends within the retirement community industry. The good news is
that the demand for retirement living will increase, especially as the population
continues to age, and that retirement living is perceived to be an attractive option
by all age groups. Because of recent stock market performance and real estate
inflation, especially in California, seniors today are more affluent than in the
past. In addition, they are more highly educated, more demanding in their
expectations for high levels of service and amenities, and more interested in
independence and autonomy. The Board concluded that the Masonic Homes of
California needs to prepare as an organization to respond to these demographic
and social trends.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 309

Early this year, a survey was mailed to a sample of 10,000 California
Masons and widows over the age of 70. There was a remarkably high return
rate of 26%. Of those responding, an impressive 80% expressed an interest in
admission to the Homes at some point within a 5-year time frame.

Fully 29% of all Masons in the State are between the ages of 70-79 and
another 26% are over the age of 80. The Homes represent a tremendous asset for
the members of the Craft in these age groups and the survey results provide
important guidance on what the membership wants.

One of the key survey findings was that alternative payment options
were strongly preferred over assignment of assets among future potential
residents. This echoes the overall trends within the retirement community
industry. For the most part, asset assignment has not appealed to members of
the Craft who have substantial assets and income or those whose homes have
substantially appreciated over the last decade. Naturally, many would prefer to
pass those assets along to their children and grandchildren.

Management also reviewed with the Board a telephone survey of about
15 other Masonic Homes throughout the country. With the exception of Florida,
which has adopted a partial assignment of assets policy, all other Homes have
either added fee for service payment options or dropped assignment of assets
altogether. Although some Homes lack the generous endowment funds
available to the Craft in California that enables us to provide substantial
subsidization for many residents, all Homes report that the blending of residents
with different financial means has proved very harmonious.

As a result, the Board contemplates adding options including an entry
fee, monthly rental fees and partial asset assignment in the near future. It is
important to reiterate that in no event will a potential resident without financial
resources be turned away from the Homes. Instead, revenue generated from
entry and monthly fees will allow us to extend our financial support to more
members of the fraternity, their wives and widows. These changes will be
finalized after a review of the topic as a decision of the Grand Master at the
October Annual Communication.

Other findings from the survey may point to the need for additional
campuses in other parts of the State as well as larger accommodations such as
two bedroom apartments and townhouses. These possibilities will be addressed
in the development of a three-year strategic plan early next year.

Finally, I have presented a document outlining key management goals
and objectives for Fiscal Year ending June 30, 2001. The goals, which were
approved by the Board of Trustees this summer, are divided into four sections:

I. Quality Improvement


310

II.

Ill.

Iv.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In addition to the initiatives to improve dining and menu
selection and “aging in place” philosophy on both campuses,
we are instituting a formal continuous quality improvement
process in our skilled nursing facility, focusing on formal
tracking of fall prevention, skin care and proper hydration.
We also have a goal of streamlining and expediting our
admissions process, enabling quicker response for applicants
who have made the important decision to seek retirement
accommodations.

Strategic Performance and Program Development

We will be completing a three and five year strategic plan for
the facilities during the next fiscal year including the
development of campus master facility plans. A marketing
plan is being implemented under the direction of our new
Director of Marketing, Hallie Holtzman, that will be designed
to achieve close to full occupancy of both adult facilities by
June, 2001.

We also will be giving careful consideration of the growth and
direction of our children’s program in Covina as a part of that
strategic plan.

Organization

In addition to the updating of Board policies and Committee
structure as a follow-up to our March Board retreat, we will be
conducting a board development and educational program.
We are establishing a parallel organizational development
program for managers at the Homes and Masonic Outreach
Program (formerly known as Non Residence Assistance) to
enhance their management skills and build an effective
leadership team.

Performance and Cost Improvements

While our operational costs per patient day at Covina are
fairly consistent with industry standards, especially given that
we provide our residents with a number of benefits not offered
in other retirement communities (e.g. monthly stipends), at
Union City our costs are high. In part this is due to a gradual
drop in census by almost 100 residents at Union City over the
last five years without a proportional drop in fixed costs.
Through a variety of initiatives we are attempting to reduce


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 311

our annualized operating expenses over the remainder of the
fiscal year by $1.5 million. This represents a modest five (5)
percent reduction in costs; we feel it can be achieved with no
adverse impact but instead simultaneous enhancement in the
quality of our care and services.

In summary, we have a number of exciting enhancements underway at
both our campuses, which promise to make the Homes comparable to the very
best retirement communities. Our goal is to continue to make the Homes a
wonderful resource for all Masons, their wives and widows.

Respectfully submitted,
Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President


312 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE ADMINISTRATOR
MASONIC HoME, UNION CITY
1999 — 2000

In April 2000, our great fraternity celebrated its Sesquicentennial year
of FreeMasonry here in California. While many events were held in honor of
this 150 year milestone, the Masonic Home for Adults entered its 102" year of
continuing to provide excellent care to our retired Masons, their wives and
widows.

During this past year the major renovation of several buildings
continued. In September of 1999, Residents from the Administration Building
were relocated into the newly renovated North Wing, consisting of 36 Assisted
Living studio apartments. Environmental and Facility Services offices were
moved to the basement as well as our PBX Communication Center, which
houses our 24-hour switchboard operator, emergency pull cords, apartment
intercoms and master fire alarm notification equipment. This major and vital
undertaking was required in order to begin the demolition of the Administration
building.

At the beginning of this fiscal year the Trustees made the decision to
accelerate the renovation process on our campus by doing the internal
demolition work on both the Administration and South Wing together.
Fortunately, we were able to relocate the South Wing residents into available
Acacia Apartments and other available buildings on our campus.

The original completion date to re-occupy these two buildings was July
2001. As of this writing, however, it now appears that we will be able to move
into these newly renovated 71 apartments by January of 2001. Current Acacia
residents will be given priority status to move first, as they currently have no air
conditioning.

The Ratcliff Architects of Emeryville, California and Tilden Coil
Constructors, Inc. of Riverside, California are to be commended for their work
on these three original red brick buildings. John Kariotis of Kariotis &
Associates, Sierra Madre, California, is also to be commended for his
coordination and design of the major earthquake retrofitting work of these three
buildings, plus the Wollenberg and Adams renovations.

In addition to relocating Residents this past year, several staff offices
were moved including my own, our Assistant Administrator, Personnel,
Business Services and Social Services. Our Environmental Services Director,
Mike Heaviside is to be complimented for his excellent coordination of all
Resident and employee moves. Diana Vera, my Administrative Assistant, did
an outstanding job of chairing the Resident Relocation Committee.

During the year our MIS Department, under the capable Leadership of
Elysia Everett, conducted an extensive computer education program for most of


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 313

our employee population. Employees were trained in three groups - Level 1
(Beginning), Level 2 (Intermediate) and Level 3 (Advanced). As our entire
campus is now computerized, the Trustees were very supportive of this program.

In April 2000 the residents and staff were excited to be part of a new
Endowment Board video coordinated by the new chairmen, Richard Pumerantz.
This video showcases both our Union City Home and the Covina Home. It
contains some marvelous shots of our Grider Health Center, newly renovated
North Wing, resident’s computer lab and our ever popular ceramics program.
The title of this video is “Champions of the Industry, Masonic Homes of
California” was a Pat Summerall Production.

Notable resident achievements this year included the birthdays of:

Lily Wilkinson Age 109 Charity Jasman No. 362
Edward Jesse Lacy Age 109 San Fernando No. 343
Eleanor Webb Age 102 Marin No. 191
Adolph Lehman Age 102 Stanislaus No. 206

Several married couples celebrated anniversary milestones:

Lucius & Edith French 74 years Winnedumah No. 287
Carl & Mabel Oxe 68 years East San Diego No. 561
Serafin & Araminda Rensen 65 years Old West No. 813
Will & Sarah Conner 63 years San Marino No. 588

Virgil & Maxine Land 60 years Santa Cruz-San Lorenzo No. 38

Arthur & Betty Ashfield 56 years San Diego No. 35
Howard & Gladys Lewis 54 years Table Mountain No. 124
Frank & Carol Albro 51 years Glendale No. 368
George & Eleanor Petri 48 years DeAnza No. 511

As of June 30, 2000, we had 212 residents; their averaging age was 88
years of age. Our population consisted of 45 Masons and 167 spouses/widows,
including 20 couples.

Once again we are grateful to the many Masonic affiliated groups who
have assisted the Home in various ways. During the past year we were visited
by 41 Masonic Lodges, 12 Eastern Star Chapters, 24 Assemblies of Rainbow
Girls, 18 Bethels of Job Daughters and 3 Demolay Chapters.

Special appreciation is once again extended to the following
Lodges/Associations who hosted special events throughout the year:


314 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

East Bay Past Matrons’ Association 86" Annual Jam & Jelly Day
Alameda Lodge No. 167, Fremont Octoberfest
Crow Canyon Lodge No. 155, Castro Valley Strawberry Festival
Acacia Lodge No. 243, Hayward Steak Barbecue
Fremont Lodge No. 825, Fremont Summer Picnic

Hopefully this report of the past year’s events gives you a flavor of
what life is like at this great Masonic Home. However, to really feel and see the
brotherly love that goes on daily, I invite all brothers and their families to stop
by and visit.

In closing, I wish to once again express my gratitude to our dedicated,
caring employees who serve our residents, 24 hours per day, 365 days per year,
as well as the members of the Board of Trustees for the seemingly endless hours
of concentrated time they spend on behalf of our Home.

A special “Thank you” for the generous support and hard work of our
Endowment Board members. Their work exemplifies all of the donations of
both money and material items that are contributed to the Home.

As residents and employees of this great Masonic Home, we are
“thankful to all California Masons” for their contributions which make this
Masonic Home for Adults a great place to live and work.

Respectfully submitted,
Kelly Wiest, Administrator

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 321

REPORT OF THE ADMINISTRATOR

MASONIC HoMEs, COVINA
1999-2000

Once again it is my great pleasure to provide the Fraternity with this
annual report that describes the many developments that have taken place at our
beautiful Covina Home during the first year of the new millennium. And if you
thought the fireworks that brought in the New Year were spectacular, by
comparison, you will be in awe of what has taken place at the Home over the
past year.

After much planning and just plain hard work, we embarked on one of
the most ambitious building programs ever undertaken at Covina. The ultimate
result of this physical re-structuring will be the establishment of some of the
finest communities of their kind inthe nation. Furthermore, these communities
have been designed to serve not only those who live here today, but future
generations as well, whose needs will most assuredly be different than those
who now call this place home. In other words, because of your continuing
support, we just seem to keep getting better, and I am most anxious to provide
you with the details.

Before getting into the details of the children’s building project, I think
it is important to bring you up to date on the implementation of our Family
Home Model of Care, the childcare program initially developed at Boys Town
in Omaha, Nebraska, and for the last four years the program employed at your
Covina Home. Last year I reported that the final piece of the program was being
set in place, that being the establishment of an educational group on campus that
would provide all future training of staff in the Family Home Model of Care. I
am pleased to report that this educational group, which consists of a
Trainer/Evaluator and Program Coordinator, performed exceptionally
throughout the year and has assumed virtually all educational responsibilities of
staff. We continue to maintain a relationship with the Boys Town organization,
but their role is now that of advisor, not educator. The transition has worked
well for us, and the success of our efforts is well regarded by the Boys Town
organization. In fact, the Boys Town professionals are so pleased with the
implementation of our program; they have indicated we most assuredly will be
granted certification status by the end of 2000. A rare honor indeed, and a
tremendous compliment to the efforts of those employed within our childcare
program.

Another important accomplishment of the past year was the successful
implementation of our “Academics Success 2000” program. If you recall, our
“Academics Success 2000” program was designed by our childcare staff to
address the special academic needs of the children who live at the Masonic
Home. Unfortunately, because the family environments from which our
children come rarely include an appreciation for academic success, our children
often find themselves unable to successfully compete in the classroom. Our
goal this past year has been to provide our children with all means necessary to


322 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

overcome their academic shortcomings so they can realize their fullest potential.
Again, I am happy to report that real progress was made in meeting our goal.
Grades for those who have been with us for several years improved, and for
those who were admitted during the year, they too have begun to enjoy greater
academic success. Of equal importance is the improved relationship we now
enjoy with our local schools and our children’s teachers. Educators have
become better informed about the type of child we have in residence and
resultantly are better prepared to provide individualized instruction. Our
constant interaction with the academic community has demonstrated that we are
totally committed to our children’s success. We are convinced that as we move
forward into the future, this continued commitment will result in even more
success for those under our care.

Finally, and perhaps the most exciting development over the past year,
was the physical re-development of our childcare community. As of this
writing, the first 3 new houses are scheduled for completion by the end of the
calendar year. This is the first phase of a total re-development project that will
ultimately result in the construction of 8 new childcare houses. These houses
will replace all but four of the existing cottages wherein we currently provide
care. Functionally however, all future care of our children will be provided in
the new houses, and the existing four cottages will be utilized to provide housing
to visiting teachers who participate in the Masonic Foundation’s “Masonic
Student Assistance Program”, a program designed to educate our public school
teachers about at-risk children.

Our new houses have been specifically designed to permit the full
implementation of the Family Home Model of Care referred to earlier in this
report. The design and number of houses will allow us to serve more children
than has been possible in the past. Depending upon the number of children we
elect to have live in each home, our total capacity will vary somewhere between
48 and 56 children. This new capacity is important given the changing nature of
out-of-home placement and the outstanding reputation the Masonic Home for
Children enjoys within the social services community. Because of these factors
and the resulting demand for our services, we fully anticipate all 8 houses will
be filled within 1 year of completion. In other words, in very short order we will
be maximizing our contribution to the Fraternity and the community within
which we live. More importantly, many future generations of abandoned,
neglected or abused children will now have a real chance to experience the
happiness every child deserves.

During the past year, building expansion was the theme on the adult
side of the Covina Home as well. Last year I reported to you that the Board had
approved the funding for a new healthcare center to extend our capability of
providing assisted living care. That building commenced earlier this calendar
year and is scheduled for completion by the end of the first quarter of 2001.
What will this mean for our Home? It means that our residents will have the
comfort of knowing that we have done everything possible to allow them to age


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 323

in place while receiving all of the services a community like ours is licensed to
provide. It will also mean that our assisted living residents will have every
amenity they previously enjoyed as independent living residents.

A new dining room overlooking our back -property will be on the
second floor of this beautiful new building. In addition, a large activity room
with an adjacent kitchen will be on the lower level. This activity room is walled
on one side in glass and has automatic sliding doors that will permit easy access
to a multi-purpose outside activities area. Shuffleboard courts will be built into
the decking and covered to allow for use during the hot summer afternoons. The
rest of the outside decking will be coated in a space age product that facilitates
the dissipation of heat from the sun and consequently provides comfort to those
who choose to sit at the many umbrella-covered tables on our new patio.

For those of you who have made it a practice to visit us during the
summer months, you will be glad to know that we are re-establishing our
barbecue facilities in the new activities area. Gas fired grills and griddles will
be built into the walls of the new area and a washbasin to permit easy clean up
will be built in as well. The most important aspect of our new barbecue area is
the fact it will be easily accessible by our seniors, all of whom enjoy
participating in lodge barbecues conducted at our Home. I encourage all lodges
to make their reservations early for the 2001 summer season as we expect the
schedule to fill rather quickly. It’s a great chance for your lodge to have a
wonderful “away from home” event, and you will not have a better opportunity
to see the many changes your Home has undergone.

Another important development on the senior side of our Home this
past year was our continuing outreach to the greater community. What we have
learned is that our residents’ lives are enhanced by inviting outside groups to our
Home to interact and participate with our residents. As a result, we continued to
serve as a satellite campus for Mt. San Antonio Community College,and many
seniors in the nearby communities learned more about our Fraternity and the
gentle nature of those who live here. We have also expanded our interaction
with other important organizations in our community including 2 Rotary Clubs
and our local Chamber of Commerce. We now regularly host Chamber of
Commerce mixers and Rotary Club events that often include our residents who
were members of such organizations before entering the Home. Again, it’s an
opportunity for our residents to enhance the quality of their lives while exposing
those outside the Home to the goodness of our Fraternity.

Over the coming year it is our aim to expand our services even further
and to be more involved in the community around us. Whether it be serving as a
site for an important community event such as a hospice fundraiser, or simply
serving as a polling site for local, state and national elections, we are determined
to make our presence known, while at the same time enhancing the quality of
those we serve. Speaking on behalf of all those who work and live at the
Masonic Homes at Covina, I extend to you our sincere gratitude for your
support. Together we have made the Masonic Homes one of the finest


324 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

organizations of its kind in America, and together we will make it even more of
a wonder in the future.

Respectfully submitted
Dale Ankney, Administrator

330 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

Most Worshipful, Grand Lodge Officers, Past Grand Masters, Past
Grand Officers, Distinguished Visitors, Members of the Grand Lodge and my
Brethren.

It is with great pride that I stand before you as the elected President of
the Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation for the third year,
and I extend my thanks to each of them for their confidence in me, for their
dedicated service to our Fraternity and to each of you for your continued
support.

The Board meets quarterly, twice in the Covina Home and twice in the
Grand Lodge Office in order to fulfill the mission of the Foundation: To support
the youth of our country as they strive to become productive citizens.

My Brethren, because you believe in this great Fraternity and have
supported the activities of the California Masonic Foundation, we have made it
possible for teachers to do the job for which they were trained. We have given
them the tools to identify students at risk and the skills to provide the necessary
interventions for successful learning experiences. This year 3,260 students were
referred to a student assistance team trained in Covina through the MSAP
Program. Two thousand eighty-three of those referred received some type of
intervention. During the past five years of our program we have trained 2,410
educators in 576 different schools or districts, and they have received 16,848
referrals for assistance. Special help was provided to 6,629 students. We thank
you for your generous support of our program. Special thanks is given to David
Azcarraga who completed a term of eight years on our Board. We wish you
much success in your future endeavors.

The Foundation has made the following expenditures this year:

1. $10,000 to assist the National Masonic Foundation in the expansion of
the Masonic Student Assistance Program throughout the United States,
and we are proud of M..W.. William F. Stovall who is President of the
National Board.

2. $2,500.00 to each of the four youth groups for use in their leadership
training which they could not provide without our support.

3. Certificates of Recognition and $1000.00 checks to each ‘of the five
“California Teachers of the Year” to be used in their respective school
districts as they wish. These presentations were made in the teacher’s
own community thereby affording us the best public relations program


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 331

possible. Each one was awarded by a member of the Foundation Board
of Trustees along with the Grand Master or Deputy Grand Master with
the intent that awareness of our Fraternity’s involvement in Public
Schools through the Masonic Student Assistance Program and our
Scholarship Program could be made to the local community leaders and
the press.

$1,000.00 checks and certificates to each school hosting a Cornerstone
Ceremony. These were presented to the principals of the schools to be
used for the improvement of the schools’ programs.

As you are all aware, the cost of a college or university education
continues to increase and many more students would not be able to
attend without financial assistance. I am pleased to report your
Foundation presented $2,500.00 each to 134 young adults upon
graduation from high school in their pursuit of knowledge, which is an
increase of 37 this year. In addition, 15 C.E. Towne Scholarships of a
little more than $11,000.00 were presented this year. The total value of
this year’s awards was $1.5 million. This was due to the wise
investments by Tom Holm, Grand Treasurer.

The MSAP Program continues to be fully subscribed again this year.
We increased the number of classes for the 1999-2000 school year to
12 with the number of booster classes from two to five. This has been
made possible by the generosity of our Masonic Homes of California

sincere thanks to Dale Ankney and his staff, and Kelly Wiest and his

are grateful for the hours spent by the members of the Public Schools

with teams in their areas. They have been very instrumental in public
relations in their communities. I invite you to visit our display on “EX”
Floor and read some of the responses from the school teams. It will
help you realize the value of your contributions to insure the success of
the educational system of California.

The greatest public relations outreach of Masons in California
continues to be the Child ID Program. This year the Foundation
expanded its support of “KIDS” or Child ID Program. Additional
computer systems were purchased thanks to generous contributions
received from individuals, the Bush Foundation and constituent lodges.
In addition, all expenses incurred in the use of the equipment has been
paid by the Foundation. Be sure to see the program in operation on
“EX” Floor.
