GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 333

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

As it was last year, this annual report of the Trustees of the California
Masonic Memorial Temple (CMMT) is presented as an encapsulated and very
general report of the activities relative to the maintenance and preservation of
the beautiful edifice which houses our Grand Lodge and its administrators.
More detailed information is recorded in the quarterly minutes of the CMMT
Board of Trustees, available through the Grand Secretary.

Perhaps what should be stated clearly before going into detail is that
our California Masonic Center (which designation is now being used for public
relations and commercial purposes) is in extremely good repair and its finances
are very healthy. The proper management of it and its assets continues to ensure
its continued well being without being a burden in any way to the general
membership of California Masonry.

Although it is estimated that the building in general should not present
any major problems for at least the next few decades it is imperative that
constant attention be paid to those parts of it which can and do wear out through
time and usage. These concerns are addressed consistently with an eye toward
being prepared for the inevitable malfunctions which necessitate repair or
replacement. Long range planning and scheduled maintenance act as our
insurance policies in this area.

The physical repairs, replacements and/or changes which have occurred
or are underway this year include, but are not limited to, a required updating of
our life safety/fire alarm system, new security cameras in our parking garage,
new speakers in the auditorium to keep us abreast of technology in that area and
new air conditioning coolers and chillers in the building.

A great deal of the credit for the financial well being of the CMMT is
the superb business acumen of the Nob Hill Masonic Management (NHMM)
Board, presided over by Past Grand Master Warren Blomseth. That board
oversees the business of renting our auditorium and parking garage, as well as
seeing to those parts of building maintenance which falls under their
responsibility. It was a stroke of genius when Grand Lodge instituted this board
a few years ago. We, the CMMT Board, have developed a wonderful mutually
beneficial working relationship with NHMM, and perhaps the most important
priority of the current and future members of the board would be to see this
relationship flourish. To that end we have modified our lease agreement with
NHMM this year which greatly enhances their ability to manage their portion of
this property to the benefit of all concerned.

The Grand Treasurer, Thomas C. Holm, who is also the Treasurer of
the CMMT Board, has done a magnificent job of overseeing our long-term


334 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

financial investments. His ability to make wise decisions and recommendations
based on input from top flight professionals in the field of finance, and then
communicate the rational for them to the trustees in terms we can understand
has made him invaluable to a board such as ours. We will truly miss his
guidance and expertise as he journeys into his retirement, and we wish him all
the best.

Two others who are invaluable to the board are John L. Cooper, Grand
Secretary, and Douglas A. Scott, Chief Financial Officer. They keep us on track
with real life input on what is happening and needs to happen daily in the
operation of this enterprise.

Our grand jurisdiction’s historic sites, buildings and cemeteries are also
under the oversight of this board. Recognizing the need for enhanced attention
to these areas of responsibility we have a sub-committee developing policies and
procedures for the proper management of these assets and monuments to
Masonry.

The ultimate usage and staffing of our new library-museum continues
to be elusive. To assist us in achieving the best possible results from this
beautiful asset we have engaged the services of top professionals on this subject,
Museum Management Consultants, Inc. They are at this time conducting
interviews of prominent and forward thinking masons from all around the
jurisdiction with the goal of identifying and pursuing the library-museum’s
highest and best use.

The CMMT Board is composed of dedicated Masons who have been
selected for their professional expertise, business acumen and leadership
qualities. They are the caliber of men who in the business world would
command high compensation for guiding an enterprise such as this. The Grand
Lodge and California Masonry in general are indeed fortunate to be able to call
upon them . . . and they work as volunteers! As President I am humbled to be
able to work with Brothers such as these.

This year we lost one of these fine Brothers. The death of Trustee L.
Albert Wheeler, III left a vacancy in our brotherhood as well as on the CMMT
Board. He was a fine and exemplary man and Mason, and we shall miss him.

Brother Ronald A. Sherod, PGM, was appointed to the Board by the
Grand Master to fill the vacancy left by the death of Brother Wheeler. We
welcome this great Mason aboard.

Respectfully submitted,
Lowell F. Daniels, President
Sean N. Foran, Vice President

Tan Atkinson J. Lloyd Clayton
Ronald A. Sherod, PGM C. Ray Whitaker
Robert J. Winston Henry W. Coil, Advisor

G. Thomas Melugin, Advisor


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 335

REPORT OF THE DIRECTORS
NOB HILL MASONIC MANAGEMENT, INC.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:

As President, and on behalf of the Board of Directors, I am pleased to
present the eighth annual report for Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. (Nob
Hill).

Briefly stated Nob Hill was established by this Grand Lodge to operate,
for-profit the California Masonic Center’s auditorium, parking garage, exhibit
hall, conference rooms and various rental properties under terms of a lease with
the California Masonic Memorial Temple (CMMT), a non-profit 501(c)(3)
corporation, the owners of this Masonic Center.

Today’s report is for the fiscal year July 1, 1999 to June 30, 2000. Full,
complete, detailed and audited Financial Statements are available for your
review and consideration in the Annual Proceedings.

It is, however, a pleasure to confirm that by every possible measure this
past year was the most successful year in Nob Hill’s eight year history. As a
result of outstanding financial performance, we were able to contribute nearly
$700,000 either to CMMT, the owners of this building, or to the Grand Lodge,
our sole stockholder, in the form of lease payments, special projects, fair-wear-
and-tear project funding and dividends.

With regard to dividends, every effort will be made to increase Grand
Lodge’s annual dividend in 2001 from the current level of $60,000 to $90,000.
The actual amount of a dividend, if any, will be determined by Nob Hill’s net
profit after taxes. In addition, Nob Hill has a corporate mandate that prior to
dividend consideration it must satisfy a three-fold obligation: To maintain this
Memorial facility in first class condition; to invest in operating equipment as
required to remain competitive; and to build cash reserves sufficient to ensure
continuity during unfavorable economic cycles. Obviously, continued
operational and financial success is required to increase dividends. That
objective is now and always has been, my personal and the Board’s ultimate
goal.

You may recall from last year’s report, that CMMT and Nob Hill have
undertaken and completed extensive replacement, remodeling, renovation and
repair work. This year Nob Hill will undertake a major restoration project of
special interest to the membership of this Grand Lodge. We are justifiably
excited and know you will share in our excitement.

As background, this Memorial building was first planned in 1947,
completed at a cost of $6,625,000, and dedicated as a part of the 109" Annual
Communication on Monday, September 29, 1958, almost forty-two years ago to
the day.


336 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The focal point of the 48 foot high main lobby, or Great Hall, is a 38
foot high by 48 foot wide, state-of-the-art, classic, one of kind glass and plastic
artistic masterpiece. The artist, Emile Norman, designed and constructed the
mural (properly called an “Endo-Mosaic”) of 45 acrylic panels measuring 5 foot
by 7 foot. This “Endo-Mosaic” masterpiece, depicts, in a unique artistic form,
the significant impact of Freemasonry upon Western Culture. “Endo-Mosaic” is
a term which is applied to an artistic process in which different types of
materials are sandwiched between sheets of plastic.

As a footnote - three or four years ago CMMT contacted Emile
Norman regarding his thoughts and recommendations concerning the restoration
work then being considered. He was surprised to learn of our plans to restore
his work. And he surprised us by reminding us that he had originally been
commissioned in 1956 to design an “Endo-Mosaic” mural with an expected life
span of 40 to 50 years. And that was, indeed, my brethren the design criteria for
this entire facility.

Now in the year 2000, we confidently expect this Masonic Center to
serve us for another 40 or 50 years. I am well aware that doubling this Masonic
Center’s life span was in large part due to the employees who have made this
building their career and their legacy. We acknowledge their dedication and we
are thankful that they continue that tradition of service year-in and year-out.

To reinforce the employees efforts and to further ensure the Masonic
Center’s longevity, CMMT and Nob Hill have collectively spent millions of
dollars over the past three or four years on renovating, remodeling and
upgrading various components of this building. The latest of these projects was
a $1.3 million dollar expenditure by CMMT to install three new chillers, a new
cooling tower and related components. This project is being completed as I
speak.

Recently we collectively determined that it was now time to undertake
the preservation and restoration of the “Endo-Mosaic” mural. Following
negotiations, Nob Hill entered into a contract with Architectural Resources
Group (located here in San Francisco) to act as project managers to design and
oversee the conservation and restoration of the mural and to redesign, modify
and re-build the existing protective windscreen to minimize exposure to sun,
wind and rain.

Because of the importance of the mural to our Grand Lodge, allow me
to take a minute or two to briefly summarize what is entailed in the preservation
and restoration work. As I do so, please be aware that this artistic, precise and
demanding undertaking requires overseers and master craftsmen of the highest
standards. Their work brings to mind a similar undertaking by the overseers and
master craftsmen employed by King Solomon to decorate and enhance his
Temple.

One of the features of an “Endo-Mosaic” is the wide variety of small
pieces of materials which make up the design of each of the 45 individual


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 337

panels. The material used within each panel, includes glass, tile, marble,
parchment, felt, linen, silk, seashells and vegetable matter. Some of these
materials have slipped or fallen within the panels and some have moved against
the next design element. The exact procedures for repair will be evaluated and
determined during the early part of the preservation and restoration project. One
approach under consideration is to drill a small hole in the side of each panel,
insert a piece of metal and use magnets to move materials back into their exact
original configuration and then inject epoxy to hold the materials in place. This
is a challenging and exciting project with numerous unknowns and problems to
be solved. Although we are just now getting underway, we have established an
estimated cost of $200,000 and expect completion during the first half of 2001.

Because of the high level of interest in this project, we asked
Architectural Resources Groups’ David P. Wessel, Principle Architect for the
project, to prepare an exhibit to provide an overview of the work. Please visit
this exhibit in the main lobby. It will give you a greater appreciation for the
procedures to be used and the challenges to be solved to fully restore this rare
and irreplaceable work of art.

Our magnificent “Endo-Mosaic” mural, has been “center stage” at the
Masonic Center for well over 40 years and continues to be a vital part of the
history and tradition of this Grand Lodge. We invite you to spend some time in
the main lobby of your Masonic Center enjoying this unique Masterpiece and
sharing our excitement in the challenge of the preservation and restoration
project.

Finally, on a personal note, I ask that each of you my brethren, take a
moment or two to greet and thank the Nob Hill Staff. The Nob Hill Staff of ten
(including Chris Carpenter our new General Manager) will appreciate your
recognition of their commitment, dedication and involvement in every aspect of
this Masonic Center’s operation and maintenance. They serve us well.

The Officers and Directors of Nob Hill wish to thank our Grand
Master, Alvin J. Weis, for the opportunity to serve. And Most Worshipful Sir,
we would take this opportunity to acknowledge the Junior Grand Warden, M.
William Holsinger, the Grand Treasurer, Thomas C. Holm, the Grand Secretary,
John L. Cooper III, the Chief Financial Officer Douglas A. Scott and Worshipful
J. Lloyd Clayton, Recording Secretary for their individual and collective
contributions to our success. And a final thank you, to you, my brethren, our
stockholders for your continued confidence and support.

Respectfully submitted,
Warren J. Blomseth, PGM, President
Donald B. McClary, Vice President
Verl B. Lobb W. Bruce Pruitt
Garrett S. Chan, Consultant M. William Holsinger, JGW

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON CHILD ID PROGRAM

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

The Child ID Program was instituted in April 1998 by the Grand Lodge
of California and is financially supported by the California Masonic Foundation.
Three systems were acquired in 1998, three in 1999, and five in 2000. Since the
program was started over 100,000 ID Sheets have been provided to parents and
guardians. The committee’s responsibility is to schedule and support local
Lodges who wish to participate at local county fairs, street fairs, festivals, malls
and other events. The committee also maintains the equipment.

In addition to the Grand Lodge equipment the following Lodges have
also purchased complete systems for use in their areas.

Live Oak Lodge No. 61
Liberty Lodge No. 299
Porterville Lindsay Lodge No. 303
Alhambra Lodge No. 322
Vista Lodge No. 687

This program has been extremely well received by the public and the
participating Lodges. Members of the fraternity are very enthusiastic about the
program because it provides them with the opportunity to become known in
their communities and they receive positive feedback from parents about the
good work they are doing for their communities.

A special thanks to the following who have so generously provided
funds in 2000 to the California Masonic Foundation to acquire five additional
systems.

Los Angles Masonic Service Bureau
Ionic Lodge No. 520
Washington Lodge No. 20
Mark Pressey

The following is a summary of the activity for the year ended August
31, 2000.

Volunteer Hours 12,400

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON FRIEND-TO-FRIEND

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

During the 2000 Masonic Year your Committee on Friend-to-Friend
concentrated on reorganizing and revitalizing the Friend-to-Friend Program. In
order to adequately cover the whole jurisdiction more committeemen were
required. A Vice-Chairman was assigned to coordinate and direct the activities
for each Division. An organization was established which would assign a
committeeman for every four Districts. Candidates for committeemen were
individuals who would aggressively pursue the implementation and management
of an effective Friend-to-Friend Program within their assigned area.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 345

A Friend-to-Friend Executive Committee consisting of the Chairman
and the Division Vice-Chairmen was formed to establish policy and procedures
for the execution of the Program. The Executive Committee met three times
during the year. Activities of the Executive Committee first focused on the
recruitment of committee members. The need for a revised Friend-to-Friend
Instruction Booklet was recognized and two of the members accepted the task of
revising the present booklet. This Booklet should be available soon after Grand
Lodge. The need for an indoctrination program for new committee members
was identified. The indoctrination program is scheduled for implementation
soon after Grand Lodge.

The Committee made several presentations at the Officers’
Management Workshops, Inspectors’ Seminars, District Schools of Instruction,
and at several Lodges. All were very well received.

The most significant activity of the committee took place at mid-year,
when we assumed the responsibility of following-up on requests for information
received by the Grand Lodge Web Site. We anticipate that this activity will
increase in the future.

Respectfully submitted,
Paul E. McCleary, Chairman
Bruce H. Abanathie Bruce O. Carmitchel
Ronald C. Perry Jay V. Smith

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON INVESTMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

To fulfill our responsibilities in accordance with the Grand Lodge
Investment Policy and Philosophy guidelines, the Investment Committee meets
quarterly with our Investment Consultant and Investment Managers. We review
their respective performances in conjunction with their stated investment
objective pertaining to their individual asset classes. This timely review enables
us to determine if they are meeting, exceeding or falling behind our investment
performance expectations.

The changes your Investment Committee has implemented in recent
years, with the guidance of our investment Consultant, have proven their worth
in the improving performance for our various portfolios. Our widely diversified
asset classes are now more in compliance with traditional investment guidelines
for large non-profit organizations.

The market value of the total portfolio on August 31, 2000 was
$686,264,925 compared to $621,376,965 a year earlier, an increase of 10.45%.


346 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Attachments that follow will provide you with a complete performance
summary from a manager’s inception by quarter and years, compared with the
various major indexes.

It is important to remember that these assets have been accumulated
over these many years through the contributions, generous bequests and
continuing efforts of Masons and their many friends. Your Investment
Committee has through their efforts; discipline and dedication endeavored to
increase these assets so as to meet the future needs of our Homes. The
establishment of common goals and the mutual cooperation of the Masonic
Homes Board and the Grand Lodge Investment Committee are evident in the
successful performance we have enjoyed in recent years.

Since I will be leaving this Committee at the conclusion of this Grand
Lodge Year I want to extend my eternal gratitude, to the Investment Committee
and the Masonic Homes Board. Also, to our Grand Treasurer, Thomas C. Holm,
Chief Financial Officer, Douglas A. Scott, and Masonic Homes Board President,
Past Grand Master Stanley M. Cazneaux, for their valuable assistance to me in
the performance of my responsibilities.

A very capable member of this committee has accepted the
appointment as Chairman for the ensuing year, Russell E. Charvonia, and I wish
him the very best in this new position.

Respectfully submitted,
Joseph W. R. Ashwill, Chairman

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 349

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON JURISPRUDENCE

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to
carry out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it
comments on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and
on the legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolutions. After the
Annual Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code
to reflect the legislation which was adopted.

In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Weis’s decisions and
dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion. In the
Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 15th of
this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents, and the legislation
contained in Grand Master Weis’s recommendations which he will present in his
message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that each piece of this
legislation has the legal effect set forth in it.

The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 15th of this year
and the legislation contained in Grand Master Weis’s recommendations require
no separate motion because they are automatically before this Annual
Communication. The presiding officer will announce the vote required for
passage of each item before discussion begins. Any legislation which does not
amend the California Masonic Code or the Ritual requires a simple majority
only for adoption. Any amendment to the California Masonic Code or the
Ritual must be adopted by a five-sixths affirmative vote. If the amendment
receives a simple majority but less than a five-sixths affirmative vote, it carries
over to the next Annual Communication when only a two-thirds affirmative vote
is required for adoption.

We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity which he has given us
to serve this year.

Respectfully submitted,
R. Stephen Doan, P.G.M., Chairman
Richard S. Andrews Leroy W. Blankenship
Donald J. Drew John C. Nolan
Richard L. Riemer Robert L. Sills

Ross A. Simonet


350 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC HISTORY

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

The report of the Masonic History Committee to the 2000 Annual
Communication of the Grand Lodge of California is probably best represented
by the history that the Committee has produced. The sesquicentennial history
book, entitled, California Freemasonry 1850-2000---The Past Fifty Years, has
been completed and mailed to all pre-publication purchasers. It will be on sale
at the Masonic History Committee booth on the exhibit floor at the Annual
Communication, and following at the Grand Lodge supply room.

This book of 270 pages covers the activities of Grand Lodge for the
period 1950- to 2000. Although the format is considerably different, it does
serve to complement the epic work of Past Grand Master Whitsell’s four volume
One Hundred Years of Masonry in California. It was the feeling of the Grand
Lodge Masonic History Committee that we wanted to produce a book that might
be shared with non-Masons. To that extent we elected to include hundred of
pictures of various Masonic activities, mostly in color. The result has been a
book that is not only attractive and eye-catching, but also is an excellent source
of information.

The Committee also did sizeable research, telling in considerable
informative detail the process by which the various changes _ took place in the
fifty-year scope of the book. Examples are the articles on “Modification of
Physical Requirements,” “Adoption of the Cipher Ritual,” “The Issue of
Candidates’ Proficiency Requirements,” “Prince Hall Recognition,” and others.

The Grand Lodge Masonic History Committee is justifiably proud of
the completed work, and most appreciative of the favorable reaction of the
brethren to our efforts. But we recognize that we still have a task before us—
that of selling the rest of the books.

Respectfully submitted,
Charles H. Alexander, PGM, Chairman

Richard Bingle Ralph H. Head
Brett A. MacDonald Jorge Soto
Elton L. Turner Michael G. Arnold, Consultant
Richard B. Eaton, Consultant Theodore Meriam, Consultant

Hugh A. Shuffleton III, Consultant


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 351

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC HOMES ENDOWMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

I would first like to take this opportunity to thank all those who
contributed to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund (MHEF) this past year.
Brothers, widows and non-masons contributed a total of $969,914.98 to the
MHEF, which is more than last year. This total does not include the numerous
bequests we have also received for the year. We have had a steady decline in
contributions since 1987, but this year we turned it around, and we did it with
fewer members than last year. Of significant success was the implementation of
a gift incentive program. This program was so successful at increasing higher
level giving that the total number of contributions of $100 or more increased
over fiscal year 1999 by nearly 53%, and nearly tripled the total from fiscal year
1997.

During the year we implemented several system improvements to
enhance the collection of donations to the MHEF. These included new software,
new procedures, and new staff. Each had a direct impact on our success for the
year. One of the new outcomes from the improvements is an accurate
accounting of contributors. This means that we can now have a true count of the
members who have contributed. Past efforts to compile this information led to
Lodges having in excess of 100% participation rates, which obviously reduced
the credibility of the old tracking systems. The good news is that we can be
very accurate now; the bad news is that our participation rate for this jurisdiction
is less than 18% total (15,659 contributors; 88,559 Master Masons). Brethren,
we have a long way to go. The insight that less than 1 out of 5 Masons in
California are contributing to our number one charity is saddening to the
leadership of this Grand Lodge and the Masonic Homes Board.

I have received correspondence from Lodges all over the state. Some
have bemoaned the changes, while others have heralded them and the
corresponding results. One Lodge even reported in its Trestleboard that
contributions for this year were double last year’s, and ten times the year before.


352 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

This was the case all over the state. Another of the changes to the system
related to the method of collecting donations. There are a number of reasons for
the contributions to come directly to the Homes and not through the Lodge.
While this is not the time or place to expound on them in detail, they do include
legal and tax implications, and the benefit gained by the Homes having the
money invested sooner. While the extra effort to send a separate check to the
Homes may seem excessive, I can think of few prouder moments during the year
than when I write out a check to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund. It is my
annual and tangible exemplification of our peculiar practice to aid and assist all
worthy Brother Master Masons, their widows and orphans.

We are resolute in our convictions to support the mission of the
Masonic Homes of California, and we will strive to spread the word to the
Brethren that we must all support the Homes. We must, all of us, reach into our
pockets to help. To further this endeavor, the Endowment Committee produced
a 5-minute video about the Homes. A 2-minute version of this video was
broadcast on MS/NBC on April 14th as part of the “Champions of Industry”
segments hosted by Pat Summerall. These videos are in the hands of the
committeemen, Homes board members, and others, to be shown at Lodges and
homes all over the state. Where a video cannot be shown, a script has been
provided to cover the same information.

Next year there will be further enhancements and additions to our
programs, including new gift incentive items. Our task is to help raise
awareness of the Masonic Homes of California, and the importance of
contributing to further its success. The future of the Homes rests in our hands.
Let us make sure that future generations of needy Masonic family members will
be able to benefit from our foresight and generosity.

Respectfully submitted,
Richard Pumerantz, Chairman

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 353

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC SERVICE BUREAUS

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

The Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus is pleased to submit its
annual report on the operations and conditions of the nine Masonic Service
Bureaus for the year ending June 30, 2000. The committee is in its 73 year.

Right Worshipful David C. Decker, Deputy Grand Master, was’
appointed liaison officer to the committee, and Joel H. Springer III, Assistant
Grand Secretary, as the committee secretary. We appreciate their support and
assistance during the year.

This committee is well aware of its responsibility in overseeing and
directing the operation of the various bureaus in a uniform manner. The Service
Bureaus assist sojourners, their widows and orphans; operate blood banks in two
bureaus, arrange and conduct funerals for deceased Masons; make
investigations; assist in processing applications for the two Masonic Homes and
homes in other jurisdictions; visit the sick; and promote fraternal relations with
sojourners. The committee also assists the Masonic Service Association in the
continuing development of its “Hospital Visitation Program” at several of the
Veterans Administration facilities in California.

Committee Members

The committee had two changes in November 1999. Worshipful
Howard Dumont completed his tenure of service, having served the committee
for seven years and Chairman for five years. We wish to thank him for his
efforts and leadership during his years on the committee for the betterment of
Masonry in California. Also, Worshipful Edgar L. Friedman, appointed to serve
as Chairman, resigned in December due to health problems. He had done a lot
for Grand Lodge and the committee and will be missed. In December,
Worshipful A. Bruce Ivie was appointed by the Grand Master to take over the
committee again, having served as Chairman for five years prior to 1992. We
welcome Worshipful John P. Gibson from the San Diego area on to the
committee. We are sure he will be an asset to the committee.

Executive Secretaries

We would like to thank Worshipful Joseph A. Silva for his many years
as Executive Secretary of the Sacramento Bureau. We welcome aboard his
replacement, Worshipful Bill Faught, who has taken over the helm for the
Greater Sacramento Bureau. He is going to be an asset for Masonry in
California and Greater Sacramento. Also, thanks to Worshipful Ron Reed, who
has been serving Santa Clara Valley Bureau as Executive Secretary, for his
efforts and now will be working with Worshipful John Lawler as Executive
Secretary together for the Santa Clara Valley.


354 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Meetings

Because of changes this year we had only one meeting in the Los
Angeles area for the plans for the year. All the committee members have been
working in their areas to keep the Bureaus in uniformity.

Bush Masonic Relief

The Bush Masonic Relief is the primary source of funding for the three
Service Bureaus (Los Angeles, Long Beach and San Gabriel Valley) in the
greater Los Angeles area. These funds, which provide a majority of the funds
for these Bureaus, are a result of the overseeing of the funds by the Bush
Masonic Relief Board. We would like to thank them for their efforts.

Operating Expenses

Total cost for operating the nine Bureaus for the year ending June 30,
2000 amounted to $163,057.09 which is $4,953.41 below the planned budget.
Some Executive Secretaries receive a modest salary while others receive no
salary. A few choose to receive mileage only.

Conclusions

This committee sincerely appreciates the support it has received from
Most Worshipful Alvin J. Weis, the Grand Lodge office staff for their continued
support and to the Grand Secretary, Very Worshipful John L. Cooper III, for his
guidance during the year.

Appreciation is also expressed to the Presidents, officers and
representatives of all the Bureaus in this grand jurisdiction for their great and
continued interest in and devotion to the activities of the various Bureaus and for
their help to our sojourning brethren and their families.

Our Executive Secretaries deserve special note as they are in the
forefront of our fraternal and charitable business and are on call every day and
night of the year. These men are the backbone of our Bureaus and to each we
express our deep and abiding appreciation for their work.

The committee budget during the past year was sufficient for us to
function properly and a budget of $2,500.00 has been asked for the coming year.

Respectfully submitted,

A. Bruce Ivie, Chairman
John P. Gibson George C. Hesse
Alexander T. Kirkpatrick Robert W. Meier

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 359

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MEMORIAL SERVICE

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

My Brethren, each year we gather here to remember those members of
this Grand Lodge who are now within the comforting arms of our Divine
Creator. Yes, while it is true that we mourn the loss of their kindly friendship,
we must also call this gathering a celebration of their lives among us— for it was
our Great Creator who gave them to us for only this short period of time into the
Brotherhood of Man to love and honor as He has Commanded we should do.
While it is true that we honor all departed members of this body, I ask that you
permit me to mention the passing of Francis Atkinson, who for many years was
our Grand Organist. As one of our members stated, “The world that was
titillated by the trail of mirth and music, is now in a better place.” For myself,
may I add that the world needs more “free spirited” men like Francis Atkinson, a
man who brought nothing but joy and happiness into our lives. He was a man
who played the organ, not with his hands but with his heart. His innovations,
which brought not only smiles and warmth to our souls, proved that his free
spirit was an inspiration to us all.

I am therefore prompted to call upon your memory a few lines from the
popular song, “My Way” which I believe may be taken as a man’s confession of
life:

“And now the end is near, and so I face the final curtain
My friend I'll say it clear, I’ll state my case of which I’m certain
I’ve lived a life that’s full, I’ve traveled each and every highway
And more, much more than this — I did it my way.”

You might now ask— why this sony “My Way?” Because I’m sure you
remember that there were many times in the past when our departed brethren
“did it their way.” The many times they found themselves in a position that
called for an important decision and they “did it their way.” The exception, of
course, as we all know, were the times when we were required to do it
“according to Ulysses”, particularly when our Grand Lecturer admonished us to
“always follow your rod when turning” — unless when required to turn more
than 180 degrees — for he always assumed we remembered our high school

geometry.

Yes, humor is good for the soul. Perhaps no one could have more of a
sense of humor than that of our Creator, for as we read “And He took clay and
molded it into His own image and likeness.” I would then perceive that with a
great show of humor, He breathed into it a soul so we could “do it our way.”
Couldn’t we say that coupled with this soul is the gift of a free will? He then
“tickled” our ribs, removed one and gave us woman; who without a doubt,


360 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

beginning with our mothers, demanded of us the utmost of morality. And why,
even with this great influence, do we err? There must be an answer, for I’m sure
He must have great satisfaction watching us scurry around attempting to make
the many moral judgments and decisions required of us during our life time.
The song goes on to reflect this dilemma:

“Yes, there were times, I’m sure you knew,
when I bit off more than I could chew
But through it all, when there was doubt
I ate it up and spit it out.”

We must ask ourselves, as did many of our departed brethren, the
perplexing question, “What gets in the way of a man making MORAL
judgments and decisions?”

I’m sure many of the brethren present have been confined to bed for a
period of time and tried to think things out as I did several years ago. I asked
myself the perplexing question, “What makes me differentiate between a ‘snap’
judgment as opposed to a thoughtful MORAL judgment or decision?” The one
thought I came up with was the single word “EGO.”

Yes ego, my brethren. Ego appeared to me as the one thing that gets in
the way of making moral judgments. Too often we attempt to cover ourselves
with the cloak of omnipotence, that which is reserved for our Creator, and then
we go so far as to place ourselves at His right hand— and even on occasion, with
great audacity we ask Him to move over. THAT is ego my brethren— but please
don’t confuse ego with pride. My dictionary defines ego as “one who regards
his own self-interest as the foundation of morality” or again as “looking upon
questions — chiefly in relation to one’s ownself.” Pride was defined as a “feeling
of elation or high satisfaction from some accomplishment.” It has often been
said that honest pride can be one of man’s greatest motivators. In other words,
my brethren, be proud when you deliver a good Third Degree Lecture and
receive with pride the congratulations of your brethren for a job well done,
knowing full well, that another brother has, in all probability, performed even
more eloquently than you have. That is honest pride. There is however grave
danger, my brethren, when we find ourselves in a position of authority and use
that authority as “looking upon questions— chiefly in relation to one’s own self”
— or having ulterior motives benefiting one’s own self or a personal opinion
devoid of morality. My brethren, I’m sure we all have the highest respect and
regard for authority and the law. But too often we look upon authority andthe
law as absolute. It is not absolute, as we all know, it is subject to interpretation.
In our lives we must commit ourselves to interpret with a strong moral
conscience.

We might now ask, “What about the difference of opinions?” Our past
brethren often found themselves presented with this dilemma. I’m sure we all
recognize that there is nothing wrong with a difference of opinion, as long as we


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 361

include morality in that decision. Some of the greatest men of history, and I like
to call them revolutionaries, were called upon to make some of the world’s most
profound decisions. Men like Abraham, Moses, Jesus, Martin Luther and men
like Washington and Garibaldi, including the philosophies of Aristotle, Plato,
Spinoza and, yes even men like Hitler and Stalin. But what makes the
difference between these men? We must ask ourselves only one question. Were
their opinions based on moral judgment and done without ego? With
differences of opinion it is often stated “that arguments for and against never
PROVE they only serve to ILLUMINATE.” We must therefore learn to couple
our opinions with honesty and morality.

During this session of our Grand Lodge, we will be called upon, as
have our deceased brethren in years past, to make serious decisions. We must
demand of ourselves that we base our conclusions by putting aside our egos and
rendering decisions upon the foundation of moral judgment. We must not forget
to do the same when we are called upon for decisions within our own
households, and our work place, for too often we allow our egos to stand in the
way of that which we know is just and right.

Let us therefore be reminded of those special brethren of yesteryear
whom we remember and cherish— those very special brethren who “did it their
way” and who had the fortitude to make moral judgments and decisions.

And now as the song concludes it goes on to say:

“T’ve loved, I’ve laughed and cried, I’ve had my fill, my share of losing
And now, as tears subside, I find it all so amusing
To think I did all that.
For what is a man, what has he got, if not himself
Then he has not the things to say he truly feels,
And not the words of one who kneels.
The record shows I took my blows — and did it “MY WAY”

Yes, my brethren, each man is entitled to do it “his way” which is the
privilege of the Free Spirit which was endowed upon us by our Maker. Let us
remember that we must use this Free Spirit with the morality, as was
exemplified by the life of a man like Francis Atkinson, which is the very essence
of Brotherly Love. I am confident that if we make our decisions based upon
moral judgment, we will be able to stand confidently before our Maker when we
are called upon to answer for a spent life— and may we ask our Heavenly Father
— that as we enter into our Eternal Home, may we be privileged to enter with
love in our hearts, to the music of Francis Atkinson.


362 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Most Worshipful, may I call upon our Grand Chaplain, Lester W.
Miller, to deliver the closing Benediction for this Memorial Service?

Respectfully submitted,
Emile R. Laversin, Chairman
V. Kent Armstrong, Jr. Larry W. Coyle
Robert N. Deter Mark Sandstrom
Lester W. Miller, Consultant

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON OFFICERS’ MANAGEMENT WORKSHOPS

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

Your Officers’ Management Workshops Committee completed a
successful year of seven Workshop presentations. An average of approximately
seventy Masons attended each. Workshops were held in San Diego, Anaheim,
Bellflower, Fresno, Folsom, San Jose and Redding. We set forth herewith the
several policies, which we have developed during the past several years.

Target Audience

The Lodge’s Junior Officers: the Marshal, Junior and Senior Stewards,
Junior and Senior Deacons and prospective officers with no previous leadership
experience.

Purpose

Introduce useful, basic leadership topics about Freemasonry and
organizations of men in general prior to attendance at the Wardens’ Retreats.

Our Mission

The Officers’ Management Workshops Team provides Lodges with
leadership training programs for Junior Officers (the Marshal, Stewards and
Wardens) and prospective officers.

Our Vision
Effective Masonic Leaders through Leadership Training
Involved, Enjoyable Lodges from Effective Masonic Leaders
Better Communities from Involved, Enjoyable Lodges
Repetition of the Teaching Modules

Experience in education has shown that repetition is one key to
learning. We have intentionally chosen topics that can be developed
sequentially for the participants in the Workshops and in the Wardens’


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 363

Leadership Retreats. The topics are classified into several “strands” of thought.
Six such strands have been identified; they include Masonic Education,
Financial Management, Membership Development, Program Development,
Organizational Structure and Community Involvement.

The topics within these strands will be usefully developed as a Masonic
leader participates in the several Grand Lodge leadership training programs in
the following way.

Using a Masonic analogy, the introduction of a topic at the basic level
of the Officers’ Management Workshops is seen as the creation of an ashlar in a
quarry by an entered apprentice. Learning to do this is a basic skill, which
creates a necessary building block for others to use creatively.

Following the metaphor, the principles of the use of these building
blocks are taught to the Junior Wardens at the Junior Wardens’ Leadership
Retreats. There they are shown how the builder integrates the many necessary
building blocks to form a useful structure, or, in this case, an organization of
men.

Finally, the use of these building blocks for the creative benefit of the
Master’s unique Lodge is seen as the subject of instruction at the Senior
Wardens’ Leadership Retreats. The Senior Warden is pictured as an architect
who manipulates these basic building blocks by the understanding of how they
fit into a building to ultimately create a specific building, or Lodge, which meets
the needs of the members of his specific Lodge.

The Three-Year Plan

We developed a three-year plan of alternating sites for future
Workshops within specific targeted geographical areas. We also changed our
presentation methods to allow more time for discussion of each topic. As a
result, we could not present all topics at each Workshop. We therefore
developed a three-year plan of topic presentations so that each topic will have
adequate time for discussion, and, also, each year’s Workshops will be different
from those the year prior.

Respectfully submitted,
C. Richard Hutchinson, Jr., Chairman
Frederick G. Kleyn III, Vice Chairman

H. Edward Barr Mark G. Gershon
Mark N. Gibson Howard E. Lewis III
Donald E. Newcome Larry A. Reynolds, Sr.
Robert A. Reynolds Hal G. Scott

364 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON SESQUICENTENNIAL CELEBRATION

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

Our 150th year of Masonry in California was an extremely busy year
for the committee. The committee continued to meet and refine its plans on a
regular basis. Its primary focus was on the final planning and implementation of
the Grand Lodge celebrations and to encourage Lodges to continue their local
planning.

In the 1999 proceedings, we outlined the goals and objectives that were
established for the celebration. I am pleased to report that of the seven goals
established by the committee, nearly all were achieved. Numerous constituent
Lodges were active in their community and used the sesquicentennial year to
increase their local awareness and recognition. From a Grand Lodge
sponsorship standpoint, we did plan and successfully implement two major
events focused on increasing public attention to the fraternity. All profits from
these events were designated to charity. Commemoratives were commissioned
and made available to the craft and at every opportunity we emphasized
Masonic contributions to California’s rich heritage. Although uniform
marketing images were developed, we were disappointed by overall
implementation efforts.

A recap of the programs of our Sesquicentennial Celebration is as
follows:

Beginning with the 1999 Annual Communication,
commemoratives were made available for ordering. For the first time
in our history, commemorative aprons were designed and marketed.
Marketing continued throughout the year and resulted in fulfilled orders
of 247 aprons, including eight full Lodge sets, A net revenue of
$10,583 was realized and used for funding the major celebration
events. Marketing of the commemorative poster was less successful
with under 20 prints being ordered, but initial costs were insufficient
and were covered from funding proceeds raised through apron orders.

Increasing awareness and attention to Masonic heritage began
immediately at the conclusion of the 1999 Annual Communication with
the display of ten 30 foot banners at the entry to our Memorial Temple
in San Francisco. These banners were displayed periodically
throughout the year and did much to raise public interest. Additionally,
logos, banners, banner artwork and stationery was developed and made
available to constituent Lodges. Although there was mixed interest, we
were encouraged by a number of Lodges that proudly displayed the
Sesquicentennial themes in their communities and at their Lodges.


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 365

Continuing the focus on public awareness, two major Grand
Lodge events were held during the year with great success. On April
13, 2000, the first Sesquicentennial event was held in Sacramento, our
State Capital. For the first time in our state’s history, Grand Master
Alvin J. Weis and Grand Master Ronald Robinson, Prince Hall of
California and Hawaii, were received on the floor of the California
State Senate and there they were warmly recognized by the members of
the State Senate for Masons’ contributions to our state’s history. Later
that morning, five members of our fraternity who are current State
Senators and Assemblymen were recognized and honored in a public
ceremony on the steps of the Capital.

During the following days, receptions for local and traveling
Masons were held with the Grand Family, and on April 15, 2000, the
Grand Lodge placed a commemorative plaque at the site of our
founding at Sth and J Streets in downtown Sacramento.

Moving to the southern part of the state, over 900 Masons,
their families and friends ascended upon Dodger Stadium in Los
Angeles to enjoy recognition of our anniversary by the Dodger
organization witnessed by over 50,000 people in attendance. A pre-
game presentation included Grand Lodge. officers and Youth
Organization leaders and seven of our children from the Covina Home
“taking the field” with the Dodger players at the start of the game.
Proceeds from this event of over $500 was contributed to the Grand
Master’s Special Charity Fund and to the Job’s Daughters Charity
Program, the Child Language Disorder Clinics. At the request of the
Grand Master, plans to have similar events at San Diego and Oakland
were removed from the celebration program due to his numerous other
commitments.

Likewise, after finalizing the economics and Lodge support
necessary to successfully hold the planned Gala event at the Shrine
Auditorium, it was decided that such an event was not prudent at this
time.

On behalf of the entire committee, we would like to express our deepest
appreciation for being asked to be a part of these historic events and for the
opportunity to have served a wonderfully dedicated Grand Master. We believe
that the events were of the highest quality and met the goals set forth. We very
much look forward to our Bicentennial in the year 2050 although most of us
question that the Great Architect will grant us the longevity necessary to see that
day.

Respectfully submitted,
Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
Thomas Boles Ronald E. Cooper
Jeff H. Eichner, Jr. Gary C. Young


366 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON TOURNAMENT OF ROSES PARADE FLOAT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

Your Task Force has the responsibility for all of the tasks associated
with the entry of a float in the Rose Parade. These include the selection of the
float theme, design of the float, contract negotiations with the float builder,
voluntary fund raising, liaison with the Tournament of Roses organization,
selection of float riders, publicity, float decoration, food service for the
decorators, costumes, accommodations and transportation of the float riders,
photography, videography, design and sales of merchandise relating to the float
and numerous other details.

The theme of the 2000 Rose Parade was “LIBERTY FOR ALL”. In
keeping with this theme and in consultation with the float builder, Phoenix
Decorating Co., Pasadena, we selected a float design which would be instantly
recognized throughout the world, namely, The Statue of Liberty.

The design was an exact replica of the Statue which has resided in New
York harbor since 1886. Rising 50 feet above Colorado Boulevard with Miss
Liberty’s torch held high, the float symbolized the Parade theme
“CELEBRATION 2000 - VISIONS OF THE FUTURE”. We chose this
design because it represented a celebration of the past gift of the French people
to the people of the United States and it will serve as a continuing beacon of
hope for the future.

The figure of Miss Liberty was covered with thousands of silver dollar
eucalyptus leaves plucked from their stems and individually glued in place by
hundreds of decorators over a period of seven days. The finished statue looked
just like the oxidized copper surface of the real statue. The pedestal on which
the statue stands was decorated with crushed walnut seeds and sesame seeds.
The base of the float was an assortment of eighty flags symbolizing the variety
of countries from which immigrants have come to live in the “land of the free”.
Other floral arrangements on the deck of the float added to the overall beauty
and elegance of our Parade entry. Another interesting feature of the float was
the hydraulic system for lowering the statue to the horizontal position so that it
could fit under the freeway overpass and some power cables on its route to the
end of the Parade. There were no riders on the float because it was felt that the
main message was conveyed by the Statue itself.

The decoration of the float was accomplished by over 1,000 decorators
from the youth groups and adults from various Masonic organizations who
donated a total of 8,000 hours to create this spectacular float. Food was
provided by the Task Force with sandwiches being prepared for the dry
decoration days by the Masonic Home at Covina and hot food by the Shrine and
some Lodge members during the final week. The Masonic Home bus also


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 367

provided shuttle service from the High School parking lot to the decorating site
several blocks away.

The cost of the float was approximately $167,000 which included the
$2,000 entry fee, $150,000 for the float contract and approximately $15,000 for
other expenses such as publicity flyers, fund raising appeals, food for the
decorators, office supplies, communications, riders’ expenses, photography,
videography production, transportation, etc. Although this total cost was higher
than that of our previous floats, plans were formulated several years ahead to
make the 2000 float a very special float to celebrate the start of the new
millennium. We express our thanks to the supporting fund raisers sponsored by
three Grand Masters over the last several years and the voluntary donations from
Grand Lodges, concordant and appendant organizations, California Lodges and
individuals.

The Family of Freemasonry’s 2000 float suffered two unfortunate
setbacks. First, the float was assigned the 98th position in the 109 unit Parade
which meant that it was not covered by one of the national television stations
when it switched its broadcast from the Parade to a football game in another part
of the country. However, coverage of the float by other television stations was
generally good. Secondly, the float had to be erected to its full 50 foot height
outdoors for the judges to view it and witness the lowering and raising of the
statue. Shortly afterwards, a surprise cloudburst drenched the float and caused
some of the flag decorations to wash into each other.

The TV commentators were furnished with information about the role
of French and American Freemasonry in the design, construction, cornerstone
and dedication ceremonies associated with the Statue. The float was very well
received by the viewing public and was given special recognition by the press as
one of the greatest crowd-pleasers in the Parade in spite of some rain damage to
the flowers. As has been the custom in past years, the Task Force produced a 25
minute video which shows some history of the Statue of Liberty as well as the
construction and decoration of the float and its TV coverage during the Parade.

Shortly after the 2000 Parade was completed, a start was made on the
plans for the 2001 float.

The 2001 Parade theme is “FABRIC OF AMERICA”. In consultation
with the float builder, Phoenix Decorating Co., we chose to continue the
partriotic and freedom theme that we used for the 2000 float and titled our 2001
float “LET FREEDOM RING”. The float design consists of a replica of the
Liberty Bell as the centerpoint, a floral display of the Constitution and a quill
and inkwell in front, and the 1777 design of the original flag of the thirteen
colonies at the rear. These three features were selected because they are all part
of the fabric of America. However, another important part is the role of the
people. To ensure that these symbols are kept alive by the people, we have
invited two leaders from each of the Masonic related youth groups to ride on the
float dressed in the regalia of their respective organizations as ambassadors for


368 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Freemasonry and as guardians of the freedoms exemplified on the float. There
will be the usual title panel with the square and compass and the name ofthe
float sponsor, Family of Freemasonry, on each side of the float. The script
provided to the television stations emphasizes the relationship between
Freemasonry and these freedoms.

The cost of the 35 foot 2001 float will be approximately $107,000
which includes the $2,000 entry fee, $90,000 for the float design and
construction contract and approximately $15,000 for other expenses described
earlier for the 2,000 float.

The principal purpose of having a Family of Freemasonry float in the
Rose Parade is to stimulate interest in the fraternity and hopefully increase our
membership. In reviewing the Proceedings of the Grand Lodge of California
over the last few years, we have noted that there has been a significant increase
in the number of Entered Apprentice, Fellowcraft and Master Mason degrees
conferred. Although we have no way of relating these events to the floats, we
are convinced that they have played some role in reversing our membership
decline. We have also noted that the television commentators are now much
more familiar with Freemasonry and its works than they were ten years ago.

The Task Force wishes to acknowledge with appreciation the support
of many people and entities of the Family of Freemasonry - the Grand Masters
and Grand Lodge officers, the Grand Lodge office staff, the Masonic Home at
Covina for many courtesies and the use of the Home bus, Pasadena Scottish Rite
for hosting Task Force meetings, Pasadena Lodge for youth accommodations
and hosting the New Year’s Eve party for decorators, South Pasadena Lodge for
youth accommodations, Al Malaikah Shrine RV Club for making their motor
homes available for the comfort of the riders prior to boarding the float, York
Rite, Shrine and Lodge members for cooking and serving food to the decorators,
Midnight Mission for the use of a food service vehicle, Von’s bakery for a
donation of pies and cakes, Blair High School for the use of their parking lot,
Walco and California Auto Service for permission to use an area behind their
businesses for serving food and for providing power, and Southern California
Research Lodge for assistance in distributing float flyers to its members
throughout the world.

As Chairman, I want to express my deepest appreciation to the
members of the Task Force who have worked tirelessly to ensure the success of
each float.

And, now, it is with the deepest regret that I must announce that,
because of the steadily declining financial support for the float program, an
Executive decision has been made that the 2001 float will have to be the last
float that will be entered in the Rose Parade. The outstanding support we
received through voluntary contributions in the earlier years from 1992 through
1995 has not been sustained and, in fact, has diminished to the point that this
funding method now provides less than one third of the float cost. The


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 369

contributions from other Grand Lodges have also diminished over the years. It
is only through the commercial fund raising programs sponsored by some Grand
Masters that we have been able to continue the float program as long as we
have. Without this support or some other means of raising funds, it is necessary
to terminate the program after the events relating to the 2001 float have been
concluded.

It has been a pleasure and a privilege to serve this Grand Lodge and
Freemasonry in general in this worthwhile project. We trust that our efforts
have met with your approval.

Respectfully submitted,
Stanley L. Channon, PGM, Chairman
Frederick T. Alexander Donald A. Journey
LeRoy Bartels Thomas C. Letcher
Bart Bodnar Herschel P. Mazer
George H. Cronin II Raymond B. Rosenbaum
David H. Farlee Gaylord V. Roten
Donald A. Hull Mark E. Schrock

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON TRIAL REVIEW

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

Since the 1999 Annual Communication there have been referred to
your Committee on Trial Review nine completed trials and one application for
restoration from expulsion. In addition, there were three cases which were
disposed of before trial and three cases pending but not completed and submitted
to Grand Lodge for review.

Your committee has reviewed each of the Trial Records numbered
2416, 2428, 2429, 2430, 2431, 2434 and 2435 and finds that in each case there
was substantial compliance with the procedural requirements of our Constitution
and Ordinances and the result is supported in the record of each case, and there
are no miscarriages of justice.

Your committee therefore submits and moves the adoption of the
following resolution:

“Resolved that in each of the Trial Records numbered 2416,
2428, 2429, 2430, 2431, 2434 and 2435 the judgment and
penalty imposed be ratified, affirmed and approved.”


370 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In reviewing all of the cases this year, your committee noted that
several of the cases involved conflicts between brother Masons that should not
have resulted in Masonic charges being filed. Your committee thanks the
various Inspectors, the Grand Secretary and his staff as well as the Grand Master
for their efforts to resolve these conflicts without a Masonic Trial. Each of us is
reminded of our obligations as Masons to whisper wise counsel in the ear of an
offending brother instead of shouting about our conflicts from the rooftops of
our Lodges. If peace and harmony is to prevail in our Lodges each of us must
practice our Masonic Tenets on a daily basis.

Lastly, we encourage Past Masters to consider becoming Trial Masters.
While we do not have many trials each year, we always have trouble finding a
competent Trial Master. The job is critical to the well being of our craft and
there is training and a manual available to guide you through the process.

Most Worshipful Grand Master, we have two trials and one application
for restoration to report on separately.

Trial Record No. 2432

In this Trial the accused, a member of Natoma Lodge No. 64, was
charged with four specifications of unmasonic conduct alleging various
violations of California Masonic Code Section 32020. Based on the record
provided to your Committee on Trial Review it appears that there was in fact no
trial. Notwithstanding the irregularities set forth herein, the accused was found
guilty of three specifications and the Trial Master fixed the penalty at Expulsion.

This case has caused considerable difficulty for your committee in
several respects. First, we find that there was no proper service of the
specification on the accused as required by California Masonic Code Section
32130. Based on the record we find there was no reasonable attempt at personal
service nor was the attempt at service by mail in compliance with the CMC.
Secondly, the record disclosed an unauthorized and improper communication
between the Trial Master and the accused. Thirdly, the accused, who denies the
charges contained in the specifications was not provided with a proper trial as
required by the CMC.

Your committee after reviewing the record of the entire case finds there
were multiple errors in this Trial that have resulted in a miscarriage of justice
that requires that this case be overturned. Furthermore, the evidence in this case
clearly indicates that this matter is a business dispute between two Masons and
there was no basis for any Masonic charges being filed in the first place.
Therefore, in the spirit of CMC Section 32030, we recommend that Grand
Lodge approve the dropping of all charges in this case.

Therefore, we submit and move the adoption of the following
resolution:


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 371

“Resolved that Trial Record No. 2432 be closed without a
retrial.”

Trial Record No. 2436

The accused, a member of Palm Springs Lodge No. 693, was charged
and found guilty in Federal Court of conspiracy to defraud the Federal Elections
Commission by using deceitful and dishonest means to impair, impede and
obstruct its lawful functions by concealing the true source of illegal political
campaign contributions. This conspiracy was executed via an elaborate scheme
whereby the accused in his capacity as the Chief Executive Officer of the
Cabazon Band of Mission Indians made secret, disguised and_ illegal
contributions to various political candidates with money belonging to the
Cabazon Band of Mission Indians.

To illegally funnel substantial amounts of funds to influence the basic
right of free and honest elections is a serious Masonic offense. The accused did
not respond to the Masonic charges and the Trial Master after reviewing the
guilty findings of the Federal Court found the accused guilty and fixed the
penalty at suspension. Your Committee on Trial Review believes that based on
the seriousness of the conduct, which violates our Obligation as Masons,
requires the penalty of Expulsion.

Therefore, your committee submits and moves the adoption of the
following resolution:

“Resolved that in Trial Record No. 2436 the findings of the
Trial Master be affirmed and approved, except that the penalty
is raised to Expulsion.”

Trial Record No. 2348

This is an Application for Restoration from Expulsion. The accused, a
former member of Los Angeles Silver Trowel Lodge No. 42, was found guilty
of sexual battery on four women patients as a then practicing Chiropractor. He
was convicted in State Court and was sentenced to prison. He was found guilty
in his Masonic Trial and the penalty was fixed as a reprimand, which was raised
to expulsion by Grand Lodge in 1992.

He now appeals to Grand Lodge for restoration. His application for
restoration was voted on by his Lodge at its August Stated Meeting which by a
substantial majority voted to deny the application. It is notable that no letters
from friends, family or anyone in the community attesting to the applicant’s
character and present reputation in his business or social activities have been
submitted with this application.


372 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Your Committee on Trial Review has reviewed the record in this matter
and the application for restoration and recommends that the application for
restoration be denied. Therefore, we submit and move the adoption of the
following resolution:

“Resolved that the application for restoration in Trial Record
No. 2348 be denied.”

Respectfully submitted,
David R. Doan, Chairman
Donald B. Constine Charles W. Foster
Vernon A. Leeper Duke D. Rouse

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON U.S. CONSTITUTION OBSERVANCE

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

This year the activities of the Constitution Observance Committees of
the individual Lodges throughout the State of California has reached new
heights. I have received many, many reports of dinners with Patriotic speakers,
community parades and barbecues, all designed to not only demonstrate the
commitment of Freemasons everywhere to furthering the cause of Freedom, but
also a commitment to enlighten and educate the citizens of our communities,
young and old, about our Heritage and preserve the values which made this
country what it is today, a symbol of Freedom for the entire world.

This year marked a concerted effort to provide the Lodges with a
constant flow of materials each month that would facilitate the dissemination of
information about this document and our Heritage. “The California Freemason”
has cooperated by printing articles in each issue on this subject as well. In
addition to these efforts, extensive contacts have been made at Grand Lodge
Secretary’s meetings around the State, and Officer Management Workshops in
most districts of the Grand Lodge.

More and more reports are coming in as we enter the celebration season
of Constitution Day, and most participants, upon their receipt, will be issued
certificates of recognition and excellence for their work. Jack Ansell of
Washington Lodge No. 20 has already instituted programs involving almost
1,000 fifth grade students in Sacramento, in conjunction with the Scottish Rite
Valley there. Table Mountain Lodge No. 124 of Paradise, under the direction of
Jim Randolph, has involved the local unified school district in the furtherance of
Constitutional awareness. San Jose Lodge No. 10 has instigated a youth
oriented program as well as Delano Lodge and more, too many to mention in


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 373

this limited space. San Bernardino Lodge, in conjunction with the Valley of San
Bernardino Scottish Rite, presents “dinner theatre” productions about our
Heritage, in full period costume. Next year I personally intend to publish a
“newsletter” which will feature highlights of this year’s events so all can share
in their successes.

In Southern California, many Lodges have combined to share a
community based program featuring band music, a keynote speech by the Grand
Orator, Worshipful Ronald Cooper, a playlet featuring appearances by some of
the signers of the Constitution in authentic costumes, a parade of state flags by
Masonic youth, and a giant flag display of American flags dating from 1770.

In addition, Norwalk Lodge No. 315 produced and presented an
American Heritage program, attended by the M-.W.. Alvin J. Weis and 10 other
members of the Grand Lodge of California. This program involving over 18
historical re-enactors, celebrated a timeline of our History from the Revolution
of 1776 to the cultural revolutions of the 60’s and 70’s.

I have learned also that over 17 Lodges are participating in “I Signed
the Constitution” events utilizing materials obtained from the National
Constitution Center in Philadelphia. This program gives you everything you
need including plans, guidelines, news releases, and materials for distribution at
a minimal cost, money well spent.

It was a “very good year” and hopefully next year will be even better.
The thrust for this upcoming year will be EDUCATION, for only by the
education of our youth, as well as the adults of the community, can we be
assured that future generations will be able to keep this Nation of ours Free and
Independent forever.

Respectfully submitted,
B. Eugene Collins, Chairman

Michael J. Bear Mario Chiappe
Chris R. Christoff Daniel G. Cook
Maurice R. Crosby Douglas S. Lawrence
Brett A. MacDonald Michael M. Marsellos
Guillermo P. Munda Jerry D. Roberts

374 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON WARDENS’ LEADERSHIP RETREATS

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

The Wardens’ Leadership Retreat Program continues to provide
leadership and management skills that a person will need to govern his Lodge
when he becomes Master. This program continues to represent one of the best
investments a Lodge can make for the future of not only their Lodge but for
California Freemasonry.

For the year 2000, the program trained a total of 195 Senior Wardens
and 125 of their ladies, 183 Junior Wardens and 125 of their ladies. It is
disappointing, however that we are not able to attract more of the Wardens to
the program. This type of training is imperative if we are to continue to have

quality programs at the lodge level. The following is the breakdown of the
Retreats:

Senior Wardens’ Retreat, North
90 Wardens and 59 ladies
March 3-5, 2000, San Ramon Marriott Hotel

Senior Wardens’ Retreat, South
105 Wardens and 66 ladies
March 17-19, 2000, Crowne Plaza Hotel at the Los Angeles Airport

Junior Wardens’ Retreat, South
97 Wardens and 61 ladies
May 4-6, 2000, Crowne Plaza Hotel at the Los Angeles Airport

Junior Wardens’ Retreat, North
86 Wardens and 64 ladies
May 19-21, 2000, San Ramon Marriott Hotel

The Senior Wardens’ Retreat were under the direction of Deputy Grand
Master David C. Decker and his wife Nancy and were well received. The
sessions were built around Rt. Worshipful Decker’s theme, “FREEMASONRY
CORNERSTONE OF THE NEW MILLENNIUM” and leadership training was
provided to accentuate this theme and provide the attendees ideas on community
involvement; installation planning; the Master’s responsibilities and obligations;
developing Lodge leadership; planning and carrying out a 60 minute Stated
Meeting; and membership improvement. These sessions were reinforced by the
use of Team Building Exercises and Group Reports. The ladies participated in
several of the sessions with their husbands and also in the group exercise
relating to community involvement. The ladies were also provided additional


GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA 375

subjects relating to Masonic protocol and Grand Lodge Week events; MMSAP
and the California Masonic Foundation.

Right Worshipful C. Ray Whitaker and his wife Joyce directed the
Junior Wardens’ Retreats using their theme of “COMMUNICATION; KEY TO
EDUCATION”. Subjects covered in these sessions included: Leadership
Development — Your role as an elected leader, building effective relationships,
and creating a circle of influence; Program Planning; Budget planning and
managing lodge finances; Structure of Grand Lodge; Using the California
Masonic Code; and lastly Creating a Plan for Desired Outcomes. Again, the
ladies participated in several of these sessions with their husbands along with
several relating to Masonic Philanthropies, including the Masonic Homes,
California Masonic Foundation, and other Masonic philanthropies; History of
Freemasonry; planning a Lodge Program for ladies; Grand Lodge Week and
Protocol; and a video on the George Washington Masonic Memorial. Their
sessions were also conducted with breakout exercises and Group reports. We
believe that the Junior Wardens and their ladies left the Retreats with a better
understanding of Freemasonry, their leadership role in the Lodge, and ideas on
making their Lodge more successful.

Facilitators used in these sessions included David Decker, Ray
Whitaker, Bill Holsinger, Howard Kirkpatrick, Loyal Stark, Allan Casalou, Don
Nielsen, John Cooper, David Doan, John Nolan, Michael Salazar, and Homes
Administrators Kelly Wiest and Dale Ankney. Each had a specific
responsibility and carried it out in an outstanding manner. The ladies speakers
and assistants included Nancy Decker, Joyce Whitaker, Jean Holsinger, Jackie
Kirkpatrick, and Marlene Sorsabal. My special thanks to all these people for
taking their time out of their busy schedules to assist in making the Retreats the
success they are.

Without the outstanding help from the Grand Lodge Office, particularly
Kimberly Hegg, it would be impossible to have these retreats. She handles all
the registrations along with seeing that each participant has a folder with the
program for the weekend. Kim provides quality materials for the retreats. The
Supply Room has also been extremely helpful in seeing that we had sufficient
quantities of materials for the Retreats. Without the Grand Lodge Office help,
we could not provide a quality program.

I would like to thank Grand Master Alvin J. Weis for his outstanding
support of the Wardens’ Retreat Program and his personal commitment to
improving the quality of the leadership of our Lodges. The Executive
Committee is also to be commended for their continuing support of this
program. On a personal note, I would like to thank my wife Marlene for her
agreement and commitment to seeing that these Retreats are a success. Her
support is greatly appreciated.


376 PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Respectfully submitted,

forwarded on to the appropriate Grand Lodge.
